<meta>This page begins the dedicatory epistle of a philosophical or theological treatise. The writer identifies himself as a Dominican friar named John, writing to John of Gaunt, Duke of Lancaster, who claimed the titles of King of Castile and León in the late 14th century.</meta>

To the magnificent prince and most illustrious lord, Lord <unclear>John</unclear>, firstborn of the King of Castile and León, by the grace of God <unclear>Duke</unclear> of <unclear>Lancaster</unclear>, <note>original: "Iohanni primogenito regis castelle & legionis... duci lancastrie"</note> and his protector in the kingdom of Castile; <unclear>Brother</unclear> <unclear>John</unclear> of the Order of Preachers, <term>Order of Preachers</term><note>The Dominican Order, known for their focus on education and preaching.</note> his chaplain, offers persevering service with humble devotion and obedience.

Since the highest Wisdom, <note>original: "sapie" / sapientiae</note> from the heights of the Lord, has ordained all things—even my own fallible soul—not only in the <unclear>shadow</unclear> of bodily things but also in spiritual matters, and through those things which are contained within visible existence; just as the hidden complexions <note>In medieval science, "complexions" refers to the balance of humors (blood, phlegm, etc.) that determine a person's nature.</note> of the body and the degrees of virtue, power, and nobility have flowed from His magnificent will, so that among men there may be degrees not only of power but of substance and virtue.

Indeed, certain ignorant people wander among these degrees, and certain others excel among them; and now I see in those <unclear>ranks</unclear> and among those degrees, there appears in you <note>Addressing the Duke of Lancaster.</note> not only such great nobility of body, strength, and agility, but also, joined with these, a great and virtuous boldness, assigned with such mental ability. For we hear of great things in a prince such as yourself, so that we cannot—even as a herald—attain to that grace <unclear>granted</unclear> by God, whether in natural or moral philosophy, or even in <unclear>rational</unclear> philosophy and theology. 

I desire that everyone might clearly see the degree of the best in God, which is more ample than all these things discovered through wisdom. Considering this, I have composed the book *Of the Ten* <note>original: "do cenayo", likely a corruption of "De Cenario" (Concerning the Number Ten), a treatise perhaps organized by ten virtues or principles.</note> and send it to your highness, so that it may be not only attended to but <unclear>beheld</unclear> by us. This is especially important because, as the father of philosophy says <note>Referring to Aristotle.</note>, and those things which the Latins find in the book of Aristotle: "Justice is better than both the morning and the evening star." <note>A famous quote from Aristotle's *Nicomachean Ethics*, comparing justice to the beauty of the stars.</note> 

Furthermore, as Aristotle says in the book on <term>sufficiency</term><note>Likely referring to the *Politics* or *Ethics* regarding self-sufficiency (*autarkeia*).</note>, our philosophy—which many philosophers studied—was once approved for its vigorous and healing truths, in a way that the common people never dared to philosophize. And because all these things in the book *Of the Ten* are founded upon the nine <unclear>origins</unclear> <note>original: "natales"</note> of the best of princes, they are established by those gifts flowing from the Lord.

<summary>A dedicatory preface by a Dominican chaplain to John of Gaunt, praising the Duke's physical and intellectual nobility while introducing a philosophical work titled 'De Cenario' (Of the Ten).</summary>

<meta>This page continues a discussion on the biological functions of the vegetative soul, specifically focusing on how nutrients are converted into body parts.</meta>

<margin>¶</margin> ...for these powers proceed from the soul through the mediation of nutritive food and growth, just as they are generated when one is born. This appears more clearly in animals, where from the same food, movement occurs toward growth and generation. From this, blood is produced, and so with the others, through the power of the **vegetative soul** <note>In medieval philosophy, the "vegetative soul" is the level of life responsible for basic biological functions like eating, growing, and reproducing—shared by plants, animals, and humans.</note> which, according to the condition of the parts, converts that food by its power into the nature of each individual member.

### Chapter Four: On the Nutritive Power

<margin>¶</margin> The arrangement of the mass and growth is truly marvelous: how it comes from the means of nourishing and growing, what power or matter produces nutrition and growth, and how the master of nourishing operates. This has been much sought after and discussed at length by the greatest of wise men because the works of nature are often imperceptible. I will, however, treat it briefly. This mass is a highly refined substance thanks to the soul, from which nutrition better flows into the parts...

<margin>¶</margin> ...fitted to their harmony. Therefore, this mass is a certain **preparatory mass** <note>original: "massa preparatoria"</note> composed of sustenance, which in living things is called food and drink. First, in plants, it is selected by **natural heat** <note>Medieval scientists believed "natural heat" was an internal metabolic fire that processed food and maintained life.</note>, which chooses what is beneficial and rejects...

<margin>¶</margin> ...what is harmful. Therefore, this mass is selected by natural heat and separated by a certain "natural appetite" <note>This "appetite" is not a conscious hunger, but a biological pull or attraction that organs have for specific nutrients.</note> through which each member of the body attracts matter to itself. 

<margin>¶</margin> Regarding this, according to the **attractive and appetitive power** which resides in the members (another function of the vegetative soul), it extracts nutrients in each member and converts them into the nature of that specific member and nourishes it. If, however, this power is lacking in a member, and there is not enough natural heat or appetite to attract enough from this mass to suffice for nourishing and growing through the power of the vegetative soul...

<margin>¶</margin> ...then that member will not be nourished or grown. If the attractive power of the member is not great enough, nor the heat nor the appetite, so that it attracts more than is required by its volume, it will not grow at all. Indeed, if it can only attract a tiny amount, nutrition will be very weak and the body will waste away.

<summary>This page explains the medieval theory of nutrition, describing how the "vegetative soul" and "natural heat" process food into a preparatory mass that individual body parts then attract to themselves to grow and repair.</summary>

<keywords>vegetative soul, nutrition, natural heat, metabolism, preparatory mass, attractive power, growth, biology</keywords>

--- Page 52 ---
<meta>This page transitions from the general mechanism of biological growth (nutrition and expansion) to the specific study of animal generation. It resolves the physical problem of how a body can increase in size without creating a vacuum or having two objects occupy the same space.</meta>

In all weak parts, growth or increase occurs in such a way that the matter which is received into a limb is received either in a vacuum or in a **plenum** <term>plenum: a space entirely filled with matter; the opposite of a vacuum.</term>. It is not received in a vacuum, because then two bodies would exist in the same place; therefore, it is received into the limb. To this it must be said that flesh, nerve, bone, and all members are porous, being either empty pores or pores filled with certain **fumosities** <note>original: "fumositatibus"; medieval scientists believed that "vapors" or "fumes" produced by internal heat filled the spaces within tissues.</note>. Thus, when nourishment arrives, the fumosities yield, and the spaces are filled by the nourishment; from this, new flesh swells and increases by replacing what was there. The pores are generated by these fumosities, and likewise, they are filled when nourishment arrives, so that it does not follow that a vacuum is created, nor that two bodies exist in the same place. This part concerns the matter and manner of growth and change. Thus, let this suffice concerning the being and existence of **vegetative bodies** <note>This refers to the "vegetative soul," the level of life shared by plants and animals that governs growth and nutrition.</note>.

**D**iscussion follows concerning the animal soul and its material through something passed over and something to be passed over. Beings existing in the world and invigorated by certain external agents... in that which they are generated through an effusion, or through a medium of instrumental power of an alien elemental matter. This matter, once excited and invigorated, leads the matter through certain external actions, just as in the case where the most perfect bodies are generated; for **the sun and man generate man** <note>A famous principle from Aristotle’s "Physics" (original: "sol et homo generant hominem"), suggesting that human life requires both biological parents and the universal life-giving energy/heat of the sun.</note>, according to **Aristotle** <note>original: "ar'"</note> and the female matter. This matter has in itself the mode of an agent, both artificial and natural, insofar as it confirms its presence through the **natural heat** existing there. Nature is thus excited and invigorated, and the natural agent—insofar as it receives excitation and vigor—recognizes what occurs in plants, though not in an artificial way. For this reason, pregnant women do not suffer from the **menstrual flow** <note>original: "menstruo fluu"</note>, as Aristotle says in his book ***On Animals*** <note>original: "de animalibus"</note>, because the fetus is nourished by it instead. From this, it is evident that the being of animals is not drawn solely from the female matter, but from the **male seed** <note>original: "semine masculi"</note> when it is generated, though the woman contributes to the bringing forth of that existence.

<meta>This page transitions from the physical generation of life (discussed on the previous page) to a deep philosophical and theological debate regarding the nature of matter and form. It explores how a body remains the same "thing" through change, specifically addressing the "plurality of forms" theory.</meta>

Against this **matter** <term>materia: the physical "stuff" that makes up the world, which medieval thinkers believed required a "form" to give it specific characteristics.</term>, some others have found themselves to think otherwise, separately and wisely, though it is not said more properly; and it ought to be desired [that] if he had treated natural things sensibly, it is said he would have surely studied better. Others, however, posit that they are bodies; yet in one body there would be as many numerous bodies, which to you cannot be anything.

No less wise men indeed say that all the preceding **forms** <term>forma: the organizing principle or essence that makes a thing what it is (e.g., the "form" of a chair makes wood into a chair).</term> were there in the matter, and that matter of this kind, through its operation toward a complete form, allows all things to have their full actual being—namely, even the base forms, in whatever way. And yet, whatever matter has, it possesses all the being that is in it potentially through its operation toward the final form.

Whence they say it is not inconsistent for several forms to be in the same body <unclear>onc</unclear> individual forms, so that there is a material and vocal being for all, from the individual to the common, until the being is filled; except that which remains in itself in act is also in potentiality through its operation toward another thing which is more fully in act.

They prove this opinion through many natural and theological reasons and authorities, [addressing] the opinion from which it would follow that a **living body** and a **dead body** would not be the same thing in number. For they argue that a dead body exists through one form, while a living body exists through another form and the **soul** <term>anima</term>. From this, it follows that the body which was sanctified would not be the same as the body that is resurrected, and many other such things follow from this.

Wherefore, in every way, they commonly posit—though not everyone is able to show so many things remaining—that there are **two forms** in a human being: namely, the **rational soul** <term>anima rationalis</term> and the **form of corporeity** <term>forma corporeitatis: the philosophical idea that there is a specific "form" that makes a body a physical body, independent of the soul that makes it alive.</term>. In other things, however, they say there is only one form. They prove this with certain natural arguments and two arguments of faith, to which they say it is in no way possible to respond.

Regarding the supposition and the time, the whole substance of the bread is transformed into the **Body of Christ** <note>original: "corpus Christi"</note>. Therefore, if the whole is transformed into the whole, it changes its nature in every part. For properly, in all transformations, matter [is changed] into the previous matter of the things, unless when form is changed into form and matter into matter. Just as the matter of the bread will be changed into the matter of the body, which is the body, and the form of the bread into the soul. And because all the other forms in the bread do not have that into which they may be transformed—since the whole bread will not be changed into the Body of Christ—it is not [fixed?], but it is said just as matter is changed into the matter of Christ in its entirety.

<meta>This page continues the investigation into the "plurality of forms," specifically moving from the general theory to the specific examples of nutrition (bread becoming flesh) and the development of the embryo.</meta>

...and the **form of bread** <note>original: "forma panis"</note> which is changed into the soul, and what is said regarding bread is also said in other things. Therefore, however, we see through effects, and so I say that besides the forms informing the same **matter** <term>materia: the underlying physical substance that receives different forms or identities.</term> in the same being of the composite, it is not however that... a second opinion is given and it is true that **generation** <term>generatio: the process of a new thing coming into being.</term> is somewhat diminished.

To which it must be understood that every form in matter, which by **corruption** <term>corruptio: the passing away or destruction of a thing's form (the opposite of generation).</term> is corrupted from its agent, as **Aristotle** <note>Aristotle (384–322 BC) was the primary authority for medieval natural philosophy.</note> says, is placed in an instrument. Thus by art and not corrupted, the agent acts upon all things in their origin, acting so that it might be perfected, so that it might be brought forth, just as it is in all things.

For while fire acts upon wood to draw out fire, its **heat** <note>original: "caliditas"</note> acts against the coldness; then it happens, then it does not. Fire is given first in potentiality, it is moved first, and through this its action is fulfilled, just as it is said by Aristotle that in the members, symbols of health are said... which is to say it is consumed even by fire as if by water.

If, therefore, there is some form in the worldly matter from which generation arises, disposing it toward the **vegetative** <term>anima vegetativa: the lowest level of the "soul" in medieval thought, responsible for growth and nutrition, shared by plants and animals.</term>; since therefore nothing else is led out, that vegetative [state] is destroyed, yet that prior form of the vegetative remains.

The form, however, which is not given... with the vegetative, since it does not have that by which it might be destroyed, since they will not remain in the greatest things, and since it is not hindered from giving the vegetative; in the middle is found a mass already preserved, having the **form of corporeity** <note>forma corporeitatis: a controversial philosophical concept of a basic form that makes a body "a body" before it even has a soul.</note> and having the worldly form. They are said... because it has a nature from the worldly form of vegetating, and so it is with certain other things.

This mass, however, having been preserved through the members by the power of the vegetative soul, at one time... they are refuted and expelled through... and the forms remain, not as things governed, for all those forms will fail in that one [final form]. The remaining mass is directed toward the vegetative and **sensitive** <term>anima sensitiva: the level of soul that allows for perception and movement, shared by animals and humans.</term> [souls], according to the disposition of whichever member. And if there should remain **primary matter** <term>materia prima: pure potentiality; matter without any form at all.</term> and the vegetative and sensitive forms, there is no doubt that the remaining mass could not be... and especially the members increased through the vegetative power, since they are not contrary to the worldly forms, nor is the sensitive soul contrary to the vegetative soul, nor the [rational] soul...

In two ways the advent of matter... first they are able to be perfected and increased. We see also that some form of the soul is within, as in the **embryo** <note>The author is discussing the "succession of souls" theory, where an embryo is thought to first have a vegetative soul, then a sensitive one, and finally a rational one.</note> and in the whole order of which Aristotle spoke, and which is in that...

<meta>This page continues the scholastic debate on whether a single human body possesses only one "form" or multiple nested "forms" (the plurality of forms). The author is specifically applying this theory to the development of an embryo and the persistence of physical traits after death.</meta>

<margin><unclear>Aristotle...</unclear></margin>
From a certain point of view, it is said that in that **embryo** <term>embryo: the developing organism before birth, which medieval thinkers believed underwent stages of "souls" (growth, then feeling, then reason).</term>, bones and [other parts]... since therefore we see that the form of these things does not cease when the **rational soul** <term>anima rationalis: the highest level of the soul, unique to humans, responsible for intellect and will.</term> arrives, except that they might cease to exist independently and instead exist through the rational soul. Hence, the form here does not differ from the other, and I could say that bone is one thing in its essence—becoming a finished whole—and belongs to the body in terms of its **sensibility** <note>original: "sensibilitate"</note>. In this way, the **sensitive form** <term>anima sensitiva: the soul shared by humans and animals, responsible for movement and perception.</term> and **vegetative form** <term>anima vegetativa: the soul shared by humans, animals, and plants, responsible for growth and nutrition.</term> would not cease in animals.

It seems that the forms of the **elements** <note>The four elements: earth, water, air, and fire.</note> exist within the form itself, just as mixtures and compositions appear. Experiments and induction—regarding **tin** <note>original: "stagno"</note> and many other things—demonstrate this. It is also said that from the rational soul, just as from the sensitive and vegetative souls, it proceeds that since this **matter** <term>materia: the underlying physical "stuff" that receives a form.</term> received a certain form (like the form of the soul), other **accidents** <term>accidentia: non-essential properties of a thing, such as its color, size, or temperature, which can change without changing what the thing is.</term> follow those accidents that were previously in it.

Consider, for example, when the sensitive soul ceases at death. This matter is immediately informed by a certain form that retains all the conditions <unclear>eyes</unclear> to those <unclear>years</unclear> animals. It is not so much a formal accident that they cease in this state; rather, they remain and are introduced so imperceptibly that they do not change in any great quantity. To the extent that through some quantitative experiment it cannot be [detected], nor can so many qualities and colors be identified, already the same **essence** <unclear>essentia</unclear> can be declared. Its disposition is of the same quantity; they do not arrive when there is a form, nor does any matter change quantity in any respect, just as the disposition cannot be in any other matter than in the same bodily matter. When every bodily form remains, the accidents also remain. And because this form does not cease whenever the sensitive soul ceases, therefore all accidents remain in the same way as we see in animals.

This is seen in **choler** <note>original: "colera"; also known as yellow bile.</note> and **blood** <note>original: "sanguine"</note>. Their colors differ from blood naturally, since just as blood proceeds immediately after death from the <unclear>vessel</unclear> by the power of heating, and by every <unclear>light</unclear> of retribution, so too choler is produced by the <insert>virtue</insert> of heating and by every drying process. The indisposition of the form of choler, blood, **melancholy** <note>black bile</note>, and **phlegm** <note>The four humors: blood, yellow bile (choler), black bile (melancholy), and phlegm, which were thought to govern health.</note> is clear. It is evident that the distinction between them and their operations proceed from one and the same form. Through this, it is clear to everyone that there can be **multiple forms** <note>original: "plures formas"</note> in the same existing body, whatever they all may be.

<meta>This page continues the scholastic investigation into the "plurality of forms," specifically moving from the biological development of an embryo to the metaphysical transformation that occurs during the Eucharist (transubstantiation). The author is comparing how physical matter and form change in natural generation versus divine miracle.</meta>

...material and potential through comparison to the **ultimate form** <term>forma ultima: the final, perfecting "blueprint" or soul that completes a being.</term>, which is itself perfect, created, and complete, so that all [previous] forms are destroyed by its action. 

Just as they might remain [otherwise], the argument concerning faith says that the **Divine Being** <note>original: "divinum esse"</note> was made, and yet is nothing [of that sort]; thus faith and the Church speak of all things past—as the Saints say—not because it is possible for the past not to have been, but because it is in total stillness and finds no place in the heavens from which it might live. Thus, a fallacy appears if it is said to have been "made." 

And as **Aristotle** <note>original: "ar̄"</note> says in the book *On Generation* <note>original: *de generatione*</note>, in such generations there is a total transmutation, and it cannot be [returned] to the past because the whole generated thing is already in whatever is generated. In every generated thing, the bread is added to the **Body of Christ** <note>original: "corpus Christi"</note>, not within the bread, nor should the form of bread remain so that it looks out from a part.

Rather, the essence of the bread, which is divided from its form, and its matter, and even the matter of the bread, are converted into the matter of the Body of Christ, or indeed into the Body of Christ alone. And the form of the bread is converted into the Soul and Divinity, or indeed into the Body of Christ and the Soul. If the form [of the bread] is converted solely into the Body itself, and the form into the Soul and Divinity, then everything in the Body of Christ is His own. 

But regarding the **form of corporeity** <term>forma corporeitatis: the philosophical concept of the 'form' that makes a physical body a body, distinct from the soul.</term>, it would be necessary that it converts into one [substance] of bread. Otherwise, it would not be a total transmutation, because something of Christ would be in it, yet the bread would not be **transubstantiation** <term>transubstantia: the change of the entire substance of bread into the substance of the Body of Christ.</term> if the form remained in Christ. 

If the form of the bread is converted into the Soul and Divinity, as is taught in the Church—divided from the Divinity—it is necessary that two things be mutually transmuted. And thus it is said from divine forms that one thing in the bread is converted into the Soul, and another into God. But the matter of the bread seems to go into the Body and the Soul, and the form into the Divinity. How can this be, and truly be in three ways? As in the first, it is converted into the matter, and into the form of corporeity, and into the Soul of Christ. 

But if it is converted into the Body of Christ and the matter of the form, then it follows that something will be in Christ that was not mentioned before—that it was not transubstantiated, nor was the whole of whatever exists [converted] into the whole. This argument is more about the proposition than the purpose; nor can it be that <unclear>hizō</unclear> indeed in the whole that is possible in a part. If there is no place in all the heavenly parts, it shall be placed [elsewhere], and another argument [follows].

<meta>This page continues the scholastic debate on the "plurality of forms." It concludes the argument that a single "ultimate form" (the soul) contains the powers of all subordinate forms, using a political analogy of a king and his officers, before transitioning into a new chapter specifically about the nature of the five senses in animals.</meta>

That every **ultimate form** <term>forma ultima: the final, perfecting "blueprint" or soul that completes a being</term> possesses the power of all other forms; if these [prior forms] remained together, they would already exist in nature and not as parts of nature. Regarding this, I say that it has been said that all things have the potential for others, such that this potential is led into **actuality** <term>actus: the state of being fully realized or "active" rather than just possible</term> in those forms; they do not wish to depart yet, and furthermore, they have the power of all forms. Since all forms are occupied, it is said of every such work that it is brought to actuality by an aid, provided that aid has a certain **passive quality** <note>original: "passio"</note>. And because nature is occupied, as has been said through the will, and thus they are hindered, the very act gave to nature the aids of other forms, so that the potential of the forms is compatible with other forms. When it completes and perfects a human, it performs its operations, such as the power for the **sensible form** <note>original: "formam sensibilem"</note> which prepares the sense in performing its operations through those very forms.

And of this a sensible example can be given: a **king** <note>original: "rex"</note> has power in his kingdom over all his **bailiffs** <note>original: "ballivorum" — local administrative officers who represent the king's authority</note>, yet he himself cannot be present in one place and the whole kingdom at once. Because he is occupied with higher matters, he does not place his own power everywhere, but in all places he is able to preside through bailiffs who carry out the royal power. This is not for his own sake but for the kingdom, whence their power is in his power through their operation for the king. So it is in the forms, because the aforementioned forms do not remain in nature within the matter, but because the soul is **complex** <note>original: "complex"</note>, it cannot perform every action by itself. Therefore, aids were given to it in the form of smell, and hearing, and sight, and touch; which could not exist if they remained in the manner of a single form, for then hearing would not differ from sight. This is said regarding all forms, except for how the ultimate form remains when it arrives in matter.

---

### Chapter Seven: On Sensation

<image-desc>A large blue decorative initial "E" with extensive red pen-flourishing extending into the left margin where there is a vertical flourished line.</image-desc>

The being of animal bodies exists in those with a **sensitive soul** <term>anima sensibilis: the level of soul responsible for perception and movement, shared by humans and animals</term>, in some more perfect and in others less so. This is apparent through the aforementioned senses: the spirits of sight for seeing, of hearing, of tasting, of smelling, and of touching. For some animals lack these and are not able to have them, as they only possess **touch** <note>original: "tactus"</note> and no other sense. This is concluded most especially because it is clearly seen that the sense of touch comes immediately from the sensible object, more so than the other senses. Some animals...

<summary>The author explains that the soul (the ultimate form) uses the five senses as specialized "aids" to interact with the world, much like a king uses bailiffs to govern. The text then begins a detailed study of sensation, noting that touch is the most fundamental sense found even in simple animals.</summary>
<keywords>ultimate form, actuality, sensitive soul, five senses, touch, bailiff analogy, scholastic philosophy, nature of animals</keywords>

<meta>This page follows the discussion of how the "ultimate form" (the soul) governs the lower powers of a being. ...[some beings] have two senses, some three, and some four. Those, however, are **perfect beings** <term>perfecta entia: beings that have reached the full sensory potential of their kind</term> which possess five senses.

**Concerning the nature of the senses themselves, the manner of their perception, and their objects, it will be sufficient to look to the compilation of the first and second books of *On the Soul* <note>original: "de anima" — referring to Aristotle's foundational work on psychology and biology</note>. We have also excellently treated the "figure of art" and logic in those same books *On the Soul*. Therefore, because these matters do not belong to the essence of this specific treatise, but rather to the general nature of animals, we shall omit them here.**

# Second Treatise: On the Being and Essence of Accidents

<margin>
<image-desc>A large, ornamental red initial 'Q' with intricate blue and red pen flourishes extending into the left margin and downward.</image-desc>
Book the Second
</margin>

**S**ince we have already treated the reasoning concerning **being** <term>esse: the act of existing</term> and **essence** <term>essentia: the "what-ness" or core definition of a thing</term>, we must now fulfill this little work by discussing being and essence within the **accidental categories** <term>accidentalia predicamentis: traits like size or color that can change without changing what the thing is</term>, such as **quantity** and **quality**, and those remaining categories previously mentioned. One must consider these specifically and more generally as they relate to "the good," because although the essence of accidents has its own character, they never exist independently in the same way a substance does. Every thing that exists within a category does not have an essence except insofar as it proceeds from the operations of things. All such things are operations of things, and because they do not have a common existence, they cannot exist by themselves. 

Since they have being—and their being is included in their definition—just as **matter** exists in matter, they proceed from the essential properties of a **substance** <note>original: "substantie"</note>. Because all things in the division of reason regarding accidents have their being in every genus, and do not differ in being from the substance of that genus, they create a definition for an object based on some essence. For example, "man" in his total proper nature exists in these works, though some speak of it differently.

They say that accidents are found in their own right as quantity and quality; because they have their own existence, they are sent by God as a partial infusion. They have a part in existence and in the dominion of the soul, which cannot be supposed to have being except in the "composition of art," or what is more commonly called **objective being** <note>original: "obiectiva"</note>. Therefore, a human appears more operational in the division of accidents. I say that there are certain **essential accidents** <term>accidentia essentialia: properties that naturally flow from a thing's nature, like a human's ability to laugh</term>, such as the habit of sound, which result in quantities and qualities. Others, however, are real, yet they are not absolute substances; their being is always defined in "respect" to something else. No matter how much this "respect" is something real, it is not called an essence in the categories except within the **subject** <term>subiecto: the underlying thing (like a body) that "holds" the accident (like a tan)</term> in which it exists, as can be seen in the reasoning below. Furthermore, other accidents are **intentional** <term>intentionalis: existing in the mind or through perception rather than in physical matter</term>; these do not differ from the real ones except that they always exist according to their "intentional being," as is the case with all other animal categories.

<meta>Catchword at bottom right: in other [things]</meta>

<summary>This page marks a transition from discussing the sensory powers of animals to a metaphysical study of "accidents"—the non-essential properties of things. The author distinguishes between real properties and "intentional" properties that exist in the mind or perception.</summary>

--- Page 60 ---
<meta>This page continues the investigation into "accidents"—properties like size and color—specifically focusing on how Quantity and Quality relate to the nature of a physical object and its participation in the Divine Goodness.</meta>

...from quantity, qualities, and nothing else. For a clearer understanding of all these matters, note that some things pertain to the nature and measure of a composing object and its inward nature <note>original: "natura mtima" — likely *natura intima*</note>. Even if these share the same evident existence, they nevertheless arise as quantities. As the wise say, there is a twofold quantity: **indeterminate** <term>quantitas confusa: quantity that is vague or not yet measured, like "some amount" of matter</term> and **determinate** <term>quantitas determinata: quantity that has specific dimensions or numbers, like "three feet long"</term>.

Indeterminate or "confused" quantity is said to be the same in principle as **physical matter** <term>materia corporalis: the basic "stuff" of the universe that lacks specific form</term>. However, determinate quantity is said to be that same production when viewed as separate from the object. ¶ Such active things exist in such a way that all bodily qualities are present from the object by means of **alteration** <term>alteratione: the process of change in quality, such as a fruit ripening or water heating</term>. These qualities depend more upon the logic and the object itself than they do upon its quantity. I think, therefore, that quantities and qualities differ from one another, even though they have the same being. They do not differ in their actual existence when produced through the division of objects. Thus, as long as a thing can have actual being, it has **bodily existence** <term>esse corporatum</term>; and the same act that gives it the existence of being divided also gives it bodily being.

¶ The reason is that insofar as a thing stands above any other creature, it participates greatly in the **Divine Goodness** <note>original: "divina bonitate" — the medieval belief that all things exist only by "sharing" in God's perfect being</note>. For example, the soul can exist within its own nature, yet it cannot be a spiritual object while also being a body. ¶ But that which is of the lowest order does not participate in the Divine Goodness in such a way that it can exist as a completed act; in that case, it requires a body. ¶ I say that two things occur: the spiritual mode participates in the Divine Goodness because it does not possess a creature as its own property, and it is more perfect in all their beauty because it has a part of that same nature. For it is much more inward <unclear>cynam</unclear> so that it might have a fulfillment of being, which nature gave to them in their existence. They say, however, of quantities and qualities that quantities have the same nature and logic and being, whether they are mixed together or divided.

¶ Therefore, they cannot have existence of themselves, since by the necessity of **composition** <term>compositio: the way different parts or properties are combined into a single being</term> it follows that **accidents** <term>accidentia: properties like "being tall" or "being green" that are not essential to what a thing is</term> are acquired through a relationship rather than through nature itself in terms of existence. ¶ Hence, when the composite is in potentiality toward actual divided things, those actual things will have the same existence for all; everything will mix in the same way, and the same thing can actually exist in the division of the composition.

¶ Furthermore, some things are **real** <term>reales: things that exist in the world independently of our thoughts</term> while others are **rational** <term>rationales: things that exist only as concepts or "names" in the mind</term>. Real things were divine parts, while the conceptual things <note>original: "praenominales" — likely referring to nominal or logical categories</note> possess a determined and indeterminate logic. Real things are said to show something real, yet they are not "being" itself, because "being" refers to something **absolute** <term>absolutum: something that exists on its own without needing a relationship to something else</term>, whereas a real relation only refers to a respect or relationship. Hence, two things in these...

...by itself and placed upon the object, yet it is not of itself the object; rather, **fatherhood** <term>paternitas: the state or relation of being a father</term> is a certain **relation** <term>respectus: a way one thing points to another, like a father to a son</term> toward the son and does not state anything absolute insofar as it is a thing. But this is not so in every way, yet in some way, by reason of the father, the father differs as a father from the son. And yet, fatherhood does not essentially differ from **sonship** <term>filiatio</term>, just as it is said that the **eighth motion** <note>original: "motus octavus" — likely referring to the motion of the eighth celestial sphere (the stars) in medieval cosmology, used here as a logical analogy</note> differs rationally because that motion has two real relations: one to the father, the other to the son. Because truly, relations in themselves—at least some relations—do not have absolute being insofar as they are relations; and operations do not have it, nor does the sound of **generation** <term>generatio: the act of producing or bringing something into being</term>. 

However, all other **categories** <term>praedicamenta: the ten ways Aristotle classified all things that exist, such as substance, quality, or place</term> are **intentional** <term>intentionalia: existing in the mind or logic rather than as physical objects</term> and are not real except through these relations in the mind, and similarly. Because it does not differ from time except according to number. **Action** and **passion** <term>actio and passio: the categories of "doing something" and "having something done to you"</term> differ by reason and limit. From another perspective, the reason is that these categories of themselves express nothing but an **intention** <term>intentio: a mental concept or the way the mind "points" at an object</term>. 

And all things are real where existence is, such as the rational existence of the intentions of those three categories: where "quantity" is as "when," and "where" is as a "relation." It expresses **act** and **passion**; it is of **quality**, which is called **potential** and **habit** <term>potentia et habitus: the capacity to do something and the settled disposition or skill to do it</term>. From this, for all things by which actions have existence separated from reason, just as with **accidents** <term>accidentia: properties like color or size that cannot exist without a physical subject to live in</term>, they do not have existence in themselves, and so on, concerning which existence has already been discussed.

Since in the previous treatises I have already discussed **real being** <term>esse reale: actual physical or metaphysical existence</term> at the highest level as a way of introduction, I now wish to discuss **intentional** or **logical being**, though some things must be defined first. <meta>This page continues the metaphysical investigation into how the mind (soul) interacts with the world, specifically focusing on "intentional being"—how things exist in our perception versus how they exist in reality. For it is of an **intentional** nature, and this very **intention** <term>intentio: in medieval philosophy, the "stretching out" of the mind toward an object of thought</term> shows its own emergence. From this operation, it is said that the intention is <insert>a</insert> and it possesses another operation insofar as the intention is an effect of the rational soul <note>original: "anime sonalis" — likely a scribal error for "animae rationalis" or "soul of sound/speech"</note>, because it regards and emerges from this very intention. From this, it is clear that in this intention there is both a "being" and an "issuing forth" <insert>b</insert>.

Since, although someone is approaching <insert>c</insert>, when a person comes from afar, first, by the power of the object, they are apprehended simply as an "object" <insert>d</insert>. At first, as much as [is seen] of the... height of the object, through that vision the soul apprehends that figure as an object. Through that <unclear>f</unclear> it is discovered, and an emergence is produced by the soul. Through these means, the specific powers of the approaching person are not seen so much as their movement... or their features, to show that the mind intends that thing to be just as it is. 

This intention has a double **relation** <term>respectus: a logical connection or reference point</term> to the soul. As the person approaches closer, the perceptions multiply and reveal more, and from the movement itself, progress is made; the soul sees and afterwards intends to show that the person is *this* specific individual, and how much that intention enters into the mind afterwards.

This is because the mind recognizes it to be a body, such as a man and spirit <insert>e</insert>, which is stated in the first book of the **Physics** <note>original: "primo physice" — referring to Aristotle’s Physics, Book I, Chapter 1</note>: that natural knowledge begins with what is confused and indistinct, and then moves toward what is discrete and specific <insert>f</insert>. When the soul recognizes the body as a clearer object, it grasps it, and so it goes for other things—those features intended to show [the reality of the object].

This applies to all of these insofar as they are objects. And from this, the body—insofar as there is a certain intention in it regarding the object—is called "being" and the "issuing forth" of the intention from the object. This is because it has a double relation to the soul: insofar as it is an emergence from the soul, it is called **being** <note>original: "esse"</note>, and insofar as it is an effect of the mind, it is called **issuing forth** <note>original: "exire"</note>, as is commonly said... through the operation toward anything. This concerns the mode of **intentional being** <term>esse intentionale: the way a thing exists in the mind's eye rather than in physical space</term> of the body, of the soul’s body, and of other things, including man <insert>g</insert>. But because the soul is first ordered toward the object—since in its knowledge, as this matter, it ordered the body, then the body the soul, then from that [proceeds] the work...

...even the most opaque things according to the order of intentions of the soul itself. Just as it is said, the soul appears in quantity, height, and other **categories** <term>praedicamenta: the ten ways Aristotle classified all things that exist, such as substance, quality, or place</term>. Indeed, through this operation, they say it has the mode of a **substance** <term>substantia: the underlying reality or essence of a thing</term> in a different way. And this soul intends; for the soul itself is a "being and an issuing forth," and it is the "being of the body," regardless of the operation toward the object. One must distinguish between the object according to its mode and its end, just as the body and all those things that are in a certain line of a category are objects.

# Second Chapter: Concerning Composite Intentional Being.

<margin>
<image-desc>Extensive red pen-flourishing extending from the initial 'C' down the entire left margin of the page, featuring swirling motifs and small scribal marks.</image-desc>
</margin>

Since, however, this matter was not intentional with respect to things—because intentionality is generated by looking <note>original: "respiciendo" — the act of the mind "looking back" or "regarding" an object</note> through what is written already in the "one"—this [intentionality] of all those things [is formed] according to a certain "animal-ness" <term>animalitas: the abstract quality or essence of being an animal</term> toward several things placed above by distribution or union. It is generated in itself through another nature of its own, according to works, or days, or items, or actions, through which nature indeed it is a "habit" and "disposition" <term>habitus et habitudo: terms for how a thing is "held" or related to something else in a stable way</term>.

According to a certain power given in the essence through the act itself—through the predicate in several composites—it now justifies <note>original: "justificat" — here meaning to validate or make logically sound</note>. Through this justification, it is generated in itself and in the composites. Thus, it is intentional in the same way as the Creator and Nature [act] concerning this in the <insert>fourth [book] of real things</insert>. Through the goodness by which God, looking upon them, created them as real—just as prime matter <term>materia prima: the most basic, formless substrate of all physical reality</term> which was simple, regarding the essence itself—it is made and brought about through those things which He regards only in "real fourths" <note>This likely refers to a specific division of a philosophical text, possibly Aristotle's *Metaphysics*</note>. And it is then an accident or a composite of them, and one through the leading-forth of their agents or their calling out. Those four are not led down to more opposites, nor... through their separate heat and through the showing and gathering together. Then, those things which are in other ways may be led down to what was said before, and it is justified.

**To many** other things, regarding those which, by the cause of the fullness of the work of many, generate a certain intention which is of the simple ones. They look to the essence itself, insofar as that matter is active from another course toward the "height" of that present species. For the name in that nature is just as it is in the intention, and it is something "one" and similar. And thus, "to be" exists by way of creation, insofar as the mind considers "animal-ness" within it, it is intentional. And some seem to be regarding [this] in many things, which is said to be through matter, since the predicate may have several opposites toward itself by way of the majority of nature; and that calling-forth is led down as if opposed to many animals. And so it is concerning those things that are "per se" <note>essential/in themselves</note> opposites, so that they are intentional just as they are in definitions. For the thin and the composite effective "being" is like the being of these things.

And intentional being is thus defined: it is a certain intention.

Some are virtues in a wooden [object]; the principle is not seen because it is from the soul's mode of apprehending and understanding, and thus they have being and existence as intentions, and consequently the same logical [being].

For just as names [are given] through the genus of work, we also say the names of "second intentions" through respect to the "first" [intentions]; not that they do not regard anything unless they name the second intentions absolutely, but with respect to persons and in the definitions of these things.

# Genus, which regards the relationship, Chapter One: On Genus
### [Regarding] a gradual [relationship] through which it exists in the manner of the arts

<image-desc>A large decorative initial 'G' at the beginning of the main text block, executed in blue and red ink with pen-work flourishes extending into the left margin.</image-desc>

Genus is spoken of by the soul <term>anima: here referring to the human intellect or "mind" as the seat of logical processing</term> as a predicate of everything, or at least it is defined by speech in relation to "inferiors" <note>In logic, "inferiors" are the more specific species or individuals that fall under a general category.</note>. Genus is a certain gradual relationship to a common thing according to its quiddity <term>quiddity: from the Latin 'quidditas', meaning the "what-ness" or essential nature of a thing</term>, through which it predicates divisions in the manner of a definition. [It is] a relationship through genus, <insert>**for**</insert> all relationships or essentials consist in a relationship. 
However, "Man" is not always predicated as an act of men when it is a genus predicated as a common attribute without focus on the inferiors; this is proper to the soul. Yet they do not always attribute the act to the lower [species], just as it is clear that it predicates all things of all things. Relationships which are actually able to be in acts [are attributed] to the inferiors through the aforementioned soul. It is placed more generally to distinguish it from other predicables <term>predicables: the five ways a concept can be related to a subject in logic (genus, species, difference, property, and accident)</term>, as through its "total being" and "terminal being." Thus it is clear that by its action, a gradual relationship can be established; for the same reason genus is predicated of objects and accidents—which [a specific] genus does not do. For instance, color is predicated of other colors, as "whiteness" is predicated; but "color" is not predicated of "whiteness" in the same way it would be of plants and stones and other things. And it is predicated of colors because it is gradual; whiteness ought to be spoken of in its own way. 

<insert>**To**</insert> these things, however, we say that "generally" is spoken of in two ways: one by commonality, and one for "always." For it is said through merit that something is spoken of generally for a community so that it is not always accepted in that way—as with lead, of which nothing is predicated. For while some are genera, they are not always predicated, because that relationship [does not exist] "for always." But those things of which they are predicated are many, and they are always predicated; they distinguish the reasoning of accidents for all things, such as color and other genera. <insert>**For**</insert> it is accepted generally for merit and always the word "genus" is called a gradual relationship and not an accident, insofar as they are such, as is said elsewhere and better. 

There, "gradual" can stand for "how much," just as "being" is called a genus from the time it generates; and it is said by the reasoning <note>original: "ey" — possibly a reference to the Greek root 'aei' (always) or a shorthand for 'etymologia' (etymology)</note> of generation, it was established that arriving generation implies this graduation through a more general relationship. This does not have other predicaments; wherefore the four things concerning genus do not work to graduate the difference—the genus itself is the difference and the same.

<summary>This page begins a formal logical treatise on the concept of "Genus," defining it as a mental category (second intention) that expresses a gradual relationship to the essence of things. The author distinguishes genus from other logical categories like accidents and whiteness, emphasizing its role in human definition and categorization.</summary>
<keywords>Genus, Quiddity, Predicables, Logic, Scholasticism, Relationship, Universal, Species, Difference</keywords>

--- Page 66 ---
<meta>This page continues the technical analysis of logical categories, moving from the definition of Genus and its various types (most general and intermediate) to the definition of Species. It maintains the scholastic focus on how the mind organizes reality through "intentions" or mental concepts.</meta>

...is said to signify <note>original: "signat", likely abbreviated in the manuscript</note> an intentionality, placed in contrast to a real difference. This quiddity <term>quiddity: the "what-ness" or essential nature of a thing</term> is both intentional and mutual, placed in contrast to what is accidental, as it is called "the what." Hence, regarding "intention," a distinction must be made: there is a certain real quiddity which real things possess through their operation toward a thing; these are things existing outside the mind by themselves, such as a tree or a rose. By an operation toward the thing, it produces what is called "color," which exists in whiteness and blackness. By an operation toward itself, it is called quiddity in itself, not in comparison to an external object; for color does not have sound placed outside of it. Truly, an accident <term>accident: a non-essential quality, like being "tall" or "sitting," which can change without changing what a thing is</term> is not called "the what" through its operation toward an object. In the same way, intentional quiddity exists entirely through an operation toward something else. As it is said of an accident, it is not everything, and through its operation toward an object, it is not called the "about what," nor is it called a predicable quiddity <note>A category that can be affirmed or "predicated" of a subject.</note>, nor a difference, since it is not called a species of a certain work. Quiddity is also placed in contrast to the difference of species and accidents; when it is said of many things differing in species, it is placed in contrast to the most specific species.

¶ Genus is divided into the **most general genus** and the **subaltern [intermediate] genus**. ¶ The **most general genus** is that whose rank as a genus cannot belong to another being. This is so because it happens in "naming," just as Socrates <note>original: "Sortes," the standard medieval logical shorthand for Socrates</note> is spoken of. Of a well-spoken Socrates, it is said that one first recognizes him as an object that has quantity, and afterward [other traits] are spoken of. And so, through the knowledge of these traits, the first [category] is not subject to their intention. Otherwise, it is called a **subaltern genus**, for the rank of the genus exists through an operation toward those things which are known *after* it, and then it is a species through an operation toward those things which were known *before* it—such as "body," "animal," and similar steps and ranks within the most general category.

### Chapter on Species

<image-desc>Decorative initial 'S' in blue ink with elaborate red pen-flourished borders extending down the left margin.</image-desc>

Species, however, regards that special aptitude through which it is intentional. It is distinguished from another [category] by the mode of predicating <note>Predicating: the act of stating something about a subject (e.g., in "The cat is an animal," animal is predicated of the cat).</note> "the what" <note>original: "in quid," a logical term meaning to define the essential nature of something</note> of all inferiors, namely of individuals. It is defined thus: **species** is a certain special and intentional aptitude of quiddity, through which it is spoken of many things differing in number in that which [they are].

<margin>Reasoning</margin> "The what" is placed in contrast to the genus and other predicables. Where it is said "itself and another," it is spoken of the lowest aptitude, as with individuals which are species. "Specialty" is not used here for just any specific trait, but for that which is immediate and essential. Hence, whatever is accidental is spoken of immediately regarding individuals, yet not according to their quiddity <note>Meaning: you can say an individual is "tan," but "tan" is an accident, not the essential "whatness" or species of the person.</note>.

<summary>This page defines the logical categories of Genus and Species, distinguishing between "real" things (like trees) and "intentional" concepts (like color or categories). Each of the five voices <note>original: "quinque vocum." These are the five ways something can be predicated of a subject: genus, species, difference, property, and accident.</note> is predicated of individuals, not immediately but mediately. This is because, through the difference, it is said of many things in this manner: according to how one genus, which is predicated of many, is seen to differ by species or accidents. In the same way, order is placed in the definition of a genus; they either divide a species into the **most specific species** <term>most specific species: the lowest level of classification (like "human") that cannot be subdivided into further types, only into individuals</term>, or into a subaltern species <note>A category that acts as a species to the level above it and a genus to the level below it.</note> which does not participate in the definition of the difference. This is because it looks toward the higher commonality of those things for which it does not have a predicated species.

In this way, the species is "more specific" because <unclear>it does not naturally belong to the genera</unclear>. The flower <unclear>is in a subaltern species</unclear> of all things... the most specific species, for it is everything. A species cannot be a genus <unclear>which does not</unclear> arrive at the cognition of the soul, as is evident in "Man." <unclear>Reality and the soul</unclear> operate toward the thing so that they are able to have the same intention <term>intention: a mental concept or the mind's "pointing" at an object</term> not according to nature... but from the first and the last, it arrives at the cognition of the soul. Man is recognized whenever one acknowledges that there is an object, then a body, then an animated body, and finally a man. All those objects are recognized in "animal" and "man" <unclear>formally</unclear>. One recognizes "man," yet does not only recognize <unclear>the soul</unclear>...

### Chapter Four: On Difference

Difference does not regard the individual "differential" itself <note>The specific trait that distinguishes one species from another within the same genus.</note> through which a formal cause <note>original: "cā mōenialis," likely referring to the *causa formalis* or the essential nature.</note> makes a difference of the thing from all that is contained and attributed to it... by the mode of predicating in the manner said through the difference.

<meta>This page continues the technical commentary on Porphyry's Isagoge, specifically detailing the final three of the "five voices" or predicables: Difference, Property, and Accident. It picks up from the previous discussion on how a "difference" distinguishes one species from another within a genus.</meta>

<margin>Red decorative flourishes and a bracket delineate the first paragraph.</margin>

...qualified according only to the aforementioned mode in the "quality" <note>original: "in quale." In medieval logic, "predicating in quale" means describing *what kind* of thing something is (like "rational"), whereas "predicating in quid" refers to *what* it is essentially (like "animal").</note> of those things in which it exists. For the greater clarity of all things, it should be noted that "being" creates a twofold nature and makes a distinction—that is, that existence is separate from other things. It makes that thing identical to those below it, just as it first does according to predication <note>original: "predempcionem," likely a scribal variant for "praedicationem" (predication).</note> from one another, and thus it establishes the mode of predication here. 

Distinction truly calls this the qualitative mode; for then it does not look at the "being" of the thing in itself through its operation toward others, and thus it qualifies the thing in its total existence. It regards the thing through itself and thus predicates of it "in the whatness" <note>original: "in quid." This refers to the essential definition of a thing.</note> and regards it insofar as it makes the thing differ from every other thing. Thus, this is a mode of quality, although in reality it is not a physical "quality." In a definition, this can be held as the "difference." 

It may also be intentionally qualified according to the mode, even if it is not so according to the physical thing. It is rightly said that it is predicated "in quality" of that in which it exists, because existence exists intentionally <term>intentionally: relating to "intentions" or mental concepts rather than physical objects</term> insofar as it creates a difference by way of an accident and an inherence. <insert>He posits</insert> therefore differences regarding the species of other predicables, and "quality according to the mode" regarding the differences of genus and species (which are called "whatness") and regarding accidental differences (which are called "quality according to the mode").

---

->### Chapter Five: On the Property<-

The **Property** <term>Property: a characteristic that belongs to all members of a species, and only that species, but is not part of its essential definition (e.g., "the ability to laugh" for humans)</term> regards the very relationship or substance of the subject, or is qualified according to itself and according to the mode by which the mental existence of the accident itself is attributed by the mind as distinct from other things. This occurs by the aforementioned mode "in quality" concerning every species, and it differs from other lower things in which it exists. Hence, the **Property** can be defined as follows:

A **Property** is a certain aptitude for inhering in something appropriate to a species, through which the mental existence of that accident is attributed by the soul <note>original: "anima." In this context, it refers to the mind or the seat of cognitive processing.</note> by the mode of predicating "in quality" to some work in which it is inserted and in which it exists.

---

->### Chapter Six: On the Accident<-

The **Accident** <term>Accident: a quality that can be present or absent without the subject losing its identity (e.g., being "seated" or having "brown hair")</term> regards the same aptitude for inhering and not inhering, qualified according to the physical reality and according to the mode by which the mental existence of an accident is attributed by the soul to every other subject in which it exists through predication "in quality." Hence the definition is made thus:

An **Accident** is a certain aptitude for inhering and not inhering in a subject by the aforementioned mode.

<margin>Marcus Graecus's Book of Fires</margin>
# Here begins the Book of Fires, described by Marcus Graecus, the power and efficacy of which is for burning enemies both on sea and on land

The first effective fire for burning ships at sea is made thus: Take pure <term>sandarac</term> <note>original: "sandarace." A resin from the Barbary thuya tree, often used as a base for incendiary mixtures.</note> and a pound of <term>sal ammoniac</term> <note>original: "armoniaci." Ammonium chloride, a chemical used in medieval metallurgy and alchemy.</note> powder, and one pound of liquid ammonia; these two together you shall pound while stirring in a mortar and place them in an earthen vessel, suspended in the best linen with the weave set back. Then, you should arrange the fire of wood before it until it is liquefied. These are the signs: when it is seen, by a shown sign, to come through the hole in the manner of liquid butter. Afterward, indeed, pour in four pounds of <term>asbestos</term> <note>original: "asberti." In this context, it refers to a mineral thought to be unquenchable once ignited.</note> and incorporate it by stirring it first until it reaches a thickness. However, doing this under a roof is forbidden because danger would be imminent; for if the fire should rise to the rafters <note>original: "altaria." Literally "altars," but in architectural Latin, it can refer to high structures or ceiling beams.</note>, it could by no means be extinguished.

The second fire: It is intended to be made from this at sea against an approaching enemy; make from goat skin a [container], each within a reed <note>original: "calamo."</note> so that the men might swell up in the manner—I know not what—of a good mixture <note>original: "miffata." Likely a corruption of "mixtata."</note> which is right for this, and let the concoction be with oil. Place two [measures] from them and [the text is fragmented here]. And with those things removed, let him put in more; afterward, indeed, it burns. They made for him a bond with every wooden mark. And against a ship of great thickness, the [vessel] itself being broken, they [the ingredients] become a fatty liquid—the aforesaid sign—it glows in itself and you shall place it under the earth; then [pour] the oil upon it and the fire distilling, once ignited, will disperse over the water. And having been caught in the <unclear>dam</unclear>...

You can preserve this fire for 20 years. First, for the **inextinguishable composition**, you shall make the inextinguishable fire and test it: <insert>Take</insert> one part sulfur, colophony <note>original: "colophoniam," a brittle resin obtained from pine trees, often called rosin.</note>, asphalt, dregs of tartar <note>original: "tartari," the potassium bitartrate crystals that settle in wine casks.</note>, and natural nitre <note>original: "nitram natalem," likely naturally occurring sodium carbonate or saltpeter.</note>. Let all these be [ground] as fine as [flour], and all the aforesaid items, very finely powdered, dissolve in petroleum <note>original: "perleo," a common scribal abbreviation or corruption for *petroleo*.</note>. Afterward, it must be placed in a glass flask, well-sealed with a stopper; place it under warm horse dung for 15 days. Once removed from the flask, you will distill the oil in a cucurbit <term>cucurbit: a gourd-shaped glass or ceramic vessel used as the base of a still</term> joined to an iron alembic <term>alembic: the "cap" of a still that cools vapors into liquid</term>. Distill it in due time, first with a slow fire, then a sharper one. <note>Red flourish</note> And in this oil is a nature of the hottest and most subtle substance, in which you may then dip [wood]; and once lit, it permits everything it touches to be consumed, just as a ballista <term>ballista: a heavy siege engine resembling a giant crossbow used to hurl projectiles</term> hurls pitch, by means of this inextinguishable substance. <note>Red flourish</note> **The extinguishing of Greek fire**: Note that every Greek fire and every inextinguishable fire can be extinguished and smothered by four things: either by very sharp vinegar, or old wine, or sand, or gravel, or by "earths" <note>likely clay or specialized mineral earths</note> placed in vinegar and distilled three times by fire to smother the aforesaid fire. <note>Red flourish</note> **Greek fire** is made in this way: <insert>Take</insert> one pound of native sulfur <note>original: "sulphuris vivi," literally "living sulfur," referring to naturally occurring, high-purity sulfur</note>, and for natural use a fourth part of salt <unclear>calpoti apopat</unclear> <note>These terms are likely corrupted names for specific salts, possibly alkali or sal ammoniac</note>. Dissolve all the aforesaid powdered items in turpentine oil, or linseed oil, or oil of sulfur. Afterward, place it in a small glass flask and store it very well in warm horse dung for 15 days. Afterward, distill it like "burning water" <term>aqua ardens: literally "burning water," an early term for distilled alcohol or highly flammable spirits</term>. Afterward, the vessel ought to be hurled at hostile places with a machine or a device such as a ballista. <note>Red flourish</note> **Turpentine oil**: <insert>Take</insert> note that turpentine oil is the principal material of Greek fire. <note>Red flourish</note> It should be made in this way: Take turpentine and distill it through an alembic in the manner of burning water, first with a slow fire, then a very strong one, until all the moisture has been distilled. <note>Red flourish</note> Note that this turpentine oil runs and burns even upon water, and it will burn brightly until it is entirely consumed. Once fully ignited, it can be thrown to whatever place you wish and will not be extinguished. <note>Red flourish</note> And if you throw native sulfur upon this turpentine oil, you will produce a flame.

Every man who hears of its horrible and immense power will shudder.

<insert>Indeed, let the oil of sulfur be made in this way:</insert> <insert>And of sulfur</insert>, take four pounds, grind it on a marble stone, and once reduced to powder, mix in four [parts] of oil; set it aside in an alabaster vessel and cook it over a slow fire until it distills in a water bath <note>original: "distil lap maris," likely a scribal corruption of "balneo maris," a method of heating a vessel in boiling water.</note>.

<insert>Another way for the same thing:</insert> And take one-eighth [part] of sulfur <note>original: "octosidiam," likely a corruption of "octavam," an eighth.</note>, as well as egg yolks and dragon’s blood <note>original: "sanguine Draconis," a bright red resin obtained from certain plants, not actual blood.</note>, and laurel berries, one pound each; let these be well ground together and placed together in an earthenware pan. <insert>How they fly</insert> in the air: there are two compositions. The first is: one part "stone" colophony <note>original: "colophonie," a brittle pine resin.</note>, sulfurous earth, and earth of saltpeter <note>original: "salis petre," potassium nitrate.</note>, and a small amount of latten <note>original: "laitonis," a brass-like alloy; metal filings were added to create sparks.</note>, which dissolves better. <insert>The second flying fire</insert> is made in this way: <insert>Take</insert> one part saltpeter and 1/2 weight of willow charcoal <note>original: "carbone saligneo," the preferred wood for high-quality gunpowder.</note>, and the weight of two parts of native sulfur <note>original: "sulphuris vivi," naturally occurring pure sulfur.</note>; make a fine powder from them. And from this powder, you can make a fire to be arranged upon a cord <note>likely a guided rocket or a fire-ship fuse.</note>, flying in the air like a Dragon, or swimming upon the water and appearing terrifying, and for breaking the drum <note>original: "tympanum," possibly referring to a loud explosive report or a specific part of a siege engine.</note>. For the bursting, two parts of saltpeter are given, and when both are joined, there will be three parts of sulfur in the flying fire.

<insert>Note that</insert> saltpeter is a mineral of the earth and is found in the bodies of animals and fish, and in the most ancient buildings of the Saracens <note>original: "saracenorum," a medieval term for Muslims; many chemical and pyrotechnic advancements were attributed to Islamic scientists.</note>. This mineral, along with all the earth, is dissolved in boiling water, filtered, and allowed to sit for a whole day...

...[the matter] will settle and you will find at the bottom thin, crystalline sheets frozen to the vessel. **Greek fire** <note>original: "Ignis grecus," a legendary incendiary weapon used by the Byzantine Empire that could burn on water.</note> is made from oil of tiles <note>original: "oleo tegularum," an oil produced by heating crushed bricks soaked in oil; used in alchemy as a solvent.</note>, oil of turpentine, petroleum, onychinum <note>original: "onichymo," likely a resin or a gum like bdellium.</note>, Greek pitch, colophony <note>original: "colophonia," a brittle pine resin.</note>, asphalt, bitumen, burnt-residue <note>original: "ampirigmeto," likely a corruption of the Greek *pyregma*, referring to charred matter.</note>, amber <note>original: "glassa"</note>, tartar <note>original: "tartaro," the potassium bitartrate crust from wine barrels</note>, and naval pitch. **To** make azure <note>original: "azurium," referring here to ultramarine, the most expensive blue pigment in the Middle Ages.</note>, take lapis lazuli and Armenian stone <note>original: "<unclear>armmate</unclear>," likely *armenate*, referring to azurite, often called "Armenian stone."</note> and grind it in a bronze mortar; then sift it properly and pound it coarsely. Repeat this, and afterwards [remove] all dross <note>original: "scoria," the stony or metallic waste separated from the mineral.</note> upon a marble stone so that no grit remains there. Once this is done, take your azure prepared for this purpose and place it in a clean, well-leaded <note>original: "ben plumbatam," likely referring to a lead-glazed ceramic dish.</note> pan and put it in a quiet place. After a little time, you must pour that which is fine into another pan, and thus you will gather the substance as long as all the surface has been deposited.

Then you shall remove the azure itself, well-skimmed. Once this is done, you place it in a pastille <note>original: "pastillum," a ball of dough made from wax and resins used to "trap" the blue pigment particles while letting the gray stone impurities wash away.</note>. You must have a tool suitable for this with which you must work the ball with the azure in the manner of a paste. // And as the azure remains in the ball, day by day thereafter, take cold water and put it in that vessel where the ball lies, and wash the ball itself in the small vessel so that the azure which is in the ball is well-purified. And then you must throw out that cold water and put in hot water for as long as the filth recedes. Then proceed to set it out with the ball, and every day <unclear>gently</unclear> take the dross released from all the parts and every bottom. And arrange the ball, and every day move it and pour out the dross with the ball, and you will find azure at the bottom. With this known, proceed again to set it out with the ball and pour hot water over it, stirring well, so that all this is well cleansed and all filth is well purified. Then grind it with every <unclear>care</unclear> on the marble as necessary.
