<meta>This page continues the technical instructions for preparing pigments. While the previous page focused on the grinding of lapis lazuli for blue pigment (azure), this page describes the preparation of the binding "pastille" (the resinous mass used to extract the blue particles), the production of caustic lye, and the creation of a red pigment using Brazilwood and cinnabar-like materials.</meta>

Let it be left thus, and afterwards perform the skimmings <note>original: "despumationes," the process of removing impurities or froth from a boiling liquid.</note> just as was described above. Then dissolve gum arabic <term>gummi arabici — a hardened sap from the acacia tree used as a binder for pigments</term> in lye <note>original: "lixiva"</note> and in two ounces [of water], and with these you will wash it; create the body <note>original: "carnem," literally 'flesh,' referring here to the substance or bulk of the pigment</note> and use it correctly, and the work will be completed.

<insert>¶ making the pastille for the present work</insert>

To make the pastille <term>pastillum — a sticky lump of wax, resin, and oil used to knead out blue pigment from powdered lapis lazuli</term>, take one weight of beef tallow <term>sepo bovini — rendered beef fat</term> and as much of wax; weigh them equally, along with an equal portion of nut oil <term>oleo nuces — likely walnut or linseed oil</term>, and place them in a pan and cook them a little. With that mixture, place it in water and form it into one ball.

<insert>¶ For the lye</insert>

To make the lye, take two parts of sifted wood ash <note>original: "fraxinina venti clarellatam," referring to ashes that have been winnowed or cleaned</note> and one part of quicklime <term>calcis vive — calcium oxide, a caustic substance used to make liquids more alkaline</term>; place them in a small vessel and pour hot water over them. If you wish, continue until the water no longer reacts with the lime; spread it out well and clarify it.

<insert>¶ For making Cinnabar</insert>

To make the red pigment <note>original: "sinopidum," likely referring to Sinopia or a cinnabar-like red</note>, take old, strong wine and skim it well. Then, whatever remains of it after decanting, divide it into two parts. Add a portion of lye to clarified egg whites and quicklime which have been well skimmed together; to these, add a little crushed "ice-alum" <term>aluminis glacie — potash alum, which has a crystalline, ice-like appearance and acts as a mordant to fix dyes</term> so that it skims better.

¶ Then let it cool slightly and afterwards apply the lye, smearing it well so that everything that remains may be strained through a small cloth. To that which remains, I add a little linseed oil <note>original: "lincie"</note> and skimmed lye. I cause every variety to boil and strain it into another vessel; that which remains is called the "pastille."

¶ Now, while this is boiling... Then take milk and reduce it by half; the distillation from this is called the "flower." In that, cook a little Brazilwood <term>brasilio — a red dye extracted from the wood of certain tropical trees</term> which has been prepared. Combine all of this with two parts of egg and a double portion of the "flower."

¶ Likewise, when this mixture has entirely coagulated, dissolve it in clean, cold water. Distill it through a clear linen cloth, and the substance that remains in the round vessel—such as a small dish—place in round nodules upon a board and let them dry for use.

### Red and Pitchy Earth Work ### For making rose

<margin>decorative red flourishes around initial</margin>
**To** make rose <note>likely a pink or rose-colored pigment</note>, heat one pound of good orpiment <term>orpimentum — a bright yellow mineral (arsenic trisulfide) traditionally used as a pigment, though here part of a recipe for a different hue</term> in a pan over a pair of alum pieces <term>aluminis — a chemical compound used as a mordant to fix dyes to surfaces</term>; let it rest through the night. On the next day, strain it through a cloth. And in this process, heat it first on the fire; once strained, add a little wax, honey, and a great deal of alum until you see it thicken slightly. Afterwards, heat it over a slow fire. Then pour it onto a stone that has been quickly hollowed out and let it set...

### For making cinoplia

<margin>decorative red flourishes around initial</margin>
**To** make cinoplia <note>original: "cinoplia," a deep red pigment, often a "lake" made from organic dyes like Brazilwood or kermes</note>, which I have seen done by counsel as follows: Let everything be calcined <term>calcinata — heated to high temperatures to drive off volatile impurities</term>. And in all these things, as the Brazilwood <note>original: "thais," likely referring to *brasilium*, a red wood used for dyeing</note> comes [to hand], let it be placed in a small pot of water and let them boil a little. Afterwards, let it be strained like lye <note>original: "lixivia," an alkaline solution</note>, not clarified, but entirely thick. Then <unclear>ip</unclear>... and crushed, burnt, and crushed [substance], let it be placed in the said lye in a new pot that has never been used, and place it over a slow fire. And when it has begun to boil, let it be removed from the fire, and immediately after let the rest be strained through a cloth; and when it has been strained, let it be allowed to cool. Then, separately, in one pound of somewhat warmed water, dissolve <unclear>j</unclear> of pulverized alum. When it has dissolved and the first good water has cooled somewhat, sprinkle the alum water with four fingers over the previous water with lye. Do this very precisely and naturally, so that when all the alum water has been sprinkled, the first water will appear coagulated; create a cream from the bottom. [When it is] fresh... then permit it to rest for as long as it takes to say two <term>Pater Nosters — the Lord's Prayer, commonly used in the Middle Ages as a practical way to measure time without a clock</term>. Then strain the whole thing, and what remains in the strainer will be what you seek. Let this stand until you can form small pills <note>original: "pilulas," small rounded pellets or cakes of pigment</note> from it; you can make them about the size of half a large nut. And place them upon a clean board to dry. When they are dried, sell them as... <insert>note that in place of the mud, put gum...</insert> which is put in the dyes of cloths; it sells for one pound [more?] than others, and the liquid will be noble.

### To reduce iron to softness

<margin>for the softening of iron</margin>
And you may reduce iron to such softness as lead. Take white tartar <term>tartarum album — a potassium bitartrate crust that forms in wine barrels, used here as a flux</term> and let it be ground very finely and boil it in vinegar until the vinegar is thickened; then take that which is clear and it will coagulate like butter. Have iron well cleaned and purified of all impurities and anoint the iron with this [mixture]; heat it in such a way that the medicine melts and does not fall off the iron, nor fails; then take it from the fire and allow it to cool, and wash it with vinegar; it will be ductile like lead. Afterwards, heat it as it was before and quench it in pure cold water, and it will be as it was before. <gloss>Densification can always be required, and if one joins a small amount with a little <unclear>separated</unclear> vinegar so that all the water was [removed].</gloss>

### To make Greek ceruse

<margin>Greek ceruse</margin>
To make ceruse <note>original: "ceride," likely referring to white lead pigment</note>: Take one pound of ceruse, one pound of litharge <term>lithargiri — lead mono-oxide, a yellow-red mineral</term>, <unclear>minium</unclear>, one or two pounds of sandix <term>sandice — a red lead pigment often confused with cinnabar</term>, or [material] from the wild beech, finely ground. Then take as much quicklime <term>calce viva — calcium oxide, a caustic alkaline substance</term> as is sufficient at first; [mix] all together, and then mix everything together with vinegar in a <unclear>mortar</unclear> so it becomes a paste. From that paste, make small pills and [leave] them until they are dry. Then take care that they do not appear to rub [against each other] and then put all these pills [...] so that they are not placed too closely to one another, as [shown] by experience. Then take a new jar and fill it halfway with strong vinegar and suspend the <unclear>pills</unclear> in it so that they do not touch the vinegar by <unclear>two</unclear> <unclear>fingers</unclear>, and place the jar in horse manure <term>fimo equino — used as a natural, steady heat source for chemical reactions</term> for a whole month.

Take and mix for a long time, having released quicksilver <note>original: "argentum viuum" — mercury</note> with it, stirring for a long time with a stick until all the ointment is combined and turned into powder. Then put everything into a long-necked glass bottle <note>original: "ampulla"</note> so that the dry powder does not fill it more than <term>tm</term> <note>tantum — only</note> up to the middle, so that it has more room. Without closing it, place it over warm ashes for some time until it rises with a slow heat, because the gourd-shaped vessel <note>original: "cucurbita"</note> is heated in this way so that it does not break. After the opening has been heated, place it on a ring and use a sublimation apparatus <note>sublimation apparatus component</note> which raises the vapor until <unclear>sēm der sas siage</unclear> is stopped up, and take what has been sublimated.

### For making cinnabar
<note>original: "cinabrium" — a bright red mineral pigment composed of mercury sulfide</note>

Take one pound of arsenic <term>arzeneci</term> and 6 1/2 ounces and 3 [parts] of quick sulfur <note>original: "sulphure viua" — native or unrefined sulfur</note> as they say, and melt the sulfur in another vessel over the fire. Afterward, place the arsenic nearby, stirring well with some stick until the arsenic has mixed and they become a powder. Afterward, set it to be sublimated and sublimate it in the [usual] manner until the bottle is closed. On the next day, open the bottle, and you will find in the neck of the bottle the <term>cinnabar</term> and a separation.

### For making Greek fire
<note>original: "ignis grecus" — a famous incendiary weapon of the Middle Ages, known for burning even on water</note>

First, let burning water <term>aqua ardens — a high-proof alcohol or strong chemical solvent</term> be distilled. Afterward, let white tartar <term>tartarum album — a potassium-rich crust found in wine barrels</term> be calcined <note>heated to a high temperature to purify it</note> and let it soak in that water so that it absorbs all moisture, and let it be distilled again. This shall be done three times. Let it be distilled through "virgin water" <note>likely highly purified distilled water</note>; then, for a day, the tartar [should be moved] twice or three times from one vessel to another. This [liquid] alone burns everything and dissolves any great thing put into it. Put into it colophony <term>colophonia — a solid resin derived from pine</term> or camphor and a little sulfur, and as much quicklime <note>original: "calce viua"</note> as there is of those three [ingredients]. When all these are dissolved into it, it will be <term>Greek fire</term>. And if it is first sprinkled with water anywhere, it will then ignite wonderfully and break forth into flame. It is extinguished with another fire [and] the aforesaid water liquefies all lime in another way, since it will be the "written fire" <note>original: "ignis scriptus"</note>...

On this folio, concerning the making of turnsole <note>original: "tornasolo" — a violet-blue pigment obtained from the plant *Chrozophora tinctoria*, widely used for illuminating manuscripts</note>, take the seeds <note>original: "grana"</note> and let them be ground. Then squeeze all the juice out with small linen cloths <note>original: "panniculis lincis"</note> and let the cloths be well soaked. Afterward, let them be dried in the sun, and then do the same thing again—specifically in a house such as a low cellar. Then, after the cloths have been dried, they are bathed again and re-soaked in the juice of the aforementioned seeds, and afterward dried as before, repeating the process; and so [continue] up to 6 or 10 times.

And after they have been thoroughly soaked and dried, take a jar in which there is some strong red wine <note>original: "vinum amplis rubeas"</note>, and let one and then a second [cloth] be filled. Then let hoof-shavings <note>original: "ungule"</note> be placed over their mouths as needed, and place the aforementioned cloths over the hoof-shavings so that they do not touch the wine. Let the whole thing be covered with a cloth, and let the same wine [fumes] act for several days in a low place, as described above. And this is the method for the preparation of his turnsole from the outside.

...when the star and the stone agree in the <term>ascendant<note>The zodiac sign rising on the eastern horizon at a specific moment.</note></term> or in the <term>midheaven<note>The highest point the sun or a star reaches in the sky during the day.</note></term>, then let the stone be set in silver, gold, iron, lead, or whatever metal is appropriate for it. When we have understood these things, we seek to complete what we promised in the beginning, with God’s help, entirely and without any division. Thus, we shall proceed to create the present virtues, according to the most powerful order for any person's birth.

### Third Part

**H**ermes said: After we have taught the natures and virtues of the stars and especially of the stones, and the colors of both their bonds and temperaments, we wish in this part to declare the names of the 15 herbs and which stars they serve at a given time. Since lower things are ruled by the higher, and whatever is lower derives its nature and temperament from its corresponding higher source in nature and temperament, we now declare the order of these lower faculties of our ingenuity.

<margin>The herbs and stones here differ from the fifteen of Albertus Magnus.</margin>
**The first** herb is called *ambrula* <note>original: "ambrula" — likely a variation of savory or marigold, used here for its warming properties</note>; it is the herb of all vermal herbs. It is warm and tempers the whole human body and all vices. The star **Aldebaran** <note>original: "Aldayran"</note> governs it. Its seed should be placed under the earth with a living stone <note>original: "lapide viuo" — likely a reference to a natural or unrefined gemstone</note>; it is worthily doubled in power.

**The second** is the *remaclis* <note>original: "remaclis" — likely Celandine (*Chelidonium majus*), traditionally used for eye ailments</note> concerning the power of herbs for the eyes and the enhancement of all eyesight. In its natural properties it is most clear. Its star is **Capella** <note>original: "alayaya" — from the Arabic *al-Ayyuq*</note>. Its seed, along with frankincense <note>original: "thuyre"</note> and a small amount of silver, should be placed under a crystal; it doubles the light of the eyes and protects them.

**The third** herb is called *eleforis* <note>original: "eleforis" — likely Black Hellebore</note>, which is also called *nigey*. Every part of this herb is somewhat deadly; one should use this herb with great care. I place it under the stone **Diamond** <note>original: "adamante"</note> for one's birth; a potion from which he wished to obtain <unclear>something</unclear> while this space was with the Moon and the star called the **Head of Algol** <note>original: "capud Algol" — the "Demon Star" in Perseus</note>.

**The fourth** is called *piassio* or *manubrium* <note>original: "piassio" — likely Peony or Plantain</note>, which removes many <unclear>vibrations</unclear> and toothaches and is highly medicinal. It is placed under the **Chrysolite** <note>original: "caprio" — likely a corruption of *topazius* or *chrysolithus*</note> along with a little bit of mint, anthema, mandrake, and lovage. By a divine miracle and great fame, do this when the Moon is with **Algol** <note>original: "Algoyol"</note>.

**The fifth**, which is called *saumina* <note>original: "saumina" — likely Savory or Henbane</note>, this [should be used] with **Spica** <note>original: "Aychemecha" — from the Arabic *al-Simak*</note> and *hagantca*. First, the tongue should be restrained. It ought to be placed under a seal, and let it be done when **Aldebaran** <note>original: "Aldobr̄"</note> is in the ascendant or in the midheaven, and the Moon is rising with it...

<meta>This page continues the catalog of the fifteen magical herbs, stones, and stars, following the introduction of the first few herbs on the previous folio. The text focuses on the construction of astrological rings that harmonize these three natural elements.</meta>

This ring indeed grants a twofold grace; God [gives it], and it makes the man himself happy among the nations, and it provides a great potion of peace. It draws kings and other powerful men [to him] and [grants] a lively mind and works toward an infinite vision.

The **eighth herb** is celandine <note>original: "calidoma," a common medieval misspelling of chelidonia</note>, which they call *alcupena*. Let its flower be placed under the right [stone] with the flower of pennyroyal, and with this ring, join <note>original: "consute," literally "sew together," implying a social or magical binding</note> men as long as it lasts.

The **seventh herb** <note>The manuscript numbering is inconsistent here, jumping back from eight to seven.</note> is celandine, which is also called mugwort <note>original: "archemesiā" for Artemisia</note>. Let it be placed with mastic under the <term>gorgon stone<note>Likely coral or a stone associated with the star Algol, the "Gorgon's Head."</note></term>. [Place] the herb and sea-burdock <note>original: "lappam mari"</note>... under the ring and set it when the moon is <term>closing<note>Probably referring to a waning moon or a specific lunar conjunction.</note></term>. it is very effective against [the malice of] men, demons, and evil winds.

The **ninth herb** is called sage, and it is exceedingly useful and very medicinal. This herb, when mixed with hyssop, pomegranate, mugwort, and mandrake, performs many dryings <note>possibly "desiccations" of humors</note>, provided that the stone is worked when the moon is ending with <term>Atermothalycl<note>A corrupted name for a fixed star, possibly in the constellation of Leo or Virgo.</note></term>.

The **tenth herb** is called plantain. From its nature, when placed with its stalks under a <term>chalcedony<note>original: "cassido"</note></term>, it puts fevers to flight, etc. And whoever suffers affliction, let him be covered by the people [with it], as long as he sees... he ought to [do this] when he desires the stone... when the moon is conjoined with <term>Ashameth<note>Likely a corruption of an Arabic star name, possibly related to the constellation of the Scales or the Virgin.</note></term>.

The **eleventh herb** is indeed called *atorta* <note>likely birthwort, *Aristolochia*</note> which, when gathered and blessed according to the intention, stands even in water... it has the power of the magnet <note>original: "magnetis"</note>... on the day of the Sun... when it is in bloom. Join to this mugwort and pomegranate flower, then place the magnet in the mounting <note>original: "polanē"</note> and wherever you wish...

This ring is effective against all infirmities and [aids] in moving from one place to another, and it is very beneficial against sterility. [The items] should be [prepared] when the moon is well-placed and conjoined.

The **twelfth herb** is that which is called *asilio* <note>possibly henbane or a related solar herb</note> by some, and by others *yas maynun*. It should be placed under the head when the moon is with <term>Elpheta<note>Alphecca, the brightest star in the Northern Crown (Corona Borealis).</note></term>. Those who complete this work will find it good and it will speak much [favorably] before God and men.

The **thirteenth herb** <note>Manuscript marked with "y" as a placeholder or numeral.</note> is called dittany. Its vapor is very bitter. Place this with pine and [unclear] under the <term>sardine stone<note>Sard or carnelian.</note></term>... it makes one of good understanding and... we say it is of the wise. This should be done when the moon is conjoined with the <term>tail of the Scorpion<note>original: "aido reopione," referring to the star Antares or the stinger of Scorpio.</note></term> in the descendant. This work indeed preserves the mind and memory of a man so they do not fail, and it renders the man pure and intelligent.

The herb itself is called the "Scepter of Kings" <note>original: "sceptū oru"</note>...

### Concerning the sixth word, thereafter pay attention to its completion

Now take the oil which is said to come from the <term>perfection<note>original: "perfectione"; in alchemy, this refers to the final stage of a process where a substance reaches its purest or most potent state</note></term>, and this is indeed the way: you should take that which was distilled from the oil with its residue and place it in a <term>double cucurbit<note>original: "dubali cucurbita"; a "cucurbit" is the lower part of a still, and a "double" one may refer to a Pelican vessel or a flask within a flask for gentle heating</note></term>. Leave it there for an additional hour with that which is to be distilled.

Then, place it in a <term>sand fire<note>original: "igne suni" (likely sandi); a sand bath provides even, indirect heat</note></term> or in an ash fire that maintains a uniform heat, or in a very subtle fire. It distills into the receiver as a clear, white, and slow liquid. If the heat is excessive, it will burn, and it might be called "cordiaci" <note>This may refer to "heart-burning" or a specific acrid quality caused by overheating</note> in that state. Truly, it is even better if you do not place any wood in the fire at all <note>This suggests using coals or a lamp to avoid the fluctuating intensity of a wood fire</note> for about 20 hours. It must be ensured in all things that a splendid color will appear, clearer than before. You ought to strive until you reach this point, as the Lord willed, and this is the <term>Word<note>original: "Ibūm," a common scribal abbreviation for "Verbum"</note></term>.

¶ **Concerning the Word for [the oil]**
The sign that the oil is now accustomed to the fire is that the heat I have placed above will soften it. You will see a great spectacle in this apparatus... <unclear>visticis</unclear> and from this one thing, this is the Word which you recognize. It is beloved because the Lord willed it.

¶ **Concerning the Word that follows for making the oil**
When you have proceeded to this point and any of the things we have mentioned are lacking, lead the whole process back to the first fire in the same way we described. I have already told you these things so that through this labor you will know everything entirely. Beloved, take care in all things lest error befall you in this. Since you have gathered these things yourself, let nothing in our speech be a mystery to you except the goodness that is known, and the goodness of the <unclear>essoy</unclear> which you take. Beloved, I hope for this, and this is the Word.

¶ **Concerning the new [process] after the first**
Regarding the first fire with the separation of the water and the oil, which is clear and limpid: after it is rubbed and [turned into] a <term>reddish powder<note>original: "pulvis rusto"; likely referring to a calcined or oxidized residue</note></term>, or through the way you have chosen for the fire, it truly remains until the new end. All the fire that remained in the <term>cucurbit<note>original: "cuaybita"</note></term> in which the oil was, return it now to the sun, which will then become light; and it will no longer be troubled by the rays... <unclear>raye i'm q</unclear>

Let it be made into a "feeding" <term>pastum; the process of "nourishing" the stone by adding liquid to the dry matter</term>, and this is **Word 17** <gloss>mark</gloss>.

There, therefore, at that same hour, it is from the colors that I [place] after those locations concerning the conclusion. Because in that order, I proceed in the way of the fire while it is under the rule of light, which finishes and is better. And this is said to be **Word 18**.

There, at the **19th** indeed, is another divine matter [not just] because of the fire, and in the same way while it is better. And this is **Word 19**, which I stated.

There, at the **20th** indeed, is another divine matter [not just] because of the 12 <note>possibly referring to the 12th Elixir or 12 stages</note> and the fire, and when it is finished, you are better. And how this [work] may see the beginning... in this stone and the composition of those weights. And this is **Word 21**, which you must work.

There at **21**, I come likewise to speak of the water. I set down everything, and concerning the water, it takes shape until it "tints" <term>tingat; the climax of the work where the stone gains the power to transform or "dye" other metals</term>. And this has been shown [regarding] your weights, and this is **Word 22**. It concerns the nature of the liquid and it is better. I shall speak commonly of those things which are under that light.

There, your nature is that of those who speak... but nature enters... which is double... And this is... But those who speak concerning the fire and the work of those shining things say that every disciple tints. There, at the **22nd**, others say that one part tints twelve <note>original: "una p[ar]te tinguat 12"; refers to the "multiplication" of the stone, where one part of the medicine transforms twelve parts of base metal</note>, which is its [nature]. And already it shows all that was said, and which is better, and that which pertains to the one divided, in which he provides through the rotation of the **Regimens** <term>Regiminu; the successive stages of controlled heat and planetary influences</term>. It is one and the same work of the operation from the instruments. This is the master of the spirit of all knowledge, from the power of counsel and science. I can do nothing more. And this...

<meta>catchword: and this</meta>

<meta>This page continues the numbered sequence of "Albums"—specific stages or recipes for the "Whiteness" (Albedo)—moving from the 22nd to the 28th stage. It details the practical manipulations of fixation, dissolution in lye, and the final congealing of the stone into a "beautiful stone."</meta>

This is the 22nd **White Stage** <gloss>Album</gloss>; it is of the second order and it dissolves well.

**For the 22nd**
After it has sat at the limit in a small clump <note>original: "ad pugnum," literally "at the fist," likely referring to a handful or a specific small measure of the substance</note> and is placed in a mixture, let it be fixed with whatever you wish. Then, put and replace it in the "aerial fire" <term>ignis aereo; a gentle, circulating heat, often compared to the warmth of the sun or air</term> for a day with double fire, and thus it is in this state... it is neither worn away nor expanded, and this is one of the more silvery matters, specifically of this order. And this is the 23rd **White Stage**.

**For the 23rd**
After this, one shall attempt better things in a clear vessel by the covenant of the stone, very well; and as if you were to dip a finger into water, put it into one vessel and roast it with its own fire, just as a healer [is removed] from the vessel. And thus is the 24th **White Stage**.

**For the...** <margin>+</margin> **...sustained 24th**
After this, [take] from it a fusible glass and place it in a round vessel, and put it into lye <term>lixivium; an alkaline solution made from ashes, used for cleaning or breaking down substances</term> for one day and night. Then take it out and examine it, and look upon the white color which you have observed; then, while it is still in a hard, raw state and with moisture in the hand, wait until the color remains. Then "sweat" <note>original: "suda"; the process of heating a substance so it releases moisture or oils</note> all those colors, and afterwards take it out of that vessel, and thus is the 25th **White Stage**.

**For the 26th**
After this, it is before you; and from the aforesaid things, take the fusible matter and place it with the noted oil, in which [it is done] worthily. And for all things, the oil which is hardened for it with water and with weight; and from that, the fusible matter, and thus is the 26th **White Stage**, namely of this order, and it dissolves well.

**For the 24th** <note>The manuscript returns to "24th" here, though the sequence suggests the 27th.</note>
After this, let everything which follows be first dissolved and congealed in a glass vessel; then, in the secret mouth <note>original: "ore arcano"; likely the opening of the furnace or the neck of a flask</note>, give it fire until it turns into a beautiful, precious stone. And thus [it is].

**White Stage 28**
**For the 28th and tenth**
Thus it was congealed, and the order is now complete. And from our end... produce it, etc. Let the water remain and roast them together by the hand of the fire through the stated sieve <note>original: "tamis"</note>. Let this be made from our [work]; it is finished and changed from the "raised wax" <note>original: "cera eleuata"; a stage where the substance becomes soft and malleable like wax before hardening</note>, by which that covenant is fulfilled. And by a clear image, they declare what remained from the water at first, and what is to be hardened from the oil; for you have released that oil into the water which was placed upon it. And in this likeness, and for its assumption, I say that all things are dissolved by the oil, or even more.

There is, therefore, a precious solution <gloss>solutio pretiosa</gloss>, an oil from its mouth <note>original: "oleum de ore illius"; likely referring to the spout of a distilling vessel</note> which has appeared. ->Here begins the Book of Lunaria<-

Concerning the moon-plant <term>lunaria; literally "of the moon," referring either to the plant Moonwort (Botrychium lunaria) or an alchemical substance associated with silver</term>, which is entirely like a dolphin <note>original: "delphū"; this may refer to a dolphin-shaped vessel or a specific weight</note> according to the size of your head <note>original: "capit tui"; likely referring to the "head" or alembic of the still</note>. And let it be made into two plants, round in themselves like a bowl. And we also put it into a strong heat like a flame of fire, and let it be covered with the moon-plant, which has been cleaned of all earth. And the powder is ignorant of all melting <gloss>fusionis</gloss>; stir it with wooden sticks. And when the moon-plant has been thus ignited and glowing <gloss>candens</gloss> like fire, then shake it in a vessel by itself... in which its solution is finished. And the vessels: the first <insert>1a</insert> is a twin; the second <insert>2a</insert> is another glass; the third <insert>3a</insert> is another. And thus this vinegar <gloss>acetum</gloss> is a solution in color, and with the whole substance it turns to salt. And such things are pure. And let the moon-plant be made, thus ignited first in its own needle <note>original: "acū"; perhaps a sharp instrument or a small vessel</note>, and let it be guided rightly into another vessel in which the moon-plant may be ignited and handled <note>original: "cyring"; possibly referring to a syringe or manual manipulation</note> in every way. And thus from there let it be made through a small pouring or a vacuum; the powder is a mixture, very hard, very soft, and very heavy. Let every color be made, and such a powder is for tinting <gloss>tinctū</gloss>, which does not form through mixing or through dross <note>original: "scabuo"; likely scoria or waste</note> in the work of sublimation.

<meta>This page continues from the beginning of the "Book of Lunaria." Having established the philosophical "Regions" of the work on the previous page, the text now shifts into practical laboratory instructions for the purification and sublimation of tin (stannum) using sulfur and specialized furnaces.</meta>

Then, secondly, take Tin <term>stannum; in this context, tin is often associated with the planet Jupiter</term> that has been well-cleaned and heated, strained through a cloth, and washed with very strong vinegar and sulfur. And see to this in this work: the powder of sulfur should be from chalcitis <note>original: "calcit'"; likely copper ore or a type of vitriol</note> and not from Naples <note>volcanic sulfur was common in Naples; the author prefers a specific mineral variety</note>, lest in the meantime or eventually it is put into the mass and does not turn white. Because it is the first thing that tints and whitens through the lights, heat the tin with the sulfur; once they are mixed together, the tin will be transformed. Make it into a powder and sublimate it <term>sublima; the process of heating a substance into vapor so it deposits as a purified solid</term> in a well-sealed glass vessel, form it into a mound, and set it aside.

Furthermore, perform another sublimation nearby using glass. As has been said, this is the place for sulfur, just as it was said here regarding sulfur: put half of the sulfur powder in the bottom of a long vial, and place all the "donkey tin" <note>original: "stannu' asinu'"; likely referring to an impure tin or bismuth, sometimes called "plumbum cinereum"</note> on top of it. Then place the other half of the sulfur [on top]. Afterward, place two fingers' depth of purified, strained glass [shards]. Put all of this into a very smoky, high, and thick furnace, with a pipe leading from the vessel through the furnace. Keep it there for a time, so that the vapor may be exhaled by its own heat, and thus it may boil at the bottom and be completed over eight or nine days. Adjust the heat of the furnace to a medium level on both sides, with the fire slightly away from the opening once it has passed. And when these things have been received, take also a portion of the weight of the powder mentioned above and incorporate it well; when it is well mixed, it will be prepared by the medium heat on both sides, or led into a strong sun and thus pulverized.

Take the portion and place the tin into an aludel <term>alutel; a pear-shaped pottery vessel used as a condenser in sublimation</term> to sublimate over a slow fire, so that the evaporation of the work may be done thoroughly. Afterward, by strengthening the fire little by little with a strong flame, sublimate it until it is dense, until the whole is sublimated in parts; then set it aside.

->Thus within the vessels<-

Place in the bottom of a pottery vessel <gloss>vasi figulino</gloss> a portion of the powder mentioned above to the thickness of one finger, and place the first sublimated amalgam <term>amalgam; a mixture or alloy of metals, often involving mercury</term> through a tube. Through that tube, [add] the aforementioned powder to the thickness of one finger, place it in a small furnace, and provide a suitable fire.

<meta>This page continues the technical instructions for the purification of metals—specifically tin—from the "Book of Lunaria." ...beneath and above, of which I do not say by what means it becomes fixed <note>original: "fixum"; in alchemy, fixation is the process of making a volatile substance stable so it no longer evaporates or changes when exposed to fire</note>, that is, so that it does not truly flee from the fire. Note that it is said to become fixed. I see that it is called "totally fixed" except that it is commended essentially in every way, primarily regarding its volatility and space, etc. 

He says: place it beneath and put it in a round phial <note>original: "pilla," likely a variant of "phiala"</note>, held upright; then, wisely reaching the limit of the earth at one point, put it in the sign of calcination <term>calcinatio; the process of heating a substance to high temperatures in air to change its physical state or chemical composition, often reducing it to a powder</term> and make a slow fire beneath and above. And afterward, for the materials are well strengthened with new additions; and in the end, it is said by the strongest means so that the entire substance does not become "woody" <note>original: "lignefiat"; likely referring to a state where the matter becomes charred or overly dry and brittle like wood</note>.

This which is produced through calcination is "dead," and we are taught that it loses its moisture through the obstacles of the work; first, it is leveled so that it is stopped up in the depths of the vessel. It possessed two most noble "oils" of substance. This method is made from this work of calcination with dissolving water <term>aqua solutiva; a powerful alchemical solvent, such as a strong mineral acid or specialized vinegar, used to break down metallic ores</term>. It is called a "hot mass," etc. Let the practitioner prepare the thick stone of the work and place it in a closed phial, and let it not be cut off in the fumes overnight...

...that regarding this dissolving water, you should never put it aside. Through this method, it is fixed and calcined, and thus all is purified; and by a second sublimation, the whole is made into powder. Let it be cooked in a lamp and exposed to the sun or to a slow fire until all the water has exited. 

From another work that is not fixed but volatile, the powder lies beneath everything else; and place it to be sublimated <term>sublimatio; the process where a solid substance is heated into vapor and then condensed back into a purified solid, leaving impurities behind</term> with a competent fire for a whole day. And by this instruction, the best tincture <note>original: "tinnit(ur)," likely a contraction for "tinctura"</note> is made from it. Through this, I say that what is sublimated is separated from that which is not sublimated; and at the end, it is sublimated. Mix it with the dissolving water alone, as I say. 

Prepare and sublimate it; retain that which has been sublimated, and from that which was not sublimated, add more dissolving water and sublimate again. Thus, repeat the process so that it is sublimated with that which did not ascend before, mixing with the water until the whole remains fixed in the smoke of the alutel <term>alutel; a specific type of pear-shaped vessel or a series of pots used to collect the purified solid after sublimation</term>. 

Here and thus you have the first perfect "ferment" in the work. It is found in the Moon <note>referring to the *Liber Lunaria* or "Book of the Moon"</note> that all things begin from the properties and the mystery. This is the best "Sun" <note>original: "sol"; alchemical code for gold or the perfected state of the metal</note> and the provision for the examination. And note...

<meta>This page continues the technical instructions for the alchemical purification and transformation of metals. Here, the author details the process of creating "pills" or pellets of medicine and the iterative steps of sublimation and fixation required to reach the final, transmuting stage.</meta>

How, in every degree, it is said that if the man proceeds, then the order of these things is made: 
wash and cleanse <note>original: "lavae"; to purify the material by washing away impurities</note> before it becomes yours with hot, hot water, and with it the medicine is prepared and pellets <note>original: "pillulo"; small balls of alchemical material</note> are made, and they endure. And these pellets work upon the body in the following most boiling vessel, as it will be. <insert>¶</insert> And for the second degree of solution, if it proceeds: then take the medicine of the final order, which is handled with the hill-water <note>original: "aqua collo," likely *aqua collis*; in alchemy, this often refers to distilled dew or a specific mineral solvent collected from hills</note>. That which is said to be sublimated <note>original: "sublimatum"; purified by being turned into vapor and then condensed back into solid form</note> and that which remains in the bottom, let them be mixed with vinegar for a year <note>original: "aceto"; alchemists often used strong vinegar as a mild acid for digestion</note>; and then all the hill-water, and thus to the sublimation. And then mix it with the given substance which is sublimated with that which remains, until the whole is sublimated. 

And it is said in the proper oil <note>original: "olio"; likely a thick, oily liquid produced during the chemical process</note> to be handled again; let it be handled until it is calcined <note>original: "calcinetur"; reduced to a powder by heat</note>, and as it is said, let it be dissolved. <insert>¶</insert> and from the aforesaid, we cleanse it, and from that cleansing, it is truly brought to the second sublimation, which is done thus with two powders, and as it is said, cleanse it and by mixing it, let it remain until the whole "drinks" <note>original: "bibre," for *imbibere*; the process of a solid absorbing a liquid</note>. Finally, let it be handled and pulverized and sublimated. First, that which is sublimated with that which is not sublimated, by imbibing them together with the hill-water, it imbibes the stone <note>original: "lapidem"; the Alchemical Stone or Philosopher's Stone</note>, and let it be guided and sublimated. And do as it is said until the first point, until the whole is made; and then it is prepared so that you may give the <term>metessum</term> <note>a technical alchemical term of uncertain origin, likely referring to a specific metallic state or purified substance</note> in the vessel of the heart <note>original: "hodio coracio"; likely a corruption of *vas coracinum*, a "raven-colored" or dark alchemical vessel</note>.

And it is said: first degree, second degree, let them be mixed. Perform the aforesaid way to the third point, and it is said of others that the imbibing should be increased with the hill-water, fixing it <note>original: "fixandō"; making a volatile substance stable under heat</note> with vinegar, and softening it, and dissolving it. Thus, twice as much until the water which is made from the second sublimation is weighed, as it is said, and by sublimating finally with the water-oil, fixing it. And it is the third point. <insert>¶</insert> Also, note that the more water is given, the more then the value of what precedes is diminished. Let it be mixed carefully, and let the hill-water be joined. At first it rests, and the other order proceeds which is not pure; and then you will have a most perfect body <note>original: "corpus"; in alchemy, "body" refers to the purified metal</note>, without any need for mixing, and it will <term>transmute</term> <note>original: "transmutabit"; the legendary transformation of base metals into gold or silver</note> matter.

<margin>
and the cause

It is best, however, for the silver <note>original: "lunam"; in alchemy, the Moon represents the metal silver</note> itself to be either corporated <note>made into a solid, physical body</note> or both, so that no sediment remains; and the more they are iterated <note>repeated through cycles of heating and cooling</note>, the more it is purified. ¶ The water, truly, of which the fomentation <note>the application of moist heat</note> was made above, is prepared thus: first, at the same time, take half of the water to be heated with gold <note>original: "oro"; likely a variant of *auro*</note> and first one part of vitriol <term>vitriol: a sulfate of metal, used to create powerful acidic vapors during distillation</term> put into it; and from the vitriol and the powder of the other powder, and the first, and another of the "feathered" lead <note>original: "plumam"; likely referring to *plumbum*, or lead, specifically in a crystalline or flaky form</note> which is the medium of the spirit; in each of these, when well-pulverized, mix them together and so set it to be distilled, first with a gentle fire until the vessel begins to become sharp <note>original: "acim"; likely *acidum*, meaning the vapors are becoming acidic or pungent</note>, and then proceed with the fire and by lowering the receiver; and from the gold of the first water, just as with the gold, you should hold the vessel for some time before the fire lest it be broken; and it is most optimally chosen. Then, secondly, take the threefold oil with quicksilver <note>original: "argento vivo"; mercury</note> in the manner that craftsmen do for whitening <note>original: "dealbandum"; the *albedo* stage of alchemy where the matter is purified to a white state</note> those who wish to; and the pressure of the fire is for the doubtful reason, and now it is made in the middle. Then first, with the aforementioned sharp substance, and refined gold <note>original: "obryzum"; exceptionally pure gold</note> well-sealed with a paste <note>original: "pasta"; a lute or sealant used to make vessels airtight</note>, and immediately those things which heat are made, and it will become a powder called a tincture <note>original: "tentu"; a substance capable of infusing color or medicinal properties</note> which, coming out from this water, put into this water which is said to be "for washing" <note>original: "eluendum"; the process of eluting or washing a solid to remove soluble material</note>; and it is the water for washing, and all things are thus heated; and it is done with the gold and the first gold which is distilled through the alembic <term>alembic: a traditional distillation apparatus consisting of two vessels connected by a tube</term>. 

Put of the material a very small amount and make it in several vapors, and having a good generation <note>growth or formation of the stone</note>; and now in this water, make another with thick alembic vessels, and place the receiver in cold water to produce the first coldness ¶ This water, by purifying it, is called "the rill" <note>original: "rilla"; a small stream or flow</note> with the ritual and time; it is liquefied and from it comes the most sharp water; by applying the wood, put it into another flask <note>original: "ampullam"</note> and then close it with paste. And by filtering the preceding calx <note>original: "calcem"; the powder remaining after a metal has been burned or oxidized</note>, another water is made, one powder and the clearest of the clear. ¶ Again, first put in this water and refined gold well with paste, and let it boil; and at the end of the boiling, the whole will be dissolved.

--- Page 102 ---
<meta>This page transitions from the practical distillation instructions of the previous page into a formal theoretical treatise. It introduces a letter attributed to Avicenna, moving from the physical production of "water or dew" in an alembic to a philosophical defense of alchemy against those who practice it without understanding.</meta>

### We then placed it into the alembic <term>alembic: a traditional distillation apparatus consisting of two vessels connected by a tube</term> for this purpose; it is a practiced skill, and there came forth a most beautiful water or dew, which is the knowledge for each perfect work. Here begins the letter of Avicenna <note>Abu Ali ibn Sina (c. 980–1037), a famous Persian polymath; however, many medieval alchemical texts were attributed to him posthumously by European authors</note> regarding Alchemy and the benefits of Alhax <note>original: "Alhax"; likely a Latinized corruption of an Arabic name like Al-Hasan, or a specific substance name</note>.

<image-desc>A large decorative Lombardic initial 'P' in red and blue ink. The body of the letter is red with white scrolling patterns, while the interior and outer flourishes are in blue and red penwork extending into the margin.</image-desc>

<margin>¶</margin>My friends have frequently requested of me, here and there, that I might write clearly concerning certain works which they ask of me in one part, so that these things might be memorable and trustworthy for them. Indeed, I understand the ways by which we take possession of this knowledge. It must be known that it is recognized by a subtle name among natural things and they are lost. There are some who assign Alchemy to "broken" or "failed" things, where those who are led by vanity and a certain order of bondage—as has been said—and their speech is most fortified; it seems to me that they are not sensible in alchemy, even when they command from the highest heights. It is said they consider the books of those who affirm the art, and even my own, as empty of all the aforementioned things which they have at hand; and they themselves, showing everything to everyone, would overcome their own value. I have observed the condition of those men among them who spoke with worthlessness and unfitness, of whom I say there was no lack.

<margin>¶</margin>I therefore took counsel with myself, to recognize and meditate upon the matter. I speak of the thing as it is, and if not, how it is not. For it is possible for us to dye light into color <note>original: "lumen in colorem"; referring to the alchemical process of changing the appearance and nature of a substance through "tinctures"</note>, or for it to return naturally to its own color; to us, the mixture is beautiful through its stability <note>original: "subsistenc[ia]"; the ability of a substance to remain unchanged by fire</note>, and the white becomes most white. <insert>The body is prepared through a mixture of tinctures.</insert> Since it is not possible with their own bodies <note>original: "corporibus"; in alchemy, the "body" refers to the solid metal</note> and stones—or since they might feel a lighter weight and the mark of the onyx—two things are not possible in their properties; rather, they have held alchemy as if it were not from its own nature. Either it was white, or it was one of the seven wills.

<margin>¶</margin>Fear your God; cleanse through the fire and take that which remains, so that it does not cast a shadow, never flees <note>original: "fugit"; a common alchemical term for volatility, or a substance that evaporates when heated</note>, and is not consumed. If it does not adhere, I do not follow it. Therefore, let the two figures be joined; it never perfects if it does not—as I say—refuse to fly away, nor does it enter, nor is it burned up...

<summary>This page marks the beginning of a treatise attributed to Avicenna, defending the legitimacy of alchemy as a natural science. It is helped so that a "heart-memory" <note>original: "memento cora"; likely a corruption of a technical phrase regarding the core or center of the work</note> may be made, in which fire is not let in. among us, as much by reasoning as by experiment, and we found
from leaden things <note>original: "plumbeis"; substances derived from or related to the properties of lead</note> that in itself it does not lack its oil, which
[gives] firmness and adherence, and that this is altogether
more hindering of it more quickly; and that it is the burning itself
which is mixed, adding something or corrupting the firmness
to that; and we indeed have already constrained it.

<meta>This page continues the technical instructions for "albification" or whitening the alchemical body. Following the previous page's focus on sublimation and preventing the loss of spirits, this text explains the role of "fatty moisture"—often identified with a philosophical "lead"—in stabilizing the substance so it can melt like oil without evaporating before the whitening process is complete.</meta>

Therefore, I say that the aid was solely the lead of rich moisture <note>original: "plumbum humiditatis opima"; likely referring to the "Philosophers' Lead," a base but spiritually rich substance, not common metallic lead</note>, whereby through it is produced both the rich moisture and the combustible moisture, and the meager moisture mixed with a false nature; for these moistures are coagulated so that it melts like oil through fire. Afterward, as we taught, and [the substance] itself entirely retaining it, it adheres and keeps the vessel <note>original: "camen"; possibly referring to the furnace or the glass vessel</note>, so it whitens that which is not white, while it is only in a state of liquefaction, so that it is not separated from it before it remains. This occurs by the marking or construction of the flasks <note>original: "ampullis"</note>, so that there is no path for it to exit from them. And we indeed taught this in the experiment regarding solidification, a perfect experiment, because the mixed white whitens. Afterward, because it retains itself and adheres, it appears that we have reached what we desired. And who does not sublime? And we sometimes [sublime] it in oil just as water until the fiery virtue <note>original: "virtus ignea"; the active, heat-producing quality of a substance</note> is separated from it, and it disperses the lead of rich moisture entirely, and it remains from the moisture and is suitable in water.

Place it so it ought not to be purged or moved in the liquefaction itself. Therefore, let it not be moved, since the compact [matter] will emerge in the oil or water, and this is properly the work of fire.

Whence, after we have taught concerning this path, retaining the whitest state commonly in the melting <note>original: "eliquire"</note>, I command that another [substance] be made with that oil, better and with a better retention of existence, to set one in the opening <note>original: "ore"; referring to the mouth or neck of the vessel</note>, and it is to be commended at the opening.

Furthermore, <term>recipe</term> <note>Take</note>, <term>recipe</term> <note>Take</note>, O son from among you, and again with the white Levant powder until the pulverization of it, namely that which is mixed by fire; and it is said that it is attended to and ends as an ointment in the aid, because that which pertains to whitening—the White at the point of not being removed—you will mix the two most white things more, and that which is for purifying with the application of intention, the two [purify] as a honeyed purifying.

### Concerning the mixed oils

Finally, you should know that in this way moisture gathered through fire is not separated, and this has already reached us from these oils.

<meta>This page continues the discussion of stabilizing the "compact" substance mentioned at the end of the previous page. ## On the Fixed Oil

**Q**we <note>The scribe uses a large decorative initial 'Q' with red flourishes.</note> have sought the fixed oil <term>fixed oil (olea fixa): an alchemical substance that has been "fixed," meaning it no longer evaporates or "flees" when exposed to high heat.</term> and we found it to be everything that does not flee from the fire, because it is for the purpose of liquefying bodies just as [substances] that do not liquefy <note>This likely refers to the alchemical paradox where a solid metal is made to flow like oil without being destroyed by the heat.</note>; and we found all those things to be of the philosophers, for they concern the liquefying [agents] that penetrate all things in assimilation <term>assimilation: the process by which a base metal takes on the qualities of a noble metal.</term> and are superior. In these [oils], their own bodies remain for a long time; against liquefaction it is not possible to overcome them in any way. And in the sublimated matter <note>original: "sublimato"</note> there are few [fixed] things; for all things from which it is possible to take away fixation, it is possible that they return to dissolving [under] the vain work of the fire. It is necessary that by helping and exalting <note>original: "exaltantẽ"; to "exalt" a substance is to raise it to a higher state of purity or potency.</note> them—and it is possible to exalt them after combustion and melting—so that they become a powder which possesses nothing [of its former nature], and thus the form of their colors is destroyed. Those who hear this may loathe it, those who purge [the substance], for the form of their property has ceased. Therefore, when those things are possible which do not follow the matter through them, we found and here we see concerning the assimilation of silver, either from silver which is better, or from lead and tin, just as from gold which is better, or from those things for which it is possible for us regarding the same assimilation from silver and from the lost white <note>original: "amissa alba"; possibly a reference to a specific stage of the "white work" or a particular mineral.</note>. And those which are from the whitening agents are from gold, and it is a better, rare powder but it settles; and [likewise] those which are from the reddening agents <term>reddening agents (rubificantibus): substances used in the final stage of the Great Work to produce gold.</term>.

## On Composition

**P**ostquam <note>Large decorative initial 'P' with red flourishes.</note> after the oil is sought for the white [work], cleanse [it]; and take such things from all those that are to be whitened, and for the clean red [work], and such things, and all those things to be whitened and cleansed which [the practitioner] composes for us. And by the mixtures of every fixed oil—and that which is not possible except through their purity and their union <note>original: "amictionem"; literally a 'clothing' or 'wrapping', here referring to the way the chemical components embrace or combine.</note>—we have already seen in those things which we used the manner of experiment, whose mixtures and union are powerful. Then concerning fixation and coagulation and congealing <note>The author lists the three stages of stabilizing a liquid into a solid.</note>, so that they see all volatile things [become] fixed things, and they will stand with it, and not all [spirits] flee with it; and we see many things...

# Here ends the letter of Avicenna <note>Avicenna (Ibn Sina, c. 980–1037) was a famous Persian polymath; many alchemical works were attributed to him in the Middle Ages.</note>

Averroës <note>Averroës (Ibn Rushd, 1126–1198) was an influential Andalusian philosopher and commentator on Aristotle.</note>, the commentator of this work, said: as we discern, I have chosen that in which he said that it happens that they will be cleaned of straws, stalks, and stones; they pulverize it very finely, then moisten it in a mortar with **oil of bitter almonds**.

### Here begins the sacred science of the oil of bitter almonds <note>original: "oleo de amigdalis amaris"; bitter almond oil was often used in alchemy as a solvent or a fragrant carrier for more volatile substances.</note>

And take all the decorated copper vessels to the right and twenty-eight [measures of] fire in the manner described, and in the end apply to yourself again oil and powder from the pine. **Apply** <note>original: "opone"; likely a technical instruction to add or place a substance.</note> two applications; likewise wine, and in whose dried state received in the same order: first in oil and in juice, and it goes out from the application. And after boiling, wash the finest gold, pulverized most finely, [may] God [be] in the best wine with French soap <note>original: "sapone gallico"; a high-quality soap traditionally made from animal fats and wood ash, used here as an emulsifier to help oil and water-based ingredients mix.</note> in the same weight. And I shall mix it carefully; and let them be mixed afterwards by a light fire, and roast it gently and gradually extend and fix it then with the color of the juice, and when it has been as if roasted for the first time.

**Likewise** then, likewise well ground or [placed] in the following water, and in which oil or common [oil] to the weight of half the water; in the water, a fourth part, indeed well. Through the most highly roasted oil which was mixed with the application and roasted, and afterwards well dried, and with the admission some of the soap comes out and some of the oil; and because some remained in the <unclear>arsenic</unclear> <note>The Latin "assenuū" may be a corruption of "arsenico."</note> and from the dryness of the soap and from the bitterness of the oil—which is much [sought after]—seek it from the water to the most savory [point] and a drop. And the reason for this whole thing—the two well-roasted parts—is for washing away both soap and oil; and after this, again to the sun, and the powder of gold, pulverized, I shall mix with the application.

Likewise, a second time with soap and thin drachms <note>A unit of weight.</note> in the same order, I shall roast and [rinse] with salt water and by washing with following fresh water. Likewise it is said: **Complete the application**. All this science [is done] with skimmed honey <note>original: "melle despumato"; honey that has been boiled and the impurities skimmed off, used as a binder or to provide a carbon source in reactions.</note> which is the part I have applied; likewise, boil it with the soap from the juice and stay with the honey and... suction in each of those. It ends...

...I would have continued until neither in the aforesaid water [did] the cloud [remain], even in vinegar. **This is the first step in the operation to be hidden: to grind and to respond** <note>original: "Hic est primus gradus in operatione celenda contere respondere"</note> 

In my mind, the sublimation which they assert is of great value; it is the "callex" <note>likely *calx*, the powdered remains or "lime" left after a substance is burned or oxidized</note> that is in true arsenic, specifically native [arsenic], which is produced from foul elementations <note>original: "fetibus elementationibus"; referring to the "sulfurous" or "stinking" impurities found in raw ores</note> by its own rejection. And let it be washed; therefore, through its own alphabet <note>alchemical "alphabets" were often systems of symbols or specific sequences of operations</note>, there are many ways. It is said in another way that he who governs the work is called "Just," so much so that he does not permit the arsenic itself to have anything that they might first doubt. 

He calls **Sublimation** an operation [where] you take gypsum, and [the oxides] of lead and tin, and from each a gram of whitened arsenic which is purified by sublimation. And I will pulverize the gypsum and, mixed with it, it will first give a composition, and having been led [through] this ascent <note>a "rising" or distillation of the spirit</note>, he takes the step of roasting in a slow [heat]. And little by little, work with a slow, thick fire. 

Afterward, [remove it] from the fire, and holding it [near] the fire, I was continually moving it while [it was] warm in a bronze vessel <note>original: "aneno"; likely *aheno*</note> with every caution, lest the fire act upon it in a [violent] way. And then, if its endurance is ordained, it takes continuous diligence regarding the aforesaid [material], both purified and dried, because it [must be] worked by fire, yet not for a [long] time with a slow fire. For here, the fire is not to be feared; I had a [controlled] progression. And that truly is had with all sublimation. 

It is called a "Luminatorium" <note>a "light-bringer" or specialized furnace/vessel</note> made of two similar vessels which are of separate glass, used for the accepted sublimation. **The Position** <note>original: "Positio"</note> consists of columns arranged in the shape of wooden jars in which [the substance] is placed; the <unclear>step</unclear> is raised, and it is said to be closed, and it is the rising liquor. 

Regarding the **Receivers** <note>original: "Receptorum"</note>, have them joined together, to which end I have given it purified, and entirely [shining] through some light, like water in the manner of a pyramid; truly from the aforementioned [instructions], the [vessels] are closed at the top. Because he desires that which follows, have a common lip for the two instruments... with the rest and others.

First, let the <unclear>material</unclear> boil for a night and a day until it has fermented; then, let a very strong heat be applied <note>original: "dentez fortissimo," literally "strongest teeth," a metaphor for intense fire or sharp tools</note> and then [add] the other well-purified lime <term>calx: the powdery residue left after a metal or mineral has been roasted or "calcined"</term> according to your weight, for a drachm <note>original: "dram," a unit of weight approximately 1/8th of an ounce</note>, through the <unclear>substance</unclear> of potter's wood which was in the vessel, and make it very white through the said drachm. <meta>This page continues the technical instructions for alchemical transmutation. Just as quicksilver <term>quicksilver: mercury; original: "argentum vivum"</term> which draws this last lord <note>Likely a reference to a specific metal or the final stage of the process personified</note> and of the same gold <term>gold: original: "auro"</term>, namely the light-bearer; and incorporate it with the aforementioned marrow <note>marrow: used here to mean the soft essence or inner part of a substance</note> and oil of bitter almonds <term>oil of bitter almonds: original: "oleo de amigdalis amaris"</term> upon a pound <note>original: "libra," a unit of weight</note> and placed in a glass vessel <note>original: "algenuina", a variant of "vitreus" or a specific jar</note> in suspension, which is digested and washed with salt water <term>salt water: original: "aqua salsa"</term> and pure wine. And then it is, for this purpose, a powder of a most noble body; this is **gold through art** <note>alchemy was often defined as the "art" of perfecting nature</note> from which it is digested and <unclear>joined</unclear> into Quicksilver through the work of sublimation and fire.

**D**onnebam <note>possibly "Let it be given"</note> however, it is said in the middle <unclear>congaid</unclear> and take the *dysema* <term>dysema: a Greek-derived term used in alchemy to refer to a specific silver-like alloy or the "body" of a metal</term> ... and powder the gold in the same way in which it is digested ... I say three times ... namely depending on the manner of the <unclear>heat</unclear>, and in the gold I inspected a very great light of good life. It is [the case] that with the water of the previous day from the *dysema*, let nothing be placed of gold except pure and the best silver <term>silver: original: "luna", literally "the moon"</term>, than which nothing better [is found] //

<image-desc>In the right margin, there is a simple line drawing consisting of a vertical rectangular frame with two interior horizontal lines, possibly depicting a furnace, a tiered vessel, or an alchemical apparatus.</image-desc>

**C**um with this same work, namely the *dysema* of fire <note>original: "dysemum fyrum"</note> in its place, so that it remains white when purged; and now it is digested into common quicksilver. <meta>This page continues the practical instructions for the alchemical work, shifting from the preparation of the "noble matter" to the extraction of essential oils and the purification of various salts (alum, sal ammoniac, and rock salt) through dissolution, coagulation, and sublimation.</meta>

Following almost exactly the aforementioned common method so that nothing is lacking for the complete operation, I will describe how each part is prepared.

I first extracted the **oil from bitter almonds** <term>bitter almonds: *Prunus amygdalus var. amara*, often used in alchemy to produce an essential oil rich in benzaldehyde; original: "amigdalio amaro"</term> using the water. I do not need to describe this carefully, as it is well known how oil should be made from fruits. **Vinegar**, however, needs no specific instruction.

I ground **sugar alum** <term>sugar alum: A crystallized form of alum (potassium aluminum sulfate) treated with egg whites or vinegar to resemble sugar; original: "Alumen saccharum"</term> by itself and mixed it with the more precious part of the said rectified water, so that the water was double the amount of the alum. Afterward, I applied heat in a similar syringe-like vessel. Indeed, it boiled in a rhythmic play, much like the games played by men <note>The author uses a metaphor of "play" (*ludo*) to describe the gentle, controlled bubbling or motion of the liquid under heat</note>. That is, I did not pour it out or let it reach a full, violent boil, but rather it resembled the movements within a flask. 

After doing this for a little while, I strained it through a fine linen cloth and threw away the dregs at the bottom. I gently cooked the strained liquid until it thickened. I let it rest and it **coagulated like crystal**. 

As for the **sal ammoniac** <term>sal ammoniac: Ammonium chloride, a salt used to increase the volatility of other substances; original: "Salem armoniacum"</term>, I washed and pulverized it, then placed it in a warm bath of fresh water. When both were prepared, the whole substance dissolved. From this solution, the oil that was within it rose to the surface; after the oil was liquefied, I cooked it gently over a slow fire until it thickened. I took every precaution so that the fire did not act upon it more than necessary <unclear>sut</unclear> <unclear>am'q'</unclear> <unclear>co'</unclear> <unclear>&</unclear> <unclear>sal'</unclear>. This **water preparation** is a work that requires everything to be kept [pure] after the decoction <note>original: "decocten"; a process of boiling down or concentrating a substance</note>. I let it rest and it **coagulated like crystal**. Afterward, I washed it and **sublimated** it <term>sublimated: Heating a solid until it turns into vapor and then solidifies again as a purified crust; original: "sublimab"</term>.

I then took another part of **rock salt** <term>rock salt: Naturally occurring sodium chloride, often used as a purifying agent or heat-regulator; original: "salgemma"</term> and a clean, dried sponge <unclear>istia'</unclear> <unclear>p'e'</unclear> <unclear>d'aspa'</unclear>. Having pulverized these and mixed them carefully with the sal ammoniac, I desired this medicinal medium. I roasted it gently.

I repeated everything regarding the roasting and sublimation equally. I always placed it in a sublimation vessel [lined] only with parchment, and I covered it with **common salt** <note>original: "sale co'i"; ordinary table salt used here as a protective layer or seal</note> prepared in the manner already described for the medicine. I covered that salt and placed it into <unclear>ruptorum</unclear>, sealing everything <unclear>conlinebam</unclear> skillfully.

... it is in the ascent, and because I wished for it by itself ¶ To **catch the hen** <note>original: "Capere gallinam"; in alchemy, the "hen" often refers to the vessel or the white phase of the Great Work, though the author here treats it with the practicality of a kitchen task</note>, I did not purge the **sweet holy water** <note>original: "aquam dulcem sanctam"; likely a highly distilled or ritualistically prepared water used as a solvent</note>, letting it boil gently through the highest heat, and then I strained it and left it to settle for one night. In the morning, I fulfilled the task until I stirred it diligently, lest the dregs should settle at the bottom; instead, I prepared it and used the **water of horses** <note>original: "aqua eguiorum" (likely *equorum*); possibly horse urine, a common source of ammonia, or water heated by horse manure</note>, and it is better that it be done with the [unclear] of **lead** <term>lead: *Plumbum*, the base metal associated with the planet Saturn; original: "plumbi"</term>.

¶ I dissolved **common salt** <term>common salt: Sodium chloride; original: "salem communem"</term> in boiling sweet water, boiled it and strained it, and boiled it again to a thickness, and in this way I coagulated it. 

¶ I liquefied **wax** <term>wax: Beeswax, used as a sealant or a medium for digestion; original: "ceram"</term> and strained it through a cloth, and it was blown upon by me, or I processed it gently over a low fire until a certain excellent white substance appeared in the vessel. I removed this with a **feather** <note>original: "penna"; alchemists often used feathers to skim delicate films or "flowers" from the surface of liquids</note> and continued until no more of it appeared and it was clarified. 

¶ Concerning the aforementioned water, I bring forward the sweet water, but after it has been thickened, I pass it again through the **holy salt-works** <note>original: "sallatorium sanctum"; a place or apparatus for the crystallization of salts</note>. 

¶ **Alkali salt** <term>alkali salt: A basic salt derived from plant ashes; original: "salem alkali"</term>, which is the lime made from the ashes of wood and beans. ¶ I took the crude grated [pieces] and soaked them with the aforementioned water for the whole night, and having filled it in that way, I processed it and in [unclear] ways I strained it through a thick cloth and left it for about three days. Afterward, I strained it diligently and then I watered the same diligently to a better thickness, and for one night I let it settle in a spread of warm vapor, and it became a **noble salt** which was not at all [corrupted] like common salt. 

¶ **Gypsum** <term>gypsum: Calcium sulfate; original: "gipsum"</term> is pulverized and roasted and blown into. 

¶ With the same **lead** as mentioned before, I melted the lead and "fed" it somewhat with a portion of the ascent, and I moved it with a [unclear] rod very [carefully?] until it was a powder, and then to the same [unclear]. 

¶ **Algezma** <note>original: "Algezmam"; likely an Arabic-derived term referring to a mineral dross or a specific metallic powder like marcasite</note> itself [is treated] just like common salt. 

¶ And as a wise man, I did not [prepare] it in a plaster while I putrefied the "master ferment." I put the wax itself into a [glass] vessel in **very hot dung** <note>original: "fimo calidissimo"; horse manure was the standard alchemical "constant heat" source, providing a steady temperature for the slow "digestion" or rot (putrefaction) of substances</note>, and when you receive it there, every perfection [is achieved], and then...

The method of **grafting trees** <term>grafting: *insitio*, the horticultural technique of joining tissues of plants so they grow together; original: "insicionis"</term> is manifold, but that which is more subtle and more convenient is as follows: namely, in the **eye of the eagle** <note>original: "Oculario ipsiu aquilini"; likely a technical term for a specific type of bud-grafting or "shield" grafting, or perhaps named for the shape of the incision</note>, that is, one mother-branch in one tree of the length of twenty-two and a half [parts]. Afterward, by cutting and recutting in the manner of a cleaning, one reaches the **pith** <term>pith: *medulla*, the soft, spongy central cylinder of a stem; original: "medullū"</term>. Then, leave a space for the graft in length up to the middle, and with the hollow tube prepared, the other part which remains with you, fit it with a **knife** <term>knife: *cultello*</term> from the side of the pith, and provided that it fits the form of every graft, it can be injected into one area and bound; because the outer horn <note>original: "cornu"; likely referring to the hardened outer bark or a horn-shaped tool</note> of the graft itself should not be hard. You shall not scrape anything from the top, and that which sits therein... <image-desc>Small blue paraph mark with red highlights.</image-desc> Another way of grafting in its time: and the small chamber <note>original: "cupiculum"</note> is threaded and fastened with some pious wood <note>original: "ligno pio"; likely meaning "flexible" or "suitable" wood</note> for this purpose. Once you have inserted the chamber, you shall then bind it with **silk** <note>original: "sitha", likely *seta*</note> or with similar linen. Seal the graft itself... which you should slightly penetrate with the edge of a knife, and afterward bind it doubly, because such a graft which brings forth many things does not fail.

## On the Laxative Pear

If you wish that from any pear tree [you may have] another pear fruit, do as [this] teaches: in this one [hole], pour a [substance] diluted with water into a hole that has been channeled even to the marrow <term>marrow: *medullam*; the soft, central pith of the tree branch or trunk</term> of the tree, and block the hole with a peg <note>original: *clavo*; literally a nail, but in this context a wooden plug or graft-peg</note> made of the same pear tree or another. And it will become a laxative pear <term>laxative pear: *pira solutiva*; a medicinal fruit intended to have a purgative effect on the bowels</term>, and [ensure] that it [bears] only upon eight or ten branches, but open and [shrouded?].

## To Rejuvenate an Old Tree

An old tree, already failing due to age, as the **Philosopher** <note>original: *pho'*; referring to Aristotle, the primary authority on natural philosophy in the Middle Ages</note> says in the **Book on Plants** <note>original: *li' de plantis*; a work on botany attributed to Aristotle, though actually written by Nicolaus of Damascus</note>: Break apart its roots and let two or three [substances] be poured in at a distance, and apply manure <note>original: *Assy'*; likely an abbreviation for *assythum* or *stercore*</note> and cover the roots; then within... it is protected from other guardians... a tree is dressed from the inside.

## So that Pomegranates become Sweet

If you wish to make **pomegranates** <term>pomegranates: *mala granata*</term> from [trees] that are bitter, the **Master** <note>original: *arz*; Aristotle</note> teaches in the **Book on Plants** that the tree trunk should be bored through <unclear>sectato</unclear> with a bored hole, and they will be sweet. What he says about the taste of apples is similar: that from other trees they are sweetened in various ways... But also what he says... that a hole should be made in a tree that produces very sour or bitter fruit, and into that very hole...
