The fig is a tree whose fruits are born from the preceding year, as much as the **pith** <term>pith: *medulla*; the soft, spongy central tissue of a plant stem</term> is like a leader in taking root. Note what has been said about the fruits which are noted in the harvest, and the scent for finger-related illnesses. And note that there are three species of figs:

¶ **The first species** has herbal shoots, and these are somewhat thicker than the others; they are indeed the earliest to ripen, with white flowers and foliage. These are called *amsallz* in the common tongue <note>original: "amsallz"</note>, and they are small and hardy trees.

¶ **The second species** has large and very wide flowers; they are born in the summer and are planted in the heat. They have very long shoots and are small and bitter in taste, and finally they change color and purge the blood; it is true that if they fall from any damage, they resemble crows due to their size, or because crows ripen them.

¶ **The third species** has medium-sized flowers and has the taste of wine, both sharp and a sweet fig; they are small in size and are of great stature. They have long shoots and are hard, so that they can endure in the wind, and from them pleasant **pastries** <unclear>pastries</unclear> are made. They are commonly called *casseler* <note>original: "casseler"</note>. 

And note that I have seen these three species of figs growing together on one tree, and I did not learn the manner of it from a book, but they seemed to me to be investigated by those who are wise. 

A true sign where experience should be tested is in the planting of the material. In October, which is warmer, it is appropriate; however, in cold and deep regions, they cultivate the plants in February and in May, as they do in the mountains. And it is said of the onion that it thrives in three-month intervals, in veined and deep soil. They take the graft and a large cutting. Since the **woods** <term>woods: *syloes*; likely referring to the scions or woody cuttings</term> are cut from the knotty shoots and are better when transplanted. With all of these, a stake is driven deep into the earth as a sign, or the kernels themselves are placed at the depth of a palm in the earth, and then for a time they are placed in cold water.

### On the planting of gladioli

<margin>red flourish</margin>
Many people desire many things and plant gladioli <note>original: "glesis/gladiolis"</note>; there is a danger to all if they are grafted while the labor is <unclear>empty</unclear> and hungry; and they may be planted with the elm and the little fig tree in a time that is not cold.

### On the planting of roses

<margin>red flourish</margin>
Sow roses at the beginning of winter in firm ground. If you <unclear>find</unclear> them then, you should first put them out in the month of February or in the month of March. They do not sprout [immediately], because I keep them for a year until they reach the ideal size of a foot deep in the earth; they should be placed in whatever order they wish. And from the rose-thickets <note>original: "rosar nax"</note> it is possible to consolidate them from a space of half a palm, and irrigate them. I divide them all once a year with warm water. Afterward, they should not be cleaned with horses in the hay harvest.

### On the planting of melons

<margin>red flourish</margin>
Plant melons <note>original: "mellones"</note> first, and pumpkins <note>original: "pyones/pepones"</note>... these are plantable and always judged [ready]... and place them at such a distance or better so they can <unclear>spread out</unclear> and put them in dewy earth <note>original: "terra derosam"</note>.

<meta>This page continues the discussion of viticulture, moving from the astrological timing of the harvest to the practical storage and preservation of wine, including regional observations from Germany and traditional wisdom on the purity of liquids.</meta>

Concerning this wine, there are diverse opinions, because some preserve it properly for a long time. For those seeking the pleasure of wine, they should order that their vessels preserve their specific quality for some time. We do not do this for our own sake, but so that many wines might be clarified, because otherwise, such wines macerate before their time and corrupt the flavor of the wine, making it less than it should be. 

One thing is known through long experience regarding how wine should be ordered; while searching in **Germany** <note>original: "alemannia"</note> it is found that they have wine—especially white wine—which they preserve there for a year, and they even keep it in the water <note>This likely refers to water-cooling or storing barrels in damp, cold riverside cellars</note> and the wine is for the most part as it ought to be. And I say this because this work is not a light undertaking, but I have begun this work for another end. 

It must be noted that wine is preserved much better if the worker has not eaten bread with **garlic** <note>original: "alli"</note> or **leeks** <note>original: "poyerū" / "porrum"</note> or other such things that can induce a foul odor in the wine. The vessels into which the wine is to be moved should first be washed in hot water and be extremely clean, lest they cause the wine to spoil from the outside. After cleaning, they should be **sulfur-fumigated** <term>sulfumigent</term> <note>original: "sulfumigentur"; the practice of burning sulfur inside a barrel to kill wild yeast and bacteria</note> with **frankincense** <note>original: "thure"</note> of the noblest kind, as is customary. 

When you see that the **must** <term>must</term> <note>original: "mustum"; unfermented grape juice</note> is good and the weather is still golden, then pour in the must as it comes out of the **press** <term>torcular</term> <note>original: "torcular"</note> according to your pleasure. Let it be closed while it bubbles <note>referring to the active fermentation process</note> from the start; do all things as described and do not fill the vessels to the very top, lest it boil over. 

When we see rainy weather, the must often becomes disturbed; we act to expel the impurities from it so that the wine may rest. After it has finished boiling [fermenting], pour it into another vessel, so that the sediment remains at the bottom, which is the dregs, and then taste it again. 

Note also that it is a known rule that all wine is best in the middle of the vessel, while **oil** is best at the surface, and **honey** is best at the bottom. In this, **Giles** <note>original: "egidiū"; likely referring to Giles of Rome (Aegidius Columna), a famous 13th-century scholar whose works covered natural philosophy</note> agrees with others, though some say wine is best at the bottom.

<summary>This page details practical methods for wine preservation, including the cleaning and sulfur-fumigation of barrels and observations on German storage techniques. It concludes with a traditional philosophical hierarchy of liquids, noting where the "best" part of wine, oil, and honey resides in a container.</summary>

<keywords>wine preservation, Germany, sulfur-fumigation, frankincense, wine press, garlic, Giles of Rome, must, fermentation</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of wine quality and preservation, moving from the deceptive practices of sellers to practical tests for purity and the environmental factors—both atmospheric and astrological—that cause wine to spoil or change.</meta>

...wonderful forever. 
Since, indeed, the year’s quality is seen in this manner, a certain Jew in **Spoleto** <note>original: "Spoliatu"; a city in Umbria, Italy</note> told me this: he said that he had a jar full of *luia* <note>meaning uncertain, likely a regional term for wine lees or dregs</note>, which the **Lombards** call *moresta* <note>possibly a dialect term for grape pomace or skins</note>. When the water was poured off from it, and it had stood for a long time, it turned into excellent **vinegar** <term>acetum</term>. 

Wine, however, which is mixed with water, will diffuse when poured into another vessel, and thus the wine [adulteration] is not easily perceptible. [To test for this], make **oil** boil in a pan, then pour in some of that wine; if there is water in it, the mixture will sputter, hiss, and jump more violently. 

In the time of change—and I speak of what is observed in other wine—there are times and days when wine is changed by the **moon and the stars**. At such times, it lacks clarity and becomes rather dark. It must be kept from water, from the calm of sunset, and where it might be affected by heat, frost, smoke, wind, or rain. 

<margin><image-desc>Red ornamental flourish extending from the initial below</image-desc></margin> Change also occurs when all things are in bloom, and during violent **thunder** <term>tonitru</term>, because the lees themselves boil up from the effect of **lightning** <term>fulmine</term>. This happens to such an extent that the lees are no longer strong enough for **leavening bread** <note>original: "fermentandum panes"; wine yeast/lees were commonly used as a starter for baking</note>, which is a great loss for the winemaker. 

However, these damages—which occur from the wind, from doubtful water on a day of change, or from vapors—can be prevented if a **cellar** <term>cellarium</term> is built such that it has no window except the door, or perhaps an opening on the side. It should also have some small windows to let vapors escape at the proper time, depending on the winds, the cold, or the heat. Regarding the roof, I say that each element should be gathered in its proper place according to what they say. 

Although for **mustard** <note>original: "mustarda"; often made with grape must</note> this might be done immediately, in no way will wine regenerate if it stands in sharp, acidic air; it will change during the winter. I say that wine exposed to the sun <note>original: "insulatum"</note> should be given to fill [the vessels]. Dognito <note>possibly a corrupted name or "it being known"</note> says that wine exposed to the sun on rainy days and [extra] water is quite harmful. Beware that you do not do this while the **moon is in Mars** <note>an astrological warning; Mars was considered a "hot" and "dry" planet that could ruin delicate liquids</note>, lest the wine perish through its influence.

## First, on the influence of the moon's position below [...]

<meta>This page continues the introductory discussion of the branches of knowledge, transitioning from a general overview of philosophy to a specific focus on "Physics" or natural philosophy. <insert>1st part</insert>

## **P**hilosophy is divided into three parts: namely, logic,
ethics, and physics—otherwise known as rational, moral, and 
natural philosophy—about which we will speak elsewhere. Therefore, for the present, omitting the
others, we intend to touch upon a few things regarding physics, or natural 
<margin>a</margin> philosophy. Indeed, "Nature" is spoken of in many ways. <note>original: "natura multis modis dicitur"</note> ¶ In one way, nature
is said to be the intelligence of all things in existence, which is said of God according to some, because here He 
leads all things by the hand in nature, just as it is said in the *Metaphysics* <note>Aristotle's work on the fundamental nature of reality.</note> regarding intelligence.
¶ For every substance is such a nature, as is said in the introduction.
<margin>b</margin> ¶ Firstly, nature is spoken of in that which is innate, namely form and matter, as
it is said in the second book of the *Physics*: <note>Aristotle’s *Physics*, Book II, is the foundational text for defining "nature."</note> specifically, that which is the principle of nature in being. And because 
it is spoken of in nature as a certain essence. ¶ Secondly, nature signifies motion and rest
in that in which it exists by itself and not by accident. ¶ Thirdly, there is a distinc-
<margin>c</margin> tion of nature into "common" and "special." ¶ For "common" refers to every 
thing diffused among others as a particular. ¶ "Specially," however,
one thing is proper within itself. ¶ Fourthly, nature is spoken of in matter itself, as
it is in this plant; and this was the occasion for them to say that nature is a force implanted <note>original: "vis insita"</note>
in things, from which all things are procreated. Aristotle proposes all these modes
in the second book of the *Physics*. ¶ The natural philosopher, however, uses two modes of nature among many: 
one which is named from its creation, according to which it is said that nature is an essence, 
whence this exists insofar as it is present from its creation. ¶ Likewise, nature is said to be 
that which is brought into act <note>meaning something that has achieved its full potential or physical form.</note>—for that which is, is not "nature" <gloss>becoming nature</gloss>, but that 
which is made for this purpose, comes from the common truth. ¶ Furthermore, there is here a threefold distinc-
tion of nature: namely active and passive; specifically the work of nature, the work of the artisan, and 
the work of the creator. ¶ The work of nature is that whose principle is nature. ¶
The work of the artisan is that whose principle is art and will. ¶
The work of the creator is that whose acting principle is the creator. ¶ Secondly, it is said 
what the natural philosopher considers. ¶ For he is concerned with nature, that is, physics;
and here, specifically with the <term>mobile body</term>. <note>In medieval science, a "mobile body" refers to any physical matter capable of change or movement.</note> ¶ Note also that there is a twofold mobile body: 
¶ Common, ¶ and composed of elements, ¶ and of the "elemented" <note>Things made out of the four elements (earth, air, fire, water).</note>.
<margin>e</margin> ¶ Such a body has been disputed here since ancient times. ¶ For a body is
that which has three dimensions: namely length, width, and 
depth, as he says; and better according to <term>Algazel</term> <note>Al-Ghazali (c. 1058–1111), a Persian philosopher whose works on logic and physics were influential in Latin translation.</note> and others: ¶ A body
is that in which there is the aptitude for perceiving two intersecting lines.

Thus, toward the <unclear>English</unclear> <unclear>Janus-faced</unclear> and <unclear>also</unclear> <unclear>pitying</unclear> those into those toward the <unclear>English</unclear> <unclear>Sews</unclear> <note>These opening words are likely corrupted in the OCR or refer to a specific, now-obscure scholastic debate.</note> But because it is also troublesome that the subject is moved, and because it is the same power and principle of nature according to the testimony of the first of all <term>philosophers</term> <note>Aristotle is frequently referred to simply as "The Philosopher" in medieval texts.</note>. For we know that <term>privation</term> <note>The absence of a form that should naturally be present, such as "not-man" before "man" is formed.</note> is a nature, and this is explained obscurely in the second book of his <term>physics</term>, where it is said that "becoming" occurs from the side of what is present. In everything that is made according to nature, there must be something of a subject that is made in <term>potentiality</term> <note>The capacity to become something else.</note>; through this, when something is called a <term>substance</term>, it touches upon <term>matter</term>. And he shows that through that which is made, it touches upon <term>form</term>; through the fact that it is said to be in <term>potentiality</term>, he is speaking of <term>privation</term>.

From this, he says it is not unless it is a "little man" <note>original: "homuncio"</note>, and from this, a "non-man" becomes a man. And that <term>matter</term> and <term>form</term> are <term>principles</term>, it is <unclear>common</unclear> to the indivisible and the proper through its <unclear>resolution</unclear>. Of this kind, because its being is accidental, it is from the <term>form</term>, and it cannot have such being from <term>prime matter</term> <note>Matter in its most basic state, without any form or qualities.</note> which stands before. Because the way of receiving every <term>form</term> is from the <term>form</term> itself.

He says, as the ultimate <term>principle</term> of the <term>philosophers</term>, that here he knows <term>matter</term> best; for how could he not know the <term>principle</term> and the first and second <term>principles</term> according to the elements? When it is present, it is said that if there is any nature in everything that is made, that nature is in the being of the <term>matter</term>. But not...

<meta>This page continues the technical discussion of the fundamental principles of physical existence—matter and form. Having previously introduced "privation," the text now moves into specific definitions of "prime matter" using authorities like John of Damascus and Pseudo-Dionysius to explain how matter interacts with the "forms" that give things their specific identity.</meta>

Two things are considered regarding the principles that are not inherent through themselves, but first: ¶ There is the definition of **prime matter** <note>The most basic, formless substance that underlies all physical reality.</note> which <term>Saint John</term> <note>Likely Saint John of Damascus, a 7th-century theologian often cited in medieval philosophy for his definitions of substance.</note> says is **unbegotten** <note>original: "ingenita"; meaning it was not "born" from something else</note> and **incorruptible**, and that which is generated is as if it were generated together with corruption.

¶ In the third book of <term>Divine Names</term> <note>A work by Pseudo-Dionysius the Areopagite, a hugely influential Christian Neoplatonist.</note> it is said that neither does a body have any other containment except that it is <term>essence</term> and **nature**; and that the lack of order which is spoken of on account of **matter** [is] **division** and **commensuration** <note>The act of measuring or proportioning things.</note> into an infinite redundancy of itself. ¶ And **note** in these sayings: **matter** is like a fit nature and a **potential** <note>The capacity or power to become something.</note> for a redundant perfection, everything [coming together] insofar as it stands as a **form**. ¶ It must be **noted** that **forms** are not simply in **matter** in quantity; wherefore from an **exemplar** <note>The ideal pattern or "blueprint" of a thing.</note> a composition is made toward some **form**. And this mode is spoken of as being apart from every **form**, because it is spoken of through itself but not through another. ¶ Thus it is divided into certain **essential** things and certain **accidental** <note>In philosophy, an "accident" is a property that isn't essential to what a thing is—like the color of a shirt.</note> things. ¶ An **essential** thing is that which is spoken of in the third [book], things which are not [material], such as are **angels** and **souls**; modes such as in **generation** <note>The process of coming-into-being.</note> in one corporal body, and that which is in things that have a limit and are ¶ weak in their own mode—nothing of all these is an **accidental** substance; it is that which is in things from their properties, in which the **will** is a proper [property] that is produced.

¶ A **natural** [thing] is that which is in the natures of things, since the principle is **nature**. ¶ And that which is spoken of is the division of **form** and a certain portion of the **universe**. ¶ And it is that which has no beginning or end, only **essential**.

¶ Regarding the determination of **matter**, it is said that in **matter** there is something of every **form**, and this is on account of the convenient importance of it toward the **form**. ¶ The determination in which this is more clearly declared appears more clearly. ¶ **Note** the saying at the end of the first [book] of **Philosophy** <note>Referring to Aristotle's "Physics," Book I.</note> that **nature** [matter] is in a state of **desire** for **form**, just as a **woman** <note>original: "mulier"</note> desires [a man]; everything [matter] sees form, and the "ugly" [desires] the "good." <note>Aristotle famously used the metaphor of matter "lusting" after form like a female for a male, or the base for the noble, to explain why change happens.</note>

**Passion** <note>In this context, "passion" means "being acted upon" or undergoing a change from the outside.</note> is that which happens thus, [as] this someone [becomes] **corruptible** from a **substantial form** and from actual outward things. ¶ **Note** that **artificial matter** <note>Matter shaped by human hands, like wood shaped into a chair.</note> is only a **passive potential**, just as in an existing **essence**. ¶ In **natural matter**, the principles are not only a **passive potential** but an **active** one; wherefore in the first [book] of *On Generation and Corruption* <note>Another work by Aristotle.</note> it is spoken regarding proper **matter**, which is only toward **form**. ¶

¶ Indeed, a certain **passion** is **material** and **incorruptible**, such as is in the **heavens** and the **superior bodies** <note>The planets and stars, which medieval thinkers believed were made of a special "fifth element" that did not decay.</note> in which there is an active [power] beyond the perfection of **inferior** things. ¶ Indeed, a certain [passion] is **generated** and **corruptible**, such as in **seed** and the **elements**, in which there is [a power] both active and passive.

<meta>This page continues the technical investigation into the principles of physical and metaphysical reality. Having discussed the nature of matter, the text now turns to the fourfold relationship within a subject and provides a detailed classification of "form"—specifically how different sciences (Mathematics, Physics, and Metaphysics) treat the concept of form and its separation from matter.</meta>

From what has been said, gather that there is a fourfold specific relation in the subject, namely first in the genus of general and dialectical matter. <margin>2</margin> **From which** however, reason holds a bond that is thus in its genus. Simply and in genus and in order, many things are one and are joined together; and specifically they are simply one. Indeed, as it is seen in other uses, those things which have no **privation** <note>In Aristotelian philosophy, "privation" is the absence of a quality or form that a thing should naturally possess, like blindness is the privation of sight.</note> regarding themselves—because of this, you will also distinguish that which reason's lowerings do not make specific. **What** pertains to matter is about the definition that cannot be easily made known. **Form** is an abstract form; with the abstraction of matter, two things are evident: in mathematics, bodies in whose matter there is privation, because it is in **potential** <note>The capacity to become something else.</note> for no other form <unclear>against</unclear> that which, as the other matter, first deprives, as **Aristotle** <note>original: "Aristoteles"</note> says in the second book of the **Physics**.

**Concerning** that which is not: for by privation there is an action of matter to distinguish the double in everything, in the abstract and the natural; thus can a double privation be distinguished, one abstract and one natural. **By dignity**, concerning form, about which it must be noted that it is a form abstract from all matter and the continuation of matter; in such a way, matter is motion. **The End** <note>The "Final Cause" or purpose of a thing.</note> is both good and from this **Metaphysics** considers it. There **is** also a form abstract from matter, yet not from all matter, and from here **Metaphysics** considers it. There **is** also a form abstract from matter and from the continuation of matter, and from here **Physics** considers it; and every privation is as the philosopher Aristotle says at the end of the first book of the **Physics**. **The End** limits one thing and is the best and the most desirable.

**When** he says that form gives **being** <note>original: "esse"</note> in which everything that is is sent by God, he says that it is the "best" because no limit of the beginning is desirable in itself; he says that matter itself desires it—for matter does **not** desire either form or privation, as Aristotle says in that same place. **Not** that form is seen in a single way. **That** which is form is seen as **perfection** <note>In this context, "perfection" refers to "actuality" or the state of being complete and fully realized.</note> and power. Therefore, form is like a second power; the **soul** <note>original: "anima"</note> is the perfection of an organic body existing in potential. The soul is thus also the "informing form" <note>The principle that gives a living thing its specific structure and life-functions.</note> within a mixed order, and such a definition of form is found in the *Book of Principles*: "Form is a contingent composition of simple things," and in its essence, it touches upon the simple things. He says that every form in itself is **simple** <note>Not made of smaller parts; a unified essence.</note>. **Whence** it is an unchangeable essence; if it were able to be variable or receive another form, it would be an object or a subject, and not a form.

<summary>This page defines the relationship between matter and form, explaining how different branches of philosophy (Physics and Metaphysics) categorize "forms" based on their abstraction from matter. It concludes by defining the soul as the "perfection" or "informing form" of a physical body.</summary>
<keywords>Aristotle, Physics, Metaphysics, matter, form, privation, potential, perfection, soul, anima, simple essence</keywords>

--- Page 157 ---
<meta>This page continues the technical classification of "form" (the essence or defining nature of a thing) and how it relates to matter. The previous page established that form can be abstract or natural; this page dives into the different types of forms—receptive, spiritual, and corporeal—and concludes with the historical debate between Plato, Aristotle, and Anaxagoras on the origin of these forms.</meta>

And the simple species is related to the composite arrangement. **C**ountable according to the grace in a certain thing and form, remaining as a fourfold form; and according to this specific form, a **paradigm** <term>paradigma: a perfect model or pattern from which other things are copied</term> is an exemplar. **F**orm is called "receptive" when it is received from a giver into the primary fourfold and the corporeal. The **spiritual** <note>In this context, "spiritual" refers to "incorporeal" or non-physical essences rather than religious spirits.</note> form is that which makes a thing be what it is, which is spiritual in itself, such as an essence within a body, so that it is "whole in the whole" <note>This is a classic philosophical phrase meaning the essence isn't divided among parts—your "humanity" isn't 10% in your arm and 10% in your leg; it is fully present in every part of you.</note>. **C**orporeal form is that which makes a body and which exists in a physical thing, so that it is in the parts of matter, in the **m**ajor and minor.

**A**mong unified things, there is a certain union and a certain distraction. **D**istraction <note>original: "distractio," referring here to spatial extension or being spread out</note> is that which exists in adjacent things, where one part is outside of the other. **V**nion is that which exists in non-extended things, where one part is within the other. There is a union among men, and a certain particular union. **V**nion is that which is total in the whole being, so that it is not divided in any way, such as **whiteness** <note>Whiteness is a common example of an "accident" or property that covers a surface but remains a single concept.</note>. **P**artial union is that which is spread out in parts, etc., as with the whiteness mentioned above. **A**lso, consider that which is asked: the soul is the form of the heavens, and certain forms are composite, such as the forms of the elements and things composed of elements.

**I**tem: among forms, one is static, another is changing. Stasis is that which allows a thing to be centered in itself, like fire. The soul is that which changes according to matter; it does not make the thing all at once. **F**urthermore, concerning forms, one power is in a thing, another is in the **u**nity. Note that matter has a static form. Item: that matter has a potential in certain things; it places matter in a mediating office which is called the "official instrument." These instruments, because they are distinguished by one part being here and another there—as with color, where the "office" belongs to the color and is called "official"—and the body is first white, but cannot be schooled. **W**hence the thing puts matter into matter, not mediated by the soul, because those things which do not have motion are passive, and are of another kind and homogeneous.

**N**ote that regarding the soul, there was a threefold opinion of the **philosophers** <note>original: "philosophorum"</note> concerning incorporeal forms. **S**ome indeed thought that forms exist from an external source, from a **Giver of Forms** <term>Dator formarum: a concept often attributed to Avicenna, suggesting that forms are bestowed upon matter by a higher intelligence</term>, and that the soul is made separate from matter through the composition of the body; this was the opinion of **Plato**. Others thought that forms exist within the matter itself, but are hidden and insensible to us. **A**naxagoras <note>A pre-Socratic Greek philosopher</note> used to say that all things are mixed together. **W**hence, because this is the opinion, **t**herefore this is the primary way forms exist.

**C**onsequently, in our consideration, we must look at the causes: what they are and in how many ways they exist. It should be known, however, at the end and at the beginning, that there is a threefold meditation on causes through themselves <note>original: "per se"</note> ¶ **cause** and knowledge, according to both measure and knowledge, as will be clear later. ¶ **In one way**, cause is spoken of in the second book of the *Physics* as the philosophical order from which something is made <unclear>??</unclear>, just as bronze is to a statue in matter, and as we have it subjectively; and this is the **material cause** <term>material cause: the physical "stuff" or substance out of which something is made, like the wood of a chair</term>. ¶ **Note** concerning matter: it is the matter *from which* it is said by order, and it is the matter *in which*, and the matter *about which* regarding the first object. 

In another way, cause is said to be that which gives being to the unknown, and this is the **formal cause** <term>formal cause: the design, shape, or essence that makes a thing what it is</term>. ¶ **Note** that in incorporeal beings, such as in an angel and in the soul, the form is the same as the "act of matter" and is the essential reason for the composite. ¶ **In composite** <note>physical or made of multiple parts</note> things, however, it is not the same; for in man, the soul is the act of matter, but it is also the state of the whole composite. 

¶ **In a third way**, cause is said to be the principle of <margin>x</margin> change or rest, such as the one who deliberates is the power, and as the father is the cause of the son; this is the **efficient cause** <term>efficient cause: the agent or force that brings something into being; the "trigger" for an event</term>. ¶ **And note** that the efficient cause is twofold: the order of nature and measure, and it exists immensely, and according to its own manner, which is called by the theologians the "human cause" <note>likely referring to secondary causes or human agency within a divine plan</note>. ¶ **The last** is concerning the order which is called "through itself" <note>this leads into the Final Cause, or the purpose for which a thing exists</note>.

<meta>This page continues the scholastic exploration of Aristotelian causality, moving from the definition of the four causes into the complex distinction between "fortune" (luck involving human intent) and "chance" (accidental events in nature). ...equal to the oblique, and certain things <unclear>here</unclear> through the generation of the body. That which happens [unexpectedly] is called the fortunate or the accidental.

**Cause in the first mode**

**For it is necessary that fortune and chance events occur**

**Here regarding fortune and chance, since fortune exists in those things where**

...agents such as <unclear>man</unclear>... it happens. Neither a debt, nor an infant, nor a brute animal <term>brute: a non-human animal lacking rational choice</term> is subject to fortune or chance in the same way, for these do not act by <unclear>intent</unclear>. Nor is there "fortunate" or "unfortunate" among the gods, except perhaps in a secondary sense. But even the <unclear>falling</unclear> of stones or the behavior of wood and marble can be called "chance." 

For example, it is by chance that someone comes into a house and finds safety, or perhaps he enters the house to <unclear>escape an enemy</unclear> and the house itself falls; such an order of events is made by chance.

**It is not through purpose**

It is not by purpose that such a chance order is made, just as it is not by purpose that in the middle of the month <unclear>heat</unclear> occurs, or that all weight tends toward the **center** <term>center: the center of the Earth, which Aristotelian physics viewed as the natural destination for all "heavy" elements like earth and water</term>.

**But since through**

Every instance of chance is something that does not happen by [constant] necessity. **But not that** which occurs in those things done by human <unclear>agency</unclear>; all those things are from **purpose** <term>purpose (propositum): the deliberate intention or choice of a rational being</term>. First, there is the union [of elements] in the month, just as **Aristotle** <note original: "ayles">A common medieval abbreviation for Aristoteles.</note> says in the second book of the **Physics**, and throughout the **Physics**...

...and of those things, it does not happen to occur in animals, regarding both genus and body, nor in <unclear>homogeneous</unclear> plants and...

<meta>This page continues the scholastic examination of Aristotelian physics. While the previous page focused on fortune and chance as accidental causes, this page transitions into the general classification of the four causes (material, formal, efficient, and final) and begins a foundational inquiry into the nature of motion, space, and the void as discussed in Books 1 and 2 of Aristotle's Physics.</meta>

# Regarding the Order <note original: "Olimpias">This heading, though appearing as 'Olimpias,' likely refers to a specific section or commentary series.</note>

It is stated here, according to the current order, which and how many [causes] there are. For there are [four], namely: **material**, **formal**, **efficient**, and **final**. Concerning these, Aristotle says in Book 1 of the *Physics* that every single thing [appears] in three dimensions <note original: "mensiones">Likely referring to the three measures or dimensions of physical bodies.</note>. A single thing appears as a distinct entity according to its nature: the **formal** is the end of the perfection of **matter**; the **efficient** is the cause near to the end according to nature; and the **formal** itself is the composite [of form and matter] which exists and is made as one, which is the goal of all things when the species is complete.

### Book 2

And in Book 2, he says that **Nature** is the primary principle of **motion**, **rest**, and **change**. He distinguishes well regarding this principle, rendering justice to philosophy, since he speaks of cause in terms of motion and rest, place and time. In the first mode—not that which is said concerning motion according to physical [realities]—he begins with the opinions of others.

#### Opinions on Motion

For certain [philosophers] posited that motion exists, while others posited that it does not. Those who posited that motion does not exist argued that if it did, it would be impossible for two bodies to be in the same place. **Parmenides** and **Melissus** said that motion is either entirely impossible or that it cannot exist without a **vacuum** <note>The "void" or empty space.</note>. Some speak of the **vacuum** and the **plenum** <note>A space entirely filled with matter.</note>; they argued that if a vacuum existed anywhere, it would be the same as if it were generated everywhere. If the whole universe were a plenum, nothing could be moved. For if it moved through a plenum, generation [of motion] would require bodies to be inside one another, meaning two bodies would be in the same place, which is impossible. Therefore, they held that there was only one being, immutable and immobile.

**Aristotle**, therefore, says that these arguments are not entirely "physical" <note>In the sense of belonging to the science of physics.</note> because **physics assumes motion** <note original: "physica supponit motum">Aristotle argues that a physicist does not need to prove motion exists, as it is the starting point of the science; those who deny it are outside the scope of physics.</note>. 

#### Zeno’s Paradox

Others said that motion does not exist because of the order of the finiteness of space—namely **Zeno**. They argued as if to positing motion were [impossible]. He says that if a body is to move from place to place, it is necessary that it passes through the whole space. But the whole space can be divided infinitely; namely, within all parts of space there are infinite points which cannot be crossed [in a finite time]. Therefore, there is no motion.

The **Physician** <note>Meaning Aristotle, "The Philosopher" of nature.</note> solves this according to the judgment of physicists, saying that space can be considered in two ways:
1. In regard to **act** (or actualities), and in this way, space is not infinite.
2. In regard to **potentiality**, and in this way, it is called infinite because of the [potential] infinity of its parts.

#### The Vacuum

In opposition, certain [philosophers] who posited that motion exists said that motion occurs through a **vacuum**, while others said it occurs through a **plenum**. Those who said it occurs through a vacuum followed **Democritus**.

<summary>This page outlines Aristotle's four causes and begins his defense of motion against Eleatic philosophers like Parmenides and Zeno. It specifically addresses Zeno's paradox of motion by distinguishing between actual and potential infinity in space.</summary>

<keywords>Aristotle, Physics, Four Causes, Motion, Zeno's Paradox, Vacuum, Plenum, Parmenides, Melissus, Democritus, Infinity</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the scholastic commentary on Aristotle's Physics, moving from the definition of nature in Book 2 to the specific problems of the vacuum (void) and the nature of motion discussed in Book 4. It specifically addresses the arguments of the atomists and the theological implications of motion for non-physical beings like angels.</meta>

...from a light and tiny body, because they placed their points through an invisible body, which are **atoms**. For they prove that through these, "atom" <note original: "athomū">A term derived from the Greek *atomos*, meaning 'indivisible'.</note> is so called because it is "without division" <note original: "a- thomos">The author provides the etymology: 'a-' (without) and 'tomos' (cutting/division).</note>. They also argued that it is not possible for anything to act or exist before motion except through a **vacuum** <note original: "vacuum">The void or empty space.</note>. Hence it was said that if the whole were a vacuum, it would result in mixed things appearing as double in the same place. He says that if one thing were a vacuum, nothing would be moved, for it is not possible for there to be motion through a vacuum. 

This is evident in **Book 4 of the *Physics*** <note original: "4º phisicorum">Aristotle's primary text on the impossibility of a vacuum.</note>, where it is stated that motion is understood in terms of its extent and duration. It is influenced by the thinness and thickness <note original: "subtiliato et grossitie">Referring to the density or resistance of the medium.</note> of the medium through which the motion occurs. When a body moves through a very thin medium compared to a very thick medium, it is clear that the motion is fastest [in the thinner one], and the swiftest motion is measured in the least time.

But he says it is not the case that something moving a great distance in a short time is the same as the cause of motion through a vacuum; for a vacuum would exceed a medium in "thinness" infinitely. Since a body occupies a single place, it follows that this motion would be "fastest" in a way that its time of measurement would be less than nothing—rather, this is impossible. The velocity of no motion is "the fastest" unless it is compared to a time that is shorter; therefore, it is not possible for motion to exist through a vacuum.

If a vacuum were to exist, it would be relevant to the motion and presence of **two angels**. An angel is moved from place to place, yet it is not moved through a physical medium. In that case, the velocity is said to be in a "point" <note original: "in Puncto">In this context, an 'instant' or an indivisible moment of time, rather than a spatial point.</note>, and the motion of an angel is not measured by time.

<margin>Blue paraph</margin> 

And if it is objected that in the motion of the second kind there is a "middle time" <note>A duration between the start and end of the movement.</note> by which the motion of an angel is measured, it must be said that there is a **flowing motion** and a motion of the second kind that lacks a temporal interval. This is a passive motion... I say that the temporal interval belongs to measured motion, [like] the motion of an angel. But it is said just as motion occurs through a flat surface, whereas in a vacuum, or in a "brief" [moment], it is not moved through another [medium]... This is what is said, etc.

<summary>This page analyzes Aristotle's arguments from Book 4 of the Physics regarding the impossibility of motion in a vacuum, noting that velocity depends on the density of the medium. The text then applies these physical principles to the motion of angels, concluding that angelic movement is instantaneous rather than temporal.</summary>

<meta>This page transitions from the discussion of the vacuum in Book 4 of Aristotle's *Physics* to the core definitions of motion and continuity found in Book 3. It explores the relationship between potentiality and actuality, and the mathematical-philosophical requirements for something to be considered "continuous."</meta>

...and regarding one division, it is said to be defined thus, namely by another in **Book 3 of the *Physics*** <note original: "3o phisicor">Aristotle's Physics, Book III, which contains the classic definition of motion.</note>. <gloss>Act</gloss> <note original: "Actus">Also translated as "actuality" or "fulfillment."</note> is, to be specific, the [act] of that which exists in **potentiality** <note original: "p^o / potentia">The capacity or possibility for a certain state or action.</note> in an ordered way; hence it is as an actuating thing toward an actual state, another being made healthy—at the same point, it is toward the end. <gloss>Also</gloss>, it then suffices for motion to exist. <gloss>Motion</gloss> is the act of that which is in potentiality toward act, continuous in all things. <gloss>Also</gloss>, motion is defined thus: motion is the **first perfection** <note original: "p^ma p^fecio">Scholastic term for the first stage of realization.</note> of that which is in potentiality. <gloss>Which</gloss> definition is made manifest, showing that everything in potentiality, as it is argued, is not yet moved toward that potential. Everything in potentiality is clearly not beneath [the act], and such is motion: for that which is in potentiality possesses one potential to be moved and another potential for that which is acquired through motion; and thus it is clear how motion is the "first perfection" of what follows. <gloss>Act</gloss> is indeed motion, the act existing toward its end; for the potential remains until those things which are acquired through motion [are reached], which is the perfection of the existing state, and one is perceived. <gloss>Note</gloss> that it is not measured except by **time**, which we touch upon as an accident <note original: "passione">In this context, a property or 'accident' rather than suffering.</note> following from time or the continuous; and motion is continuous. <gloss>The continuous</gloss> <note original: "Continuu">A sequence where the parts share a common boundary.</note> is prior to a common limit <note original: "t’m coem / terminum communem">The point where two parts of a line or motion meet.</note> joining the parts of the motion, and it has a middle; from this, it follows that whatever is moved was previously being moved and will be moved [hereafter]. For there is a part in the starting point, and a part in the goal, and a part in the middle; for such motion is nothing but the joining of past and future parts. <gloss>Note</gloss> that we speak of the **continuous**, the **contiguous**, and the **consequent**. We call all those things "continuous" which are joined at one [limit], as a point is to a line, and a part which makes a line, and a part in comparison to a surface. <gloss>Contiguous</gloss> things, however, are those whose extremes are together and are not distant from one another, such as a pen and parchment. <gloss>Consequent</gloss> things <note original: "Conseguencia">Referring to things in succession.</note> are said to be those that do not have [their limits] in the same place in their kind, but nothing prevents there being a middle [between them]. <gloss>Also</gloss>, note that for a **single motion** <note original: "vnu motū">A unified, individual instance of movement.</note>, as required by the dialecticians and the 1st and 3rd [books] of the *Physics*, it is said that for motion to be one, the object that is moved must be one, and the goal toward which it is moved must be one, and the time must remain one and continuous during the motion, such that it is not interrupted by **rest**, as is said later in the same [book]; after many things, it is stated that a motion is not unified [if interrupted].

<summary>This page defines motion using Aristotelian concepts of potentiality and actuality (perfection) and explores the nature of "the continuous." It distinguishes between things that are continuous (sharing a boundary), contiguous (touching), and consequent (following in order).</summary>
<keywords>motion, act, potentiality, Aristotle, Physics, continuity, time, perfection, contiguous, succession</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of Aristotelian physics, specifically transitioning from the definition of motion as the fulfillment of potential (from Book 3) to the classification of different types of motion and the mechanics of local movement.</meta>

...of which all is the medium that, for this rectilinear motion <note>Movement in a straight line, which Aristotle contrasted with the circular motion of the celestial spheres.</note> of the universe, is neither one nor continuous; and this is clear to recognize when there is one deprivation <note original: "prua / privatio"> and one principle <note original: "pria / principia">. **It is said** that in two things a division occurs while a part of itself strikes against another realm, and thus it must be formed in something beyond; and rather now I say that it is formed <unclear>ecataliat</unclear>, for not that which pertains to motion by itself and to potentiality by itself in the final way. **It is given** that it is moved toward a thing in one way or form in another, and in this way by motion where also that which is moved naturally by us and all things that are in us. **In another** way it is moved toward [potentiality]... toward [actuality]... toward [position] in the whole, in which it is given it is moved by a natural motion. **To** be seen now is the division of motions, as there are two ways: there are two types of motions, one being natural and the other violent.

<margin>principle of motion</margin>
Natural motion <note original: "p natalis"> is that whose principle <unclear>m r</unclear> is within. **Nature** is that whose principle of motion is [internal]. **Violent** [motion] is that whose principle is outside, while the thing undergoing it remains a consistent whole. **And** regarding all these things, it is based on what Aristotle says in the 1st [book] of the *Physics* <note original: "Arilo in 1' phisicoz">The text cites Book 1, though the famous principle "everything that is moved is moved by another" is more central to Books 7 and 8 of the Physics.</note>: that everything which is moved is moved either by itself or by another. **And** note that natural motion is relaxed at the beginning but intense at the end <note>The author is describing acceleration; Aristotle believed objects speeding up as they reached their "natural place."</note>. **In another**, however, there is relaxation at the end and at the beginning, but it is intense in the middle. In **Violent** [motion], however, there is intensity at the beginning and relaxation at the end <note>Like an arrow slowing down after leaving the bow.</note>. Violent motion... [Aristotle] in the *Physics* divides it into types: namely pushing, pulling, and carrying. **Pushing** <term>Pulsio: the act of applying force away from the mover</term> includes both impulse and expulsion. **Impulse** is when the mover does not leave the object being moved; **Expulsion** is when the expeller leaves the object, as when it is thrown. **And** in the other, the motion is faster when pulling [and] does not separate from that which is pulled; and to this category they lead [the use of] the <term>instyas</term> and <term>castryas</term> <note>These appear to be specialized tools, perhaps related to weaving or wool-working.</note>, spatulas, and the comb <note original: "pectida">, all of which seem [to follow this rule]. **Carrying** <note original: "Vectio"> is said when [the mover] moves it [without independent motion]; and this happens in motions arising from pushing, pulling, and dragging. **Dragging** <note original: "Trigo / Tractigo"> is a motion composed of pulling and pushing. **In another** way it is said... concerning motion, there are six types: namely **generation**, **corruption** <note>Coming into being and passing away.</note>, **augmentation**, **diminution** <note>Growth and shrinking.</note>, **alteration** <note>Change in quality, such as color.</note>, and **change of place**; there are six in all.

<summary>This page classifies motion into natural and violent categories, explaining how speed changes over time in each. It further breaks down local motion into mechanical types like pushing, pulling, and carrying, concluding with the standard six types of Aristotelian change.</summary>

**Likewise**, it is said through division and a restrictive division <note original: "diuisionem d[er]ogatiuum"> and through its own division, which he himself gathers, that in substance there are two types: **generation** <term>generation: the coming-into-being of a new substance</term> and **corruption** <term>corruption: the passing-away or destruction of a substance</term>, and not before motion. But Aristotle mentions diverse **mutations** in the 5th book of the *Physics*, and these two are mutations; specifically, some are by motion and some are not motion.

For one is from subject to subject, from being to being; and again, **generation** is from non-being to being, while **corruption** is from being to non-being.

¶ One type of motion exists only in an existing and fixed thing and in time.
¶ Another type, truly <unclear>er</unclear> corporeal, is not in a fixed thing or in time.
¶ In these, truly, **generation** and **corruption** are not "motions," because they are mutations <note original: "mutacione[m]">.

<meta>This page transitions from the general nature of motion and change into a detailed scholastic categorization of "Place" (Locus) and "Time" (Tempus). It relies on the Aristotelian tradition—specifically the Physics—and the theological synthesis of John of Damascus to explain how different types of existence (physical bodies, spiritual angels, and the omnipresent God) relate to space and the passage of time.</meta>

...is the definition. ¶ In one way, a thing is said to be in a **place** through the boundary <term>circumscription: the physical limits or "outline" of a body</term> of its definition and the filling of that place according to its own nature, and to be in a place where a beginning, a middle, and an end are assigned to it, or whose parts are likewise in that place; and in this way, a **body** is in a place. ¶ **The second definition** of being in a place is that which does not have these physical properties; thus all **angels** are in a place, for an angel is "there" where it acts <note original: "angelus in ibi est ubi operatur">. <note>Scholastic philosophy taught that spiritual beings do not occupy space by volume, but are "present" wherever they exert their power or influence.</note> ¶ **Third**, in a certain respect accidentally, and it is said to go out from that which is said to be in a body through itself in some place of which there is a total **filling**. ¶ To be in a place such that it is through its whole self, and this is said of **God**, who is in every place and fills every place <note original: "replet omnem locum">. ¶ **Likewise**, since it is not entirely in a place by itself nor by passing away, and since it is seen through the act of those things which are in these. ¶ The **fourth** way is that which is truly a place and its location. Place is defined according to one source, [John of] Damascus, who says it is the immobile boundary of the containing body <note original: "terminus continentis immobilem">. <margin>8</margin> And then, according to Damascus, it is called the **place where**, such as being in a house, which is a **place where**. And the boundary of the body is likewise a measurement, neither more nor less than the place that is commanded and through the end which exists.

## Likewise concerning **time**

¶ Likewise concerning **time** and the discourse to be led regarding it: what it is and what it truly means to be in time. ¶ **In one way**, concerning time itself. ¶ **Two**, concerning the distinction of time. ¶ **Time** is thus defined by Aristotle in the 4th book of the *Physics*: **time is the number of motion according to the before and after** <note original: "tempus est numerus motus secundum prius et posterius">. <note>This is Aristotle's classic definition, suggesting that time is how we count or measure the sequence of change.</note> ¶ Likewise, concerning time, it exists just as motion does in itself, and through those things let the definitions be understood clearly from the comparison of time to local motion <term>local motion: change of place or position</term>, through and according to which time is an attribute. ¶ **Likewise**, the reason is that it has magnitude through another and through the "after," and hence those parts are said to be according to another, and the middle is for the motion; and because motion is through that magnitude, it is something divisible and is in potentiality <term>potentiality: the capacity to become or be divided, even if not yet realized</term> toward a primary "where," and places, and time. And if it is through the divisible, it is according to the parts of magnitude and the "where" and the places and time; and then let that very thing be its divisibility, and let there be a distinction and some measure of it through its passing; there will already be some measure, from which it is called a measure; it is the measure by which there is one and the same being under one in comparison to....

<meta>This page continues the discussion of Aristotelian physics, specifically moving from the definition of "Place" on the previous page to the relationship between "Magnitude" (spatial extension), "Motion," and "Time." ...through magnitude in respect to the **Soul** <term>Anima: the mind or animating principle that perceives and counts units of time</term> from which, and in respect to the **measures** from which there is continuous motion. <insert>Hence, insofar as</insert> motion is a medium, it measures magnitude; and the measure of that motion itself is a measure in its primary exercise within the soul, which continuously holds [it] in motion. The third <insert>reason</insert> comes from the power itself, continuously provided in the soul.

<insert>Hence</insert> then the multitude is one measure <insert>which</insert> is in continuous motion, and thus it is clear that it is one. But **time**, since time is something else, is a multitude of flux; under the suspicion of being continuous, time is nothing, except for the "now" <note original: "praesentis">, that is, the present, which is not a [permanent] being because it is in flux and does not remain. <insert>Hence, here it must be known</insert> that concerning time—<insert>namely, every measure</insert>—and the multitude changed by the **Soul**, one <insert>is the multitude</insert> in the past, <insert>where</insert> we can [perceive] no multitude, <insert>namely</insert> like a ten or an eight and such things, since the multitude <insert>Likewise</insert> is lacking, and eight are lacking, and such things. **Number** is named from multitude in the thing itself, and then... and from the lack of multitude. <insert>Number from multitude</insert> is truly said according to the **Soul** and according to its own <insert>Number</insert>. <meta>This page continues the physical and metaphysical analysis of time, shifting from the continuity of magnitude and the soul’s perception to the formal definition of time as a "measure." It explores the Aristotelian concept of time as the "number of motion" and introduces the distinction between a measure that counts (mensurans) and the thing being counted (mensurata).</meta>

...[the soul] possesses in motion such a <term>measured measure</term> <note>original: mensura mensurata; the thing that has been marked out or quantified</note> by two steps. And earlier, because all things which are made through it—**accordingly** as time is measured—are all things "before" and "after" in time, and from the "before" and from a single <term>measured thing</term> according to its being. In the same way, while the order remains, the object is said to be <term>measured</term>; and it is called one from the object alone, though in another way it is called one because it is time as a measure. But it is so called because motion is so called, and thus time is the <term>measuring measure</term> <note>original: mensura mensurans; the active standard or "yardstick" used to count</note> of motion.

For having considered earlier how much time there is, from the manner of all things that are able—by reason of which and by reason of its <unclear>relation</unclear>—it signifies the quantity of motion. And thus it is as **Aristotle** <note>Aristotle (384–322 BC), whose *Physics* Book IV defines time as the "number of motion in respect of the before and after."</note> says: that time is the **number** <note>original: numerus; here meaning a "count" or "ordered sequence" rather than just a digit</note> of motion. For from motion there are two parts [perceived] by the mind; two [separate things] by themselves measure nothing.

**Number**, however, is called a <term>measuring number</term>; it is like the number two. Time measures motion and is measured by it, in the same way that ten soldiers, when taken, explain the parts of an army as distinct types through smaller parts. Time does not multiply the **number**; it is only called a measure of two things: the "before" and "after" in that <term>measured thing</term>. It determines the parts of the motion itself according to **number**, in the whole extent of the motion, and thus it is clear how time is the measure of motion.

But having been measured **according to** the "before" and "after," it is also clear in this that motion cannot be the measure of time. Through this, it is clearly stated in **Aristotle’s** book regarding the cause of time: that it is not the cause of being moved, but it is the efficient cause. **However**, in motion, the thing is not the same insofar as it remains in the object but does not remain in the same state of being, because that which is a part of motion is not permanent.

However, what "duration" is, is permanent in the part, the same in the object, and it is in the place of the thing endured by reason of motion. Thus it is clear what time is: it is an attribute <term>passio: a property or phenomenon arising from a subject</term> of motion, and [it is clear] how it measures motion. 

Thirdly, it should be noted that [the term] is understood in two ways: it is spoken of as the "object of time" (which has been discussed), and in the manner mentioned, and that **measure** by itself is said [to relate] to the earlier height. For let us suppose, though it is impossible, a flowing river moving along a line, and that the point of that river continuously created a successive line; a line which is not permanent in its parts, and in which there will be nothing except the "state" of the point which is said to exist. Even if it is different, since what is "before" is here in length, and "after" is with that point; we have shown this.

<meta>This page continues the analysis of time and motion, specifically focusing on the "instant" (the "now") as the boundary or limit of time. ...motion and by reason of the difference of time, and here it is not accepted. And it is that time [exists] in the mind. From length, there is first <note>original: prima</note> [magnitude], and in that to have a distinction; then it is said that it is the **limit** <term>terminus</term> <note>A boundary or end-point that defines where one thing stops and another begins.</note> of time, and no further is it said [to be so]. And in comparison to motion, it is as was said of motion. **And** if the genus would want to distinguish according to nature—that just as that which is the number which is the object of time, and is said through the "prior" [part], which does not stand [still], nor [does it exist] between that which is time and the reason of the part—time limits the name of the "now" itself.

**And** note that first it is said to "stand," and now [relative] to the great time in which [things] are said to have a limit; and in both cases, it is called a distinction. **It should be noted**, and yet considered, that there are many things which behave as though they are not limits at all in distance—not because it possesses that which it did not have previously, nor because it is made on the ground <note>original: humum; perhaps referring to a physical mark or track</note>, neither flowing nor non-flowing—but in the manner of a "turn" <note>original: flexu; likely referring to the turning point in a circular motion or a change in direction</note> in its [turning], it makes time. And now it is, and in the "before" it was not.

The object of the mind is said to be [that] in which, on account of the <term>instant</term> <note>original: instans; the "now," which Aristotle defines as the link between the past and the future.</note> and the "now," [it is] continuing in a certain way. And yet, to have that which now is, by reason of time, time is called a limit; because what was "before" is not "now." By reason or manner of time—for the reason that it does not exist except through time according to its own nature and according to the nature of time—which, as it is not said to have every operation toward some motion through a limit; it is continued [as] a "before" and an "end." And therefore, because it is continued by a **point** <note>original: puncto; just as a point joins two halves of a line, the "now" joins two halves of time.</note>, it uses the same point twice. 

And therefore, because now it is further according to the line of motion, but it is held above that which now is; by reason of the point, it is continued. It uses itself twice toward the end, toward the preceding <term>magnitude</term> <note>original: magnitudo; the physical size or extent of the motion being measured.</note>, and it does not come by it into motion—which is motion—but in motion it is not in the end; otherwise, in magnitude, it is the limit in the aforementioned time. **Whence** also by reason of the limit itself in reason; but then it is the limit which is called "after," which is of the manner of magnitude. 

**And** according to whichever prior [part] of one point, it is said of it in the mind; in the mind, the point is said twice, which is at the same time with it in the <term>instant</term> in the mind. And now, in its certain way, it is the limit now, which is simultaneously in that which is called a point concerning the end and now in that which comes to the same point, as was said before. In time, in the mind is time, and that which is the <term>instant</term> is the instant *per se*.

<meta>This page continues the scholastic investigation into the nature of time and motion, specifically moving from the definition of the "now" as a boundary to a categorized analysis of how things exist within time, citing Aristotle’s Physics Book 4.</meta>

In the fourth [book] of the **Physics**, <note>original: "in 4to phisicorum"; referring to Aristotle's *Physics*, Book IV, which contains his famous treatise on time.</note> [it is stated] that in a single motion, a point is joined to two <unclear>limits</unclear> [at the] <unclear>end</unclear>. There is therefore a difficulty regarding the beginning [and] the continuation, since it is on a line where the divisions [occur] at points and [at] the end, whence it follows that there is nothing in between them <unclear>that</unclear> [separates] the beginning and the end. And thus the point <unclear>is</unclear> [conceived as the] <unclear>boundary</unclear> <unclear>of one</unclear> <unclear>line</unclear>, and thus the "now" is not [a part] in time, and the <unclear>now</unclear> is indivisible.

The <term>reason for indivisibility</term> <note>The argument for why an instant cannot be divided into smaller parts.</note> is said in one way to be that which exists through itself; and because of <unclear>this</unclear>, something can be moved <unclear>in time</unclear> <unclear>through</unclear> <unclear>itself</unclear>, <unclear>as in</unclear> an inquiry, and then it is in time through another. And in one way, that time is therefore the "now," as in the flow of time itself.

It remains [to be asked], however, what it is **to be in time**. And for this, it must be known that something is said to be "in time" in four ways:

1.  In the **first way**, something is in time as if in motion and in the <term>passions of time</term> <note>original: "passionibus temporis"; in scholastic philosophy, "passions" are the properties or attributes that a subject undergoes or possesses.</note>, just as [one is] in time through itself—that is, according to the long and the short, the much and the few.
2.  In the **second way**, something is in time like that which is in a single object; for instance, an art now exists in time, which is the object of [that art] itself.
3.  In the **third way**, it is in time as [perceived by] the intellect of the soul, and thus the "now" is said to be in time.
4.  In the **fourth way**, every [thing] is in time just as [it is] in the world, and thus there is time, and through time, measure.

Now, therefore, it happens in two ways: namely, through itself and through position. Through itself, motion is in time, and [maintains] the <unclear>measured</unclear> order that is in motion. Through position, therefore, [is] the <unclear>genus</unclear> and the <unclear>generation</unclear> <unclear>likewise</unclear> <unclear>which</unclear> is referred to motion. And "to be in time through itself" [happens] <unclear>still</unclear> in two ways: namely, being measured by the whole time, and by a part of a single time. This is because motion is spoken of in the whole time, whereas in a part of time, motion is <unclear>standing</unclear> <note>original: "stans"; likely referring to the state of rest or the instantaneous state within a larger movement.</note>.

In comparison, indeed, "to be in time" is said by way of <unclear>attainment</unclear>, that is, to be calculated, and for there to be an immobile order, and [the capacity] to be, to be able, and to act. It is to be calculated toward an immutable and mobile order within the order containing [it] in time and afterward; and to be a containment—and this is specifically what it is.

Next, it is [necessary] <unclear>to know</unclear> what the end and the beginning are. And because one thing is called an "end," it is and always is this: that which calculates the order.

<meta>This page continues the scholastic inquiry into the nature of time and motion, transitioning from the mathematical definition of the "now" to the metaphysical application of time to different types of existence—specifically spiritual (angelic) versus corporeal (physical) beings—drawing on the Aristotelian tradition.</meta>

To be [of a certain sort] is its quiddity <note>original: "quidus" likely "quidditas"; the "what-ness" or essence of a thing.</note> and to have power beforehand is to be in the state of actuated motion; [motion] is the movable <unclear>outside</unclear> <unclear>infinity</unclear> and that which exists in it.

And I say that an angel is said to be in time according to all degrees of time, insofar as there must be a transition through the angel by many acts of willing, just as the act of willing exists; this is in respect to <term>deformed cognition</term> <note>original: "cognitionem deformatam"; in this context, it refers to cognition that is shaped or limited by specific objects of thought rather than being universal.</note>. And through this, it is said that God measures the spiritual creature by time. 

The corporeal creature, however, is measured through time; and for that reason it is said that it is <unclear>another</unclear> thing which is [existence] <unclear>insofar</unclear> as the whole is that which, according to <unclear>this</unclear>, pertains to continuous motion—to that motion of the angel. And this happens through a middle order in which two modes are posited. Since even in [potentiality]... there then is what pertains to this and to the form, and we are "now," or "now" together as time. 

All changeable and corruptible things are in time. The <unclear>measure</unclear> of the "now" in this is always the **full future and the past** <note>original: "plenum futurum et praeteritum"; suggesting the "now" contains the weight of what was and what will be.</note>; for that which ends and is not full is [the state of being] finished, for it is like a continuation to the instant.

Again, it is not so that it exists through a finite and "full now," but it is [a property] of the finite through the "full now"; this is another thing; it <unclear>prevails</unclear> through transitory things in the same way. It is only that which is a <unclear>little</unclear> [distance] from the "full now."

**Suddenly** <note>original: "Repente"; usually referring to an unexpected event in sensible time.</note> is that which, in sensible time, is [before] <unclear>death</unclear>. **Instantly** <note>original: "Subito"; often used in scholasticism to describe change that occurs without a middle duration.</note> is [attributed] to the <unclear>virtually</unclear> blessed. The transition in established theology is to go forward and not [as an addition] to the manifest.

In which way they receive time maturely. I say it is the [space] of their time which, by the addition of motion... according to what they intercede in that; I say it does not have [this] above in the [way], and it is not in corporeal forms but in spiritual forms.

<meta>This page continues the scholastic inquiry into the relationship between God, eternity, and time. While the previous page focused on the motion of angels and the definition of the "now," this section moves into how God’s divinity "touches" or measures created things and the distinction between the invariability of God and the relative stability of blessed souls and angels.</meta>

In the measure of the possessor, by him to whom the measure belongs, touching [the thing measured] through himself because it is his own attribute <note>original: "passio"; in scholastic philosophy, an attribute or property belonging to a subject by its nature.</note>. Truly, it is said here that measurable divinity touches the creature of old. Hence, they shall not be called [ends] through themselves nor ends, because that which was of old is nothing else from eternity except through its own property.

And thus it is said that **God**, who exists through Himself, even in relation to those things that exist in eternity; in the first sense, He is not called "blessed," and His blessedness is not yet [viewed as] His own in the way it is here, but absolutely in whatever manner is His. Nevertheless, here through application, He is called both divine truth and blessed.

**Note** that it is said regarding the difference <note>likely an abbreviation for "differentiam."</note> of time that it is not entirely something else from itself for its own sake, as an end in itself; and even if it were not placed there, it is still entirely through itself and by itself, as if not distant.

Sixthly, it is said regarding the difference that it is not simple in its order. And not in those things whose measure is a process, except for that which is called simple.

And **note** that, secondly, this is said of all things "through themselves" <term>per se</term> <note>meaning existing by its own nature or essentially.</note>, later concerning the **Creator** and concerning created things.

And in every condition, divinity itself is spoken of as both simple and invariable; whereas the "mutable" is not only spoken of according to the end of every creature, and thus only to those things which are held together by it. For He alone is not only invariable, but [the creature] is mutable.

Among creatures, insofar as they provide for a single independent way, such are the **angels** and **blessed souls**; and [freed] from the body after the resurrection, they are prepared through mutability alone by their essence—ends which are not equal. On account of this, angels and thus the blessed are then said to exist after Him, both in eternity and thus in the middle of the world, in the **Author** through invariability.

Yet truly, those who prepare in this manner, and mutability according to the mutability of ends by essence, thus they then say <unclear>only</unclear> that it is in the **Author**, not that they do not prepare for invariability alone.

Invariability is spoken of through essence, a condition which is the fullness <note>original: "amplitudo"; the breadth or abundance of perfection.</note> of all merits in the world and in created things.

Just as it is now said that measure differs from the soul to the **God of all things**, and from <unclear>nature</unclear> through the true mode—namely, of the world—possible things provided from eternity are neither like these things here, nor are they [strictly] in eternity.

To each thing which is eternal in the world and in the **Author** Himself, certain things do not exist, and [the same is true] of those which do exist.
