<meta>This page continues the scholastic investigation into the nature of eternity versus time. It builds on the previous discussion of how God's measure "touches" the creature by explaining how things participate in eternity and using the geometric analogy of a point on a line to distinguish the "moving now" of time from the "standing now" of eternity.</meta>

...and in eternity. For everything that exists through **participation** <term>per participationem</term> <note>A central concept in Thomistic and Scholastic philosophy where created things possess a quality (like existence or goodness) only by "sharing" in the essence of God, who is that quality itself.</note>, even if it is not in eternity by its own nature, is nevertheless enclosed by eternity as its measure, even while existing in time. 

Two logical points cannot stand: first, that something would arise in eternity through participation without also being immutable; second, that the **One** would remain in the immutable without that which follows.

Secondly, we must consider the **"now" of continuation** <note>original: "nunc continuationis"; the concept of a "now" that bridges or connects moments.</note>. From this stands the place of the "now," which, regarding continuation, is a "now" that stands still and does not move. However, through a certain unity, it becomes a "now" that is better understood by comparing it to this image of the "now" of time:

Imagine points **a**, **b**, and **c**. Let there be a point that moves along its line from **a** to **b**. I distinguish the sequence of **a**, **b**, and **c**. The first part is defined by its priority and the latter by its posteriority. Here, let us understand the whole of time through the line **a-b**. The **"now" of time** <note>original: "nunc temporis"</note> is understood as that which moves continuously from **a** to **b**. Because of this movement, there are two aspects: the present, which is finite, and another which is always moving in this time **b**, leaving behind the posterior and adding to the sequence. From one "now," it always stands at a boundary.

If the point were to stand still on the line and not move, there would be neither a "before" nor an "after" on that line. In the same way, if the **"now" of time** stood still in one moment, it would not be limited, nor would there be a past or a future. The cause of this is that in the image of a "now" that stands still—which I say exists in that continuation—there is no past or future; it is seen as a perpetual present, and shall remain so. 

Thus, what is in eternity is not in time. Therefore, that which is in eternity exists only in a single **indivisible** moment. Furthermore, you now see that this is true of all things, even those that are changeable. 

Also, the "now" of continuation is indivisible in its addition; it extends through all time, encompassing the whole and the infinite reach of time. Likewise, it captures the whole of time both in a state of rest with any given part and through itself. 

We already know it is a useful comparison to say that the **soul** is in the whole body, and yet it is also entirely in every single part. In the same way, even if something is simple <note>In this context, "simple" means not composed of parts, like a spirit or a geometric point.</note>, it will be the whole; and just as the soul extends through the whole body, so does this principle. To this end, there is something changeable which is joined to time in a single continuum, which is indivisible...

<summary>The author explains eternity as a "standing now" (nunc stans) using a geometric analogy of a point on a line to distinguish it from the "moving now" of time. He compares the presence of eternity across all time to the way a soul is present in every part of a body.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the scholastic investigation into the nature of eternity versus time. It builds on the previous discussion of how God's measure "touches" the creature by explaining how things participate in eternity and using the geometric analogy of a point on a line to distinguish the "moving now" of time from the "standing now" of eternity.</meta>

...of definitions, and that for a short space it holds sway over subsequent points.

To understand this, it is as follows: **Augustine** says that in that same thing which is time, in the **most present alone** <note>original: "praesentissimo solo"</note> remaining and occurring, it suddenly illuminates our whole consciousness.

...it happens often that in one moment of time there is a "before" and an "after" extending into infinity, because here and there, is that not the same thing?

The "prior" of the more prior—that is, the longest part of the day—is that which is through the world; and the "prior" is more remote than that which is more within the world. Thus, it is called an infinity of months.

...it says in book 17: when it is said that there is no sense, but it is in a world, we distinguish the past, the determined, and the future.

Regarding two common volumes by which it is proved: because that which is negated cannot be maintained, two **contradictory opposites** <term>contradictorie opposita</term> <note>In logic, these are two statements where if one is true, the other must be false, such as "it is raining" and "it is not raining."</note> cannot exist in the same moment of time. It is impossible for any two contradictory opposites to be true at once; for example, that the whole returns and also does not exist. Because in one world, the **aeviternity** <term>aevum</term> <note>A state of existence between time and eternity, specifically the type of "time" experienced by angels and celestial bodies. Unlike time, it does not change; unlike eternity, it has a beginning.</note> is expanded infinitely more than the moment of time. Therefore, as is said in our treatments, two contradictory conditions cannot exist together, such as a river being both present and not present at the same moment of its passage, according to the truth of the world which exists in every part of time.

### Secondly, note what is called the definition of the "Aeviternity."

**Aeviternity** is a measure having a beginning but lacking an end.

And note that it is commonly said that just as time exists in two ways—namely, in the **motion of the first moved** <term>motu primi mobilis</term> <note>In the medieval worldview, this refers to the outermost sphere of the heavens which rotates daily, serving as the universal standard for measuring all other motions and time itself.</note>, which measures all inferior motions as the smallest and most ample of its kind, and consequently is the measure of the motion of the first moved—so also aeviternity exists in two ways as the most ample condition by which all other conditions are measured.

Others say that it is a certain condition of all things, since no single condition is the cause of others, just as the motion of the first moved is the cause of all other motions.

From the above, it can be gathered that an **angel**, regarding its cause, exists in **aeviternity**. However, regarding its superior acting—such as knowing by **evening knowledge** <term>cognitio vespertina</term> <note>A term from St. Augustine, used by Scholastics to describe an angel's knowledge of created things in their own nature, which is considered "darker" than their "morning knowledge" (cognitio matutina) of things as they exist in God.</note>—it is in time.

But regarding the act of **contemplation**, the angel exists in eternity.

So much for the first part; let these points suffice.

<meta>This page transitions from the abstract discussion of time and eternity on the previous page into the realm of natural philosophy. Having established how the "now" relates to the eternal, the author now begins a specific study of the physical universe, specifically the celestial bodies, following the curriculum of Aristotle’s "Physics" and "On the Heavens."</meta>

<insert>Jesus Christ</insert>

**B**ecause we have spoken concerning the **mobile body** <term>corpus mobile</term> <note>A technical term in Aristotelian physics referring to any physical object capable of change or motion.</note> in its own right and in general, it now remains for us to consider the mobile body in its specific form. First, we consider that which is **ungenerable and incorruptible**, which is the **heaven** <note>original: "celum"; in this context, it refers to the celestial spheres or the firmament, which medieval scholars believed were made of a fifth element (aether) that never decayed.</note>. As Aristotle says at the beginning of his *Physics*, it is necessary to proceed from generalities to particulars. In the beginning, all men called the gods "fathers," and later [they named] the wilder forces.

The division of these topics follows. The first point to be shown is that there is **one world and not many**. Secondly, what the essence of the heaven is. Thirdly, that all bodies are contained within the heaven and none exist outside of it. Fourthly, we must demonstrate its nobility and perpetuity, and finally, its motion.

To prove that there is only one physical world and not many, we proceed by logic: Let us suppose there are two worlds. Either that other world is of the same species as this world in its parts, or it is not—at least in name. If it is the same in its parts, then by this reasoning, because that earth is like our earth, and that water is like our water, and that fire is like our fire, and so on; and because things of the same nature tend toward the same place—therefore, that water and our water tend toward the same place, and that earth and our earth do the same. If they tend toward the same place together, they are parts of the same world. Therefore, there is only one physical and universal world. It might be said that the other world is entirely unmoved in name, but then it would not be a world of "things," because a thing of this world is not that perfect or physical world, which is the measure of every operation and which contains all substance and reason.

<u>**Here begins the argument that the full heaven is finite**</u>; first, not as Aristotle says in the third book of the *Physics*.

**Infinity** is understood in many ways, but primarily in two. First, the infinite is that which is **impossible to cross** because it is not of a nature that *can* be crossed—just as a voice is "invisible." In this sense, it is called infinite by negation, because it <u>does not have a limit defined by nature</u>, and is so named accordingly.

In another way, the infinite is spoken of regarding a crossing of its measure; though it is of a nature that can be crossed, the crossing never finds an exit. Because of its present opacity, it never reaches an end. In this sense, it is called infinite by **privation** <note>The "privative infinite" refers to something that should have a limit based on its type (like a line), but happens to go on forever.</note> and...

***

<meta>This page continues the transition from general principles of physics to the specific study of the cosmos. The previous page established the framework for examining the "heaven" (the celestial spheres) and concluded with the intent to prove there is only one world. Here, the author shifts to the mathematical and physical nature of the infinite, arguing that the universe must be finite and spherical in shape.</meta>

Something is said to be "infinite in act" <note>original: "infinitum actu"; a term meaning actually existing as an infinite thing, rather than just being potentially divisible forever.</note> in a threefold way:
<margin>First way</margin> That which is finite by addition is spoken of in terms of discrete quantity <note>Discrete quantity refers to things that can be counted, like individual numbers.</note>.
<margin>Second way</margin> That which is infinite by division is spoken of in terms of continuous quantity <note>Continuous quantity refers to things that can be measured but are not naturally broken into parts, like a line or a flow of time.</note>.
<margin>Third way</margin> Every way that is infinite, or in the manner of time; and in these three
ways it is said that nothing is infinite in act, but only in potential.
According to this, the infinite is defined in the same way as that
which is infinite is that whose quantity, being taken, there is always something else to take beyond it.

## **It remains to prove that the heaven is not infinite**

First, it is said generally that no physical body is infinite,
because every body has a surface, and every surface is a boundary;
therefore every body is bounded, and everything bounded is finite.
Therefore every body is finite; from this it is clearly manifest that the entire
heaven is a body and consequently is finite. **Likewise, he specifically proves**
**now that the heaven is finite.** It must be considered what was
posited below: that every body is in a place, and nothing exists outside of it.
Among all bodies, the heaven is the most noble; it is incorruptible, and being
incorruptible, it is worthy of a greater essence of its soul. **For every**
agent is more noble than the patient <note>In medieval philosophy, the "agent" is the thing that acts, while the "patient" is the thing being acted upon.</note>, as the <term>Philosopher</term> <note>Aristotle (384–322 BCE), who was so central to medieval education he was often simply called "The Philosopher."</note> says in the third book of
*On the Soul*; to the more noble, a more noble thing is due, and to the heaven is due the more noble
form. The spherical form is more noble than all other forms;
for among the principal figures, through all capacity and
mediacy, nothing is contained in this unless it be finite. Therefore,
the spherical form is due to the heaven because the heaven is one and
most perfect. **Likewise, we see** that which is a simple line
which is equal. However, that figure is only found in an equal equality
which exists only in a round body, because the Moon is formed to fall
perpetually, and the Sun and all other super-celestial bodies are round;
therefore the heaven is round. **It should be noted, however, that** just as one
is unique, it is said in two ways to be one among all other figures, just as
a flat circle is contained by a single line. **In one way**, as regarding
angles, they are contained by many lines, such as triangles and
quadrangles; the circle relates to all other surface figures
in the same way the spherical form itself relates to all other figures
among physical bodies; in a sphere, there is one [surface].

<summary>The author argues that the universe (the heaven) cannot be infinite because all physical bodies must have surfaces and boundaries. He concludes that the heaven must be spherical, as the sphere is the most perfect and "noble" geometric shape, mirroring the nature of the celestial bodies like the Sun and Moon.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the logical demonstration that the "heaven" (the universe or celestial spheres) must be finite. Having established that bodies in general are bounded by surfaces, the author now provides specific geometric and physical proofs, referencing Aristotle’s Physics to show that an infinite body cannot move in a finite time.</meta>

and all containing things and other physical bodily figures are contained by discrete quantities <note>original: "discretis"; things that can be counted as distinct units</note>. <insert>From</insert> the aforementioned things it is proved that the heaven is finite and in what way it is finite. For by a center is understood a point from which all lines drawn to the circumference are equal <note>A classic geometric definition of a sphere, where the center is equidistant from every point on the surface.</note>; therefore, the heaven has a center, and every center has a radius equal to it, through which it is shown that the heaven is finite. And because it has all things set within itself, it is established that everything finite which is within it is indeed finite.

<insert>Likewise</insert>, it is proved that the heaven is finite by comparison to its motion and the measure of that motion, which is time. <insert>Everything</insert> indeed that is moved entirely in a fixed time is finite; and the heaven is moved entirely in a fixed time (namely, in a natural day <note>A "natural day" refers to a full 24-hour rotation of the celestial sphere.</note>); therefore, it is proven that the heaven is finite. 

<insert>Again</insert>, because an infinite motion cannot be measured by a finite time, as is demonstrated in the sixth book of the *Physics* <note>Referring to Aristotle’s *Physica*, a foundational text for medieval science.</note>. Let the distance a-b-c be divided into quarters, and just as first the time measures one part of the motion and then another, and so on for each part. <insert>Motion</insert> if it were infinite could not be measured by a finite time. <insert>Again</insert>, every measured motion which is completed in a finite time is not measured except by dividing the motion by its proportions; for the motion and the time ought to be proportional to each other, just as it was observed that it is a property of motion itself that neither the time nor the motion exceeds the other. And thus, if the time measuring the motion is finite, the motion itself is finite; and if the motion is finite, the body that is moved must be finite. <insert>It is established</insert>, therefore, that the heaven is finite.

<insert>Now</insert> we shall show through physics that all bodies are within the heaven and none exist outside of it. First, we shall prove that no body is outside that heaven; secondly, that there is nothing active nor violent <note>In Aristotelian terms, "violent" motion is that which goes against a thing's natural inclination, such as throwing a stone upward.</note> outside it. 

<insert>That</insert> no body exists outside the heaven is proved thus: for every body is contained within the heaven and is composed of elements. <insert>Again</insert>, the heaven is not outside the heaven, because nothing exists outside the total body; therefore, the heaven is not outside the heaven. <insert>Again</insert>, the elements are not outside the heaven, because the lowest of all elements is earth, which is heavy; then water, which is also heavy; then air in the same proportion; and fire is above the air. Therefore, since all elements are beneath fire, and fire exists beneath the heaven, it follows that no element is outside this heaven. <insert>Likewise</insert>, heavy things tend downward toward the center, while light elements tend upward toward the circumference, which is the heaven, where it is said that nothing...

<summary>This page provides geometric and physical arguments for a finite universe, arguing that the heaven's rotation in a twenty-four-hour day proves its finitude. It also describes the geocentric arrangement of the four elements—earth, water, air, and fire—as being entirely contained within the celestial sphere.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the logical demonstration that the universe (the heaven) is finite and unique. <meta>This page continues the discussion on the physical nature of the heavens. For whatever is not generated from its own corrupted parts is also corrupted from its beginning, as Aristotle says in the book *On the Heavens and the Earth* <note>original: "libro de celo et mundo"; this refers to Aristotle's foundational work on cosmology, *De Caelo*.</note>; this is evident. <meta>This page continues the technical commentary on Aristotle’s *De Caelo* (On the Heavens). The previous page discussed the elements and the moisture-inducing influence of the moon. Here, the author transitions into the metaphysical and physical reasons for the positions and motions of the heavenly bodies, specifically contrasting the "noble" circular motion of the heavens with the "violent" or finite motions of earthly objects.</meta>

Regarding the first point in the book *On the Heavens and the World* <note>original: "libro de ce. et m.o"; refers to Aristotle's *De Caelo et Mundo*.</note>, it is established and determined that everything is contained by fire in a specific way that is beneficial, and for this reason it is confirmed; a similar principle holds for the other elements.

As for why the Earth is situated downward <note>Medieval cosmology placed the Earth at the absolute center (the "bottom") of the universe, with the lighter elements—water, air, and fire—layered above it.</note>, it is because God willed to place human beings upon it. Furthermore, the nobility of the superior bodies <note>The "superior bodies" are the planets and stars, which were considered more "noble" because they were closer to the divine and made of a more perfect substance than earthly matter.</note> is established through their compatibility; for they are all unchangeable, incorruptible, and impassible <note>original: "impassibilia"; in a philosophical sense, this means they cannot be affected or changed by any external force.</note>. It is necessary that the soul... the essence of inferior things, just as the heaven is more noble than all other bodies. This is because of its own nature and its more noble motion and natural form. We find it free from all the pain and weariness that affects others.

But regarding motion without motion, we have another reasoning: because they are moved by their own motion, it cannot be separated from them. That which is called the "principle" is said to be uncertain, stemming from the fact that being in motion and "being" itself are granted together. Furthermore, every violent motion or everything that causes harm, as it seems, is far from God; his state is seen as such to common ears, just as we have motion according to what would have been here.

Therefore, it remains that everything that is in motion is moved by another, rather than by itself, unless through "passion" <note>In scholastic philosophy, "passion" refers to the state of being acted upon by an external agent.</note> they should seek out existence. The heaven is impassible, having no contrariety <note>original: "contrarietatem"; this refers to the lack of opposing qualities—like hot vs. cold or wet vs. dry—which cause change and decay on Earth.</note> whatsoever. Therefore, it was not renewed by itself; the proper motion of the heaven is eternal.

Furthermore, the motion of the heaven circles perpetually by its own nature, because it possesses neither an end to its intention nor any interruption; it is always "lord-like" and uniform. It tends more toward acquiring that state where it should be; through celestial motion, it does not tend toward some other destination, but rather toward acquiring its own perfection. Therefore, it is eternal according to the order of nature.

From the aforesaid, it is clear that the motion of the heaven is neither sudden nor violent, because every such motion is a passive motion. Let us conclude that in other bodies—whether they are moved naturally or violently—they move along a different line and through a finite space, for all things [on Earth] exist in a finite space.

That which proceeds from a certain body as an operation... it will be infinite. However, if it stops there, then the motion is not eternal. If it stops, then the question at hand arises: that motion is not eternal because it will stop, and therefore it will return. Thus, it is not as predicted. But then it would return in either the same time or a different time. If it were in the same time, it would go and return simultaneously; therefore, opposites would exist in the same subject at once, which is impossible. But if it returns in another time, then there was a period of rest in the middle. Therefore, that motion will eventually cease, and thus it is not truly eternal. <note>The author is using a logical proof to show that only circular motion can be eternal. Linear motion (back and forth) requires a moment of rest at the turning point, meaning it is not continuous and therefore not eternal.</note>

<summary>This page discusses the theological and physical reasons for the Earth's central position and the eternal, "noble" nature of celestial circular motion. It argues that unlike earthly movement, which is finite and subject to rest, the heavens move perpetually because they lack internal conflict or external resistance.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the technical commentary on Aristotle’s *De Caelo* (On the Heavens). The previous page discussed the nobility and incorruptibility of the heavenly bodies. Here, the author examines the specific nature of celestial motion, arguing that it is neither "violent" (forced) nor "univocal" (having the same nature as terrestrial movement), but is instead a unique, effortless form of natural motion that governs all life on Earth.</meta>

Regarding the first point, the motion of the heaven is <term>natural</term><note>In Aristotelian physics, "natural motion" is the internal tendency of an object to move toward its rightful place, like a stone falling to earth.</note> according to the third book of *On Nature*. It is neither uniform <note>original: "univoce"; referring to motion that is of one single kind or directed toward a single terrestrial goal.</note> nor violent [forced]. <insert>For</insert> this motion is said to be "more natural" and proper because the motion of the heaven is neither [merely] natural in the terrestrial sense nor violent. <insert>Likewise</insert>, it is proved that the motion of the heaven is not uniform because all uniform motion originates from a soul <note>original: "ab animo"; referring to the animating principle or intent.</note> directed toward something, and it always intends to acquire a specific goal; rather, it eventually turns toward rest from that motion. <insert>The motion</insert> of the heaven, however, does not cease at any point where it might stop, because it is not [simply] natural.

For they have proved by another form that the motion of the heaven is not uniform, because those things that move uniformly—being of the same species and the same nature—move toward the same destination, just as all heavy things descend toward the center and all light things ascend toward the upper regions. If the heaven were of such a nature and species that its motion were uniform, it would move toward only one direction, and there would be no division there. Concerning the division of the heaven and the First Mover, Aristotle says in the book *On the Heavens and the World* that the spherical bodies move from east to west, and the spheres move according to the manner of their own mobility, rather than all moving toward the same part.

<insert>Thus</insert> the motion of the heaven is not uniform. <insert>Likewise</insert>, it is proved by another reason that the motion of the heaven is not uniform, because the motion of the heaven is our end [goal], and earthly things follow it; for through that motion, all things of this world are generated, corrupted, and altered. That which is perfected by an agent is nobler than the agent itself when it is directed toward those ends; since its goal is achieved through that motion, it is not subject to uniform motion. <insert>And</insert> the fact that the motion of the heaven is not violent was established above. <insert>Hence</insert>, nothing "violent" [forced] is swift at its conclusion <note>Aristotle argued that forced motion, like a thrown stone, slows down as it nears its end, whereas the heavens move with constant, perfect speed.</note>.

<insert>Likewise</insert>, nothing else is shown; Aristotle says that the celestial motion is without pain and labor; nor is it by reason [calculative effort], because the motion of the heaven is without pain and without labor, and for this reason the motion of the heaven is not violent. <insert>For</insert> he says natural motion is in the middle, and because the motion of the heaven does not intensify [as it approaches a goal], it is not violent. <insert>Furthermore</insert>, regarding the idea that motion owes a part to another motion of the heaven according to nature, Aristotle brings this into [discussion]... in which it is said that it is not simply a matter of reason... it is shown that the human soul moves its own body and the body...

<summary>This page argues that celestial motion is a unique category of movement—neither forced like a thrown object nor seeking rest like a falling stone—but is an effortless, eternal natural force that drives all change on Earth.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the transition from celestial physics to the foundational principles of terrestrial matter. While the previous page discussed the "desire" and motion of the heavens, this text moves into the "principles" and "elements" that constitute the physical world on Earth.</meta>

It must be known, and rightly so, that above all else it is written what constitutes the **principle** <note>original: "principium." In this context, it refers to the fundamental source or origin of all things.</note> of all things, and great things first, in the order of its own dignity. However, **elements** are so named by convention and they consist within other things. <insert>¶</insert> **But** commonly, according to widespread opinion, the "principle" is said to belong to the body and to things mixed sensibly; and according to this definition, an element is called **matter**. <note>The author is defining "element" here as the "prime matter"—the underlying "stuff" of the universe before it takes on a specific shape.</note> It is that from which things are first composed; it exists within the thing and is not divided according to its form. Furthermore, it must exist in another form, which is why it is called **water** insofar as it is water by itself, and similarly for the other elements. According to this, the **four elements** are distinguished, namely: **fire, air, water, and earth**. These are ordered in a continuum, not according to their form, but according to the qualities of **hot and cold, moist and dry**. <note>These are the four "primary qualities" in Aristotelian physics that combine to create the elements: for example, Fire is hot and dry, while Water is cold and moist.</note> All things are said to exist from these by reason of their forms. Whence nothing is constituted according to matter alone, but rather is so constituted according to its forms. <insert>¶</insert> Regarding the **elements**, however, there are diverse opinions. Some indeed posited that the aforementioned elements were one single thing and not many, while others held that they were infinite.

**Empedocles** <note>A Greek pre-Socratic philosopher (c. 494–434 BC) who first established the four-element theory.</note> and **Aristotle**, however, posited that regarding the elements from which all things are made, there are four; nothing else is called corporeal. And so they posited that within these four elements exists the **prime matter** <note>original: "materiam primam." This is the pure potentiality that underlies all physical objects.</note> of all bodies. They also posited two formal principles of all things, namely **Strife and Friendship**. <note>original: "litem & amicitiam." Also known as Love and Strife, these were the cosmic forces Empedocles believed drove the mixing and separation of the elements.</note> They said that in the processes of generation <note>The "birth" or "becoming" of physical objects.</note> these two were present, namely the hot and the cold, and they said they were separated from them and then glued together into a single mass because of **Friendship**. Afterwards, in that space of time, as all generations came to be, they were separated by **Strife**. And thus it happened after a **Great Year**, <note>original: "magnū annū." A cosmic cycle after which the celestial bodies return to their original positions; here defined as 10,000 years.</note> that is, after ten thousand years.

<insert>¶</insert> From the sayings of Empedocles, they say by necessity that the elements are two; the principles of the element were of that mass and from that mass. <insert>¶</insert> **Democritus**, however, and **Leucippus** <note>original: "Luappus," a scribal error for Leucippus, the founder of Atomism.</note> posited that an **indivisible body** was the principle of all bodies; they called these two things **atoms**, <note>original: "athomos." From the Greek 'atomos,' meaning 'uncuttable.'</note> which they said were both infinite and the first of all things. They prove this as follows: <insert>¶</insert> It is impossible for a body to be divided into infinity. For if it were possible for a body to be divided into infinity, it would either be reduced to nothing—and then a body would consist of nothing, which is impossible—or it would reach a point that is either a body or not a body. If it is not a body, then a body would be constituted of non-bodies, which is likewise impossible. <insert>¶</insert> Likewise, if that point is a body, then it must be **indivisible**, which [the proponents of infinite division] deny.

<summary>This page explores the classical theories of matter, contrasting the four-element system of Aristotle and Empedocles (driven by Love and Strife) with the atomic theory of Democritus and Leucippus. It includes a logical proof for the existence of atoms based on the impossibility of dividing matter infinitely.</summary>
<keywords>elements, prime matter, fire air water earth, Empedocles, Aristotle, Strife and Friendship, Great Year, Democritus, Leucippus, atoms, indivisibility, physics</keywords>

<meta>This page transitions from the general definition of "elements" provided on the previous page into a specific debate among ancient Greek philosophers. It explores the nature of divisibility—whether matter can be divided forever or if there are "smallest parts"—and contrasts the theories of Anaxagoras and Empedocles regarding the fundamental building blocks of reality.</meta>

The invisible, which is invisible, is situated in two ways. **Because**, indeed, there is that which is divided into infinity and yet cannot be divided. But lastly, because what is invisible in this way—as demonstrable bodies are, like every body—an author responds to this by saying that a body is divisible into infinity in potential, but not in act <note>Aristotle’s famous distinction: "potentiality" (what could happen) versus "actuality" (what is physically realized). Here, he argues you can theoretically divide something forever, but you cannot physically do so.</note>. To the intellect, a body is divisible into infinity, and such an "invisible" thing at the very last cannot be divided any further.

**Anaxagoras** <note>Anaxagoras (c. 500–428 BCE) was a Pre-Socratic philosopher who argued that "everything is in everything."</note> posited infinite elements and spoke of the principles of all things. And **Empedocles** <note>Empedocles (c. 494–434 BCE) is famous for establishing the four classical elements: fire, water, air, and earth.</note> said that those four elements—namely fire, water, air, and earth—were the elemental things, while others exist as <term>for example</term> <note>original: "puta." Used here to introduce an illustration.</note> wood, stone, flesh, and the like. This would not be held in such a manner. **Whence** he says that those four are the elements, and the reasoning is demonstrated against others. Beyond this, that reasoning which is gathered from them is either a medium or it is not; it is not in them as it would not be consistent for all things. For what they are is just as what is within them, and therefore by the same reasoning it is said both in those things and all things regarding them. 

All things are just as the elements are, and those clear things—namely fire, air, water, and earth—are the elemental components. **Whence Anaxagoras** stood by a similar principle, and in anything it is said that it is as if it were a mixture; as even earth is in that very thing, and the same in that reasoning. For earth is toward another in truth, and so on beyond infinity. **But** now concerning the principle against him in that proposition, holding that opinion in the first book of the <term>Physics</term> <note>original: "phisicorum," referring to Aristotle's *Physica*, a foundational text on the study of nature.</note>: that some "flesh" is the **smallest flesh**, such that if that flesh is divided, it is no longer flesh. **Whence** that which is a certain minimum is bone, and concerning individual things. And in that very thing is another reason: that the reason which is in its own nature is a minimum "one," because everything that is contained as one is common to <unclear>nature</unclear>. By that reasoning, there would be a minimum of flesh, and again that which is the minimum of bone. It is impossible to be preliminary, which opinion I shall lead to its end. 

**Against this**, however, they said that it was "One," for they instantly said it is indeed mobile—that is, they are in the "unmoving" <note>original: "immoto." This refers to the concept of a static, unchanging universe.</note>. These are **Parmenides** and **Melissus** <note>Parmenides and Melissus were Eleatic philosophers who argued that change and motion are illusions and that "Being" is a single, unchanging substance.</note>, who hold that the whole is "One." Yet, it does not suit them to say a thing is moved, and they proved it in this way: saying that "being" is unmoving, yet they move toward motion—or rather, they move concerning the body in the end, because time is from motion. **Furthermore**, he said that it is one alone, however...

<summary>This page discusses the debate between ancient philosophers over the divisibility of matter, contrasting Anaxagoras’s infinite elements with Empedocles’s four elements, and introducing the Aristotelian critique of the "smallest parts" of substances like flesh and bone.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of natural philosophy, moving from the definition of elements by Anaxagoras and Empedocles to a detailed Aristotelian explanation of how the four elements—fire, air, water, and earth—relate to one another through the four "primary qualities" (hot, cold, moist, and dry).</meta>

...of matter and of fire, and that there were two formal principles according to <unclear>art</unclear> and sense. He proves this in the following manner: First, the body is mobile, and all motion originates from fire. Because no body is so soft <note>In this context, "soft" likely refers to the pliability or subtle nature of matter before it densifies.</note>, fire is the element of all other "lower" bodies. According to this theory, as fire is condensed, it becomes air; as it is further condensed, it becomes water; and as it is condensed even more, it becomes earth. Below these, fire alone is the primary element.

It remains now to speak of the first category of these varieties of the ancient elements, which are **hot**, **cold**, **moist**, and **dry**. By these, the elements are defined: 

*   First, <unclear>fire</unclear> is hot and dry.
*   **Air** is moist and hot.
*   **Water** is cold and moist.
*   **Earth** is cold and dry. 

It must be understood, however, that among these four combinations, four are possible, but two are impossible—namely, a combination of **hot and cold**, or **dry and moist**. Because of these [impossible] combinations, no element can exist according to them, for it is impossible for such contrary qualities to exist in the same thing at once.

These are called the <term>primary qualities</term> <note>In Aristotelian physics, these four qualities are the basic building blocks of all physical matter.</note> because they do not "flow" from others; rather, all other qualities arise and flow from them. For example, heat does not arise from dryness, nor does it depend on it, nor vice versa; nor does cold depend on moisture, nor vice versa; and so it is with the others. 

And that this is so, the master <unclear>God</unclear> <note>original: "dominus deo." The scribe may mean "the master [Aristotle] has proved."</note> has proved: for if heat depended on dryness, then fire would be "maximally hot" as a whole, and thus fire (which is indeed maximally hot) would also have to be "maximally dry." But this is false, as is proven below through the definition provided by **Aristotle**, because earth is actually the "maximally dry" element. The same logic applies to the other qualities. 

It should be noted that one <term>complexion</term> <note>original: "complexio." This refers to the specific temperament or "mix" of qualities in a substance.</note> does not depend on another, which is why they are called primary qualities. Following these are flavors and other traits caused by these primaries, such as: 
*   **Hard** and **soft**
*   **White** and **black**
*   **Sweet** and **bitter**, and so on. 

The same must be held regarding the properties and differences of these qualities—namely hot, cold, moist, and dry. For **heat** differs from the others during the process of <term>generation and corruption</term> <note>The natural cycle of birth/creation and decay/death.</note>. Heat gathers together <term>homogeneous</term> <note>original: "omogeneorum." Substances of the same kind or nature.</note> things and separates <term>heterogeneous</term> <note>original: "etherogeneorum." Substances of different or diverse kinds.</note> things. For example, it gathers gold and silver [of their own kind], and separates gold from iron, or lead from silver. These are then called homogeneous...

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the primary qualities, specifically exploring how heat and cold act upon different types of matter. It moves into the definitions of homogeneous and heterogeneous substances, a core part of Aristotelian physical theory used to explain how complex bodies are formed and dissolved.</meta>

From *homoios* <note>original: "homoeo"; likely the Greek *homos*, meaning same or alike</note>, which means "alike," and *genus* <note>original: "genis"</note>, they are called **homogeneous**, which refers to all things of the same genus and species. And there are **heterogeneous** <note>original: "ethomogenea"; from the Greek *heteros* meaning different</note> things, from *heteros* which means "different" and *genus*, and those are called **heterogeneous** which are composed of different genera or species.

Therefore, following the common definition previously given, we say that with the arrival of the sun's heat, it gathers chaff with grain <note>original: "paleam cum hruo"; likely referring to the threshing or drying process where heat helps separate or categorize materials</note>; even though these are all **heterogeneous**, it does not disperse them. And they respond that the principal act of the **hot** is to heat. Afterward, everything that is condensed requires <unclear>substance</unclear>. 

And so it is said that division does not occur entirely from the power of the acting agent, but from the diversity of the matter itself. For in the shadows, the **hot** is <unclear>subtle</unclear>. And it is clear that division does not come solely from the power of the agent, but from the diversity of the matter being acted upon; for instance, heat acts one way in glue, another way in **wax**, and in those things which are divided and condensed in the same way, such as in **clay**. The definition previously given was stated in comparison to the element of **earth**, regarding the providing of matter rather than the form itself. 

It must be understood that the gathering of **homogeneous** and **heterogeneous** things happens well when the heat acts with <unclear>topaz</unclear> and <unclear>silver</unclear>, so that it does not become <unclear>too hot</unclear>; rather, we would say that in **summer**, by chance, it does not <unclear>pertain</unclear>. 

In **winter**, it is observed that the air is more <unclear>consuming</unclear>, yet it does not corrupt. The reason is that **digestion** is "hotter" <note>The author refers to the Aristotelian concept of *antiperistasis*, where internal heat is concentrated and becomes more efficient when the surrounding environment is cold.</note> in **winter** than it is in **summer**. And it is clear that the cold of winter puts things to flight through chilling and division, and everything is condensed. 

**Homogeneous** and **heterogeneous** things, and others, are principally governed by these qualities. It helps to understand the <term>protheic</term> <note>Likely referring to 'protean' or primary matter</note> and <unclear>adamant</unclear>, from which agile things are made in the <unclear>presence</unclear> of those things which are cooled by heat and heated by cold <note>Another reference to the interaction of opposites in physics</note>. It is seen that the arrival of heat <unclear>moves</unclear> the natural humors and causes them to exhale, so that the body is cooled; and a world of cold consumes the body and refines the better **humors** <note>The four bodily fluids—blood, phlegm, black bile, and yellow bile—which were thought to determine health and temperament</note>, so that the body becomes effectively warm. 

So it is with every action, that these qualities are provided by that which is <unclear>the source</unclear>. It is clear that a **humor** is so called because it acts more through fluidity. And it is clear that the pores of these bodies are such because every body is porous, and the agent acts through the pores, causing the condensation which <unclear>limits</unclear> them; thus they are well-ordered and divisible.

the elements, and thus the elements were not prior [in time] <note>original: "priora"; the author argues that elements did not exist before the moment of creation, as they are the fundamental building blocks of time and matter itself</note>; on that same day they would not have been elements, which is false. <insert>It remains</insert> therefore that the elements are not generated <note>meaning naturally produced from existing matter</note> but were created, which agrees with the Author <note>likely referring to God as the Creator or an authoritative source like Augustine</note> who says that all things were created immediately together in peace <note>original: "paco," likely a transcription for "pacto" or "pariter," meaning "by agreement" or "equally/together"</note> in matter and form. <insert>Through</insert> this, it should be known that there is a twofold type of **generation** <term>generatio; the process of a new substance coming into being</term>, namely simple and gradual. Simple <insert>generation</insert> is the generation of casting away <note>the "abiectio" or casting off of a previous form</note>. Simple [generation] can be defined: <insert>Generation</insert> is the acquisition of matter by a **substantial form** <term>forma substantialis; the essential nature that makes a thing what it is</term> and the change into another substantial form.

<meta>This page continues the investigation into the nature of change, specifically moving from the general definition of substantial generation (the creation of a new thing) to specific categories of physical change: alteration, growth, shrinking, and movement. It maintains the Aristotelian framework established on the previous page.</meta>

From **substantial form** to substantial form. Likewise, in **alteration** <term>alteratio; a change in the qualities of a thing, such as its color or temperature, without changing its fundamental nature</term>, there is no permanence regarding substance. In **generation and corruption** <note>the birth of one substance and the death of another</note>, however, the change is indeed substantial. Likewise, I speak of the "sensible" according to **prime matter** <term>materia prima; the fundamental, formless "stuff" that underlies all physical reality</term> which, although it remains the same during the process of generation, is not itself perceptible to the senses.

Next follows **augmentation and diminution**.

Augmentation <note>growth</note> is said to be defined in two ways: augmentation is motion from a smaller **quantity** <term>quantitas; the measurable size or extension of an object</term> to a larger one. And I say the same for the opposite: Diminution <note>shrinking</note> is motion from a larger quantity to a smaller one. And note that the act of augmenting and the thing being augmented are not the same in all their aspects and causes. Indeed, all things behave similarly regarding growth in their forms; that is, the relationship between that which is acted upon and that which is fed <note>original: "pascenda"; referring to how an organism takes in nourishment to grow</note> is not accidental or forced.

For the act of augmenting and the thing being augmented differ, yet they affect one another; and that which is augmented is a type of motion. Furthermore, it concerns nourishment where one thing sustains another and vice versa. Food gives form, capacity, and being to its opposite <note>meaning the body that consumes it</note>, and according to this conversion, the body expands in width and length through thickening; it is not merely dilated, but truly thickened, and every such motion makes it clear that augmentation is a form of movement.

From this, it is evident that diminution is also a motion. However, augmentation and diminution differ from **alteration** because alteration is concerned with quality, whereas augmentation and diminution are concerned with quantity.

Next follows **change of place**.

**Change of place** <term>mutatio loci; movement from one point to another</term> is motion from one location to another, and such motion is threefold: namely, one that is **violent** <note>original: "violentus"; motion caused by an external force that goes against a thing's natural tendency</note> and others which have already been discussed.

Regarding change and the things that precede change, such as contact, we must first look at the nature of **touch**. Touch, or **contact** <term>tactus; physical connection between two bodies</term>, involves many things combined into one; in the context of generation and corruption, it is considered in three ways.

First, contact is said to occur in things that are contiguous, whose outer limits are together, as when someone touches a wall with their hand—they touch the wall. Second, it is considered in itself: how the toucher is touched in return. Third is contact according to "depth," according to which it is said that one unstable degree touches another, yet they are not touched in return <note>The author likely refers to a hierarchy where a higher force acts upon a lower one without being affected back</note>. In the fourth way, touch is spoken of in continuous things, where parts are joined into one and the same...

<summary>This page defines the various types of Aristotelian motion, distinguishing between qualitative change (alteration), quantitative change (growth and shrinking), and local motion (movement). It concludes by introducing the physical requirements for change, specifically the mechanics of "contact" or touch between bodies.</summary>

<keywords>Aristotelian physics, substantial form, alteration, augmentation, diminution, prime matter, change of place, contact, tactus, motion</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the scholastic commentary on Aristotelian physics. It transitions from the discussion of growth (augmentation) on page 91 into the mechanics of physical interaction—specifically "contact" and "mixture"—before beginning a new section on composite (complex) bodies. The text relies heavily on Aristotle’s *On Generation and Corruption*.</meta>

The commentary touches upon how, in the preceding section, continuity was discussed before moving to the second point, which concerns the **twofold mode** of interaction. Here, the first point is addressed as the Reason explains: the first mode occurs through the application of two spheres, whose boundaries meet at a middle point for the purpose of acting and being acted upon <note>original: "actionem et passionem"; the standard Aristotelian framework where one thing acts (agent) and another is changed (patient)</note>. They act and are acted upon by touching, just as when fire acts upon water to heat it; thus, the heater acts, and the heated finger <note>likely an example of a finger feeling heat</note> is acted upon by it. This occurs through **contact**. We see that contact exists in these things where accidents and passions <note>properties and undergoes changes</note> occur, which are joined according to a natural or voluntary order.

Next follows **mixture** <term>mixtio; the process by which different substances combine to form a new, uniform substance</term>. Note first that there is a twofold type of mixture. The first is **mixture according to the senses** <note>original: "ad sensum"</note>, which occurs when, for example, grains of barley and wheat are mixed together; this is called a mixture by the common person, but it is not a true mixture. The other is **mixture according to truth** <note>original: "secundum veritatem"</note>, which is defined in the first book of *On Generation and Corruption* as follows: "Mixture is the union of altered mixables" <note>original: "mixtio est mistiōbilūm alteratorūm unio"; a classic Aristotelian definition</note>. 

Note that all things which are mixable are also those that act and are acted upon; they act insofar as they are divided, and are acted upon for the same reason. Note further that things which do not possess **contrariety** <term>contrarietas; having opposite qualities like hot/cold or wet/dry</term> are not mixable. Therefore, the elements are mixable because they have a relationship of opposition to one another. Some argue that the elements do not remain in the mixture as themselves, but are indivisible within it. Every body is divisible, yet there are **natural minima** <term>minima naturalia; the smallest possible unit of a substance that still retains its specific nature</term> which are determined by nature. Note that the perfection of a mixture is that the mixed substance ought to be **homogeneous** <note>of the same kind throughout</note> and of the same species as the whole; otherwise, it would not be a true mixture.

Now we must distinguish between **composition**, **confusion**, and **mixture**.
**Composition** <note>original: "compositio"</note> occurs when the components remain distinct and are joined in a certain order and number, like a heap.
**Confusion** <note>original: "confusio"</note>, however, is when the primary units are not proportioned, as if thrown together by chance.
Note that these points regarding the elements should suffice for the present.

**D**iscussion has been concluded regarding the **simple generable mobile body** <note>referring to the four basic elements: Earth, Air, Fire, and Water</note>. Now we must speak of the **composite generable mobile body** <note>complex things made of elements, like plants or animals</note>, and first concerning the **elemented things** <term>elementatio; substances formed from the elements</term>. Regarding these, it must first be noted that every "elemented" or composite thing—such as vapor, exhalations, water, and earth—does not remain closed off when affected by the heat of the sun.

<summary>This page defines the physics of "contact" and "mixture," distinguishing true chemical-like mixtures from simple heaps (composition). It marks a transition from studying the four basic elements to studying complex, "elemented" bodies like vapors and minerals.</summary>
<keywords>contact, mixture, Aristotle, elements, homogeneity, natural minima, composition, confusion, composite bodies</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the scholastic investigation into the nature of the physical world, moving from the general principles of mixture and contact discussed on the previous page into specific applications within meteorology, alchemy (the composition of metals), and the biological hierarchy of living things—from plants to sensitive animals and finally to rational humans.</meta>

In two times, every element is brought into focus according to "more" or "less." In this manner, regarding the agent of change <note>original: "menstruum"; in alchemy and natural philosophy, this refers to a solvent or a catalyst that triggers a change</note> itself, **Heat** is that which is consuming from it. In double passions and impressions, there is a **Common** region of the world on the earth; since all the earth is opened above, those things that occur in the waste places are divided among all. They are called **meteors** <note>original: "meteora"; at this time, this term referred to any atmospheric phenomena, including rain, wind, and lightning, not just falling rocks from space</note>, belonging to two common realms of the air and the sublunary sphere <note>the region between the Earth and the Moon, where all change and decay occur in Aristotelian physics</note>.

Toward everything under the sphere of the moon, so much is from the bright rising, and from many other things which are spoken of in the first part. **Common** according to the world's constitution, at these times it heats the springs in winter. **One** thing itself is the first in the time of motion, and from itself of those things generating together. The matter, as certain philosophers held, was **Quicksilver** <term>argentum vivum; the metal mercury, which was believed to be the "mother" or primary material of all other metals</term>. It heats by combustion with wine and salts; the stone is quicksilver, which is the principle of metals, without one exception. Lead and tin are discovered above silver and gold.

The **Element** air is not a complex metal, which indeed is thus formed from water and gold; and in sum, and at that time it is inclined. And all these elements of the earth are composed. There are also certain intermediate things <note>original: "elementata media"</note> which are neither entirely under the earth nor upon the earth, and they are composed of two things, all mixed with earth. They were of the "earth-born" <note>original: "terra natentium"; likely referring to primitive organisms or things generated spontaneously from damp soil</note>, and they are plants, trees, and herbs; and certain ones are not like the others.

For they wish **Dormant things** <note>original: "dormientia"; referring to organisms that appear inactive or lack obvious movement, like fungi or certain plants</note> to be intermediate between animals and plants, such as sea sponges, which possess sensation yet are hardened like plants. And they are transformed like others, and the end of these is vapors; through which, when they are generated, they are more from the winds and by means of rain, just as they are found in the unclean earth, "earth-born" from mixed earth and water. 

And these are among the composites in which I sometimes compose in a certain mode—that is, a "confused comparison" which prohibited the elements from being what they were. They were and are common. Truly the waste places, along with plants, are animate and **vegetable**. **Toward** those which are called predatory <note>original: "rapaces"; here referring to active or carnivorous animals</note>, they are among the earth-born, and from these earth-born things humors are generated. 

The "unclean animals" themselves are made from two humors, as are seed and the measure of that remaining within. This is what I say in part is the sum of inconsistent opposites, by which they say that they receive from composites and such rational things in man. Because they would have been generated, I compose. The **Common** element is more densely connected in irrational animals than in the earth-born; that is, they possess a more noble and **sensitive soul** <term>anima sensibilis; the level of the soul that allows for perception, movement, and feeling, shared by animals and humans</term>. **One** thing in humans is that their matter is more temperate and more refined.

<summary>This page outlines the Aristotelian hierarchy of the natural world, beginning with atmospheric "meteors" and the alchemical composition of metals from mercury, then moving to intermediate life forms like sponges, and concluding with the distinction between the souls of plants, animals, and humans.</summary>
<keywords>meteorology, quicksilver, mercury, elements, plants, sea sponges, spontaneous generation, sensitive soul, human nature, scholasticism</keywords>

The vaporous [matter] is purified by a small [degree] and becomes that which is [refined]. <meta>This page continues the technical meteorological discussion from page 93. While the previous page focused on the transformation of elements (air into water, etc.) and the "double vapor," this page analyzes the mechanics of solar heat and cold based on the sun's position in the zodiac and its angle of reflection (obliquity) relative to the Earth's latitudes.</meta>

And there is a twofold [process]: the sun raising vapors into the air, and the **Place** [acting] in purifying and igniting the elevated vapor. **Heat** from the sun is also twofold in its effect from above: it is "bringing" and "elevating." It is **bringing** [heat] in two instances: one is the [extension] of all things, the other is the effect of the sun being in a "hot sign" <note>original: "signo calidū"; referring to the summer zodiac signs of Cancer, Leo, and Virgo</note> along with summer fruit and all the "Dog Days" <note>original: "cane"; referring to the 'Canicular' days of mid-summer associated with the star Sirius, the Dog Star</note>. 

At that time, the rays—which are needed in all regions—fall upon the earth in a perpendicular motion, falling from the center of heaven. This is **evident** from above, and they produce a maximum and continuous heat on the earth, which **brings** and continually elevates [vapor]. 

In places [moving] from the equinoctial [line] <note>the celestial equator</note> according to latitude, receding further toward the north: the more they recede from the path of the sun, the more obliquely the rays are reflected, and the angles of the rays are more widened. Consequently, the heat becomes less, bringing less warmth and instead raising more cold vapor. Thus, they obtain a moisture from the path of the [heavens] which is fragile, because with less heat, the "bringing" effect is more or less reduced.

**Cold** is also twofold. **It is** either a cold that consumes the [purity] or consumes the whole... **So that** it prevents the exhalation of air from reaching the pores of the earth, and they [the vapors] are contained so that the rays of the sun cannot radiate into them or reach the ground. There **is** also a temperate cold that [acts] so that it does not [invade?], not preventing [vapor] from exiting. This occurs when the distance from the path of the sun and the movement of the sun according to latitude [shifts] from the equinoctial to the southern region. 

When the sun is in those [signs], namely in Sagittarius, Capricorn, and Aquarius, it is greatly removed from the inhabited climates of the earth. Then the rays are reflected only very obliquely upon the inhabitants of those climates; therefore, cold and abundant vapors are found in them, because they [the cold/vapors] constrict the earth. **Hence**, it should be understood that in places of [summer] ice and vapor... **From** these divisions of vapors, places, and causes, it may be known that everything which is placed in one or the other... by such a division, the generation of such things occurs.

<summary>This page explains how the sun’s angle and zodiacal position dictate the intensity of heat and the formation of weather. It describes how perpendicular rays create summer heat, while oblique rays in winter signs like Capricorn cause cold by trapping vapors and constricting the earth's pores.</summary>

[These phenomena arise] from dry vapor as well as humid [vapor], or from fire. In these [Dragons], the matter is of equal length <note>original: "cubalis"; measured in cubits</note>, then thin and apt for flaming from the heat of the place. They are seen better and better as they follow many parts, and for that reason they are expandable. Then it is not a small thing, and much [light] appears in its very motion. Then it is said of the matter that the trajectory is widened and abundant; it joins the stretching to another equality, first and reddish-gold <note>original: "rutilum"</note>. Then, in the act of making, the fiery [part] is dilated, and the more heat the parts contain, the more and more reddish-gold they become in sharpness. It brings itself to reach hotter [regions], and they are seen because of their length. 

Concerning the higher [phenomena]: it is very dense, like a spark that lies hidden in the mass itself, and it is common all the way up to the high cord. <insert>The same</insert> applies to the motion from outer places and fire from a part, such as **perpendicular fire** <note>original: "ignis perpendicularis"; fire falling or rising in a straight vertical line</note>. <insert>If</insert> it is equally long and hot and sudden, then in these things much resistance is expanded by the fire; it is seen through the whole part, then the whole will be inflamed in the same [way]. <meta>This page continues the technical examination of "impressions" or atmospheric phenomena, moving from the general description of the "Flying Dragon" to the specific physics of how these vapors ignite. It uses the common medieval scientific analogy of a candle being relit by its smoke to explain how fire travels through the air to create meteors.</meta>

The example of a lit **candle** <note>original: "candela"</note> is considered, because when the flame is on the surface, it is then more compressed and spreads out lengthwise—unless the sight [perceives it otherwise] because it is equally subtle. Because it can only be extended in one part through a point <unclear>with truth</unclear> that is extruded. It stands in one part toward another, and then that **extinguished vapor** <note>original: "vapor ille extinctus"</note> is seen ascending through the sight. Because it is thick and because it is from something subtle, it is not well-continued nor adhesive to itself; one part sustains itself from another for a while, and then it seems to ascend, led after itself, and it flies away like a **form of spark** <note>original: "s’miallis de forma"; likely "scintilla," a spark</note>.
And in this, they all agree that all things fly upward into the fire, and from there the body extends downward. And if the vapor is not indeed pulling vehemently, then it cannot penetrate the middle part of the water, being most fleeting. And then there are likewise **impressions** <term>impressions; a technical term in Aristotelian physics for phenomena produced in the air by vapors, such as meteors or rainbows</term> from that vapor, or rather that vapor is hot, rare, and well-continuous, so that its parts cohere strongly. 
Where it is transformative and there is dissipation, then [it takes] the heat itself, or it constricts well in the intercepted water, and in fleeing the cold, it consists of part to part, and from that **compression it is inflamed** <note>this describes the medieval theory that cold air "squeezes" hot vapor until it ignites</note>. And because of the dissipation, the parts are seen. 
And there is a confusion of fire. And because much cold touches it, it seems to emerge as if segregated; and a lit **tow** <term>tow; coarse, flaxen fibers used as tinder or for wicks</term> is blown out. If it is said that the vapor is well-continuous and is expelled by much touching cold, then it is called a **compacted vapor**, gathered into a ball and firm; it is hard but not very compacted. If it is said to be not very compacted nor less so, then it will fall continuously and not in a middle way. 
And through this continuous falling, it appears to be a **long star** <note>original: "stella longa"; a shooting star or meteor</note> which prolongs the sight by falling after itself. This was examined by the **Philosopher** <note>original: "philosopho"; referring to Aristotle, specifically his work *Meteorologica*</note> in the case of a candle lighting another candle continuously through a downward path. 
And if the matter is much gathered together, then it takes on a flickering moisture from the depths and finds its occasion in the water. If it is not moderate, then they are perceived by their own friction and rage in the water; and this is what they say are the dissipations of the night belonging to the **predicted moon**. Likewise, there is another case in the water.

<meta>This page follows the discussion of "impressions" or atmospheric phenomena. The previous page explained the physics of "Flying Dragons" using the analogy of a candle flame. <meta>Modern foliation "45" in red ink at top center. Decorative red ink flourish along the left margin.</meta>

These are two species that are expelled downward; this is commonly called a **falling star** <note>original: "stella cadens"</note> and by philosophers **descending stars** <note>original: "astra descendens"</note>. In one manner, through a vibrating motion, it is sent to the side—from one hot part to another—and then, through the memory of the heat <note>likely referring to the path or "trace" of heat</note>, it is attracted to that place. There, in descending after its end, it runs far upon the ground <note>original: "in petram"; literally "onto a rock"</note>, where it is hidden and dilates in the middle. It flows through its location because a poison <note>original: "uirus"; often used in medieval science to describe a potent or foul liquid residue</note> runs out from the other side, being moist and cold. This is blown by the winds, as is clear from what has been said regarding its expansion. ¶ The middle part, however, is purified by itself. Because it moves from its end to that side where the air stands, that side becomes hot, and by that heat, it consumes and expels what it finds. ¶ But there is heat as far as the limit of **inflammation** <term>inflammatio; the technical point at which a vapor catches fire</term> in the middle way. Then it is placed in the middle region and does not penetrate it; then it steps through the distance from that place, expelled by the cold. Because it sends many mixtures beneath it, it becomes sodden and thickens into a cloud, and it penetrates the cloud where it is seen <note>original: "auis"; likely a transcription error for "visus" (seen/sight)</note>. This is called by philosophers **appearing stars** <note>original: "astra hy appens"; likely a corruption of a Greek-derived term for meteors that seem to hang or appear suddenly</note>. ¶ For truth is spoken concerning the generation of vapors and their ends: leaning to the side, they are then sublime, as is evident in the matter of the mixed rhombus <note>a geometric description of the meteor's path or shape</note>, nor does it generate all that vapor.

Now, however, we speak of the imperceptible causes generated from **humid vapor** <note>original: "vapore humido"</note>; in that case, it is like water, just as from dry vapor [it is like salt]. These two are great because of the heat which is **fervent in summer** <unclear>feruidus</unclear>. ¶ To this end, humid vapor is indeed water and not an **inflammation**. ¶ Because of this, in the two qualities of fire, it can occur in the cold and the moist, being described as coming from heat and tempered warmth. ¶ For there is also another foreign thing, which is the mixing and accompanying of vapor in water, which is said to follow and consume the vapor into **ice** <note>original: "glaciem"</note>. ¶ By this path, the vapor is inhaled into the nature of water, and <unclear>it is seen</unclear> together with the cold to be thickened, just as heat is suspected of being lost, and it becomes cold. Freezing, indeed, occurs through the strengthening of that cold, consuming the water that is placed there. ¶ It is not as if any cloud is [simply] contained in the palace <note>likely "pala" or "paludem," referring to the atmospheric basin</note>.

<meta>This page continues the medieval scientific investigation into atmospheric phenomena. <page-num>96</page-num>

It is most suitable for cold water and many other things in the <unclear>genus</unclear> of commission <note>likely referring to the mixing or "commissio" of elements</note> regarding water... <insert>It should be noted that</insert> many of those things which are frozen from a moist vapor are **inflammable** <note>original: "inflammabile"</note>.

**Also** that water is an element much thinner than earth and more compact; where, looking again at the vapor, it is not much retained by those exhalations, and it is an exhalation through cold constricting the thin vapor. This happens to many types of vapors through the region of cold towards the part of summer. But having been elevated further and further into the region of excellent <note>meaning "extreme" or "outer"</note> cold, it is tempered, by which they are either warmer or less warm.

**Also** that the moist vapor will be that which is warm and very proportional, reaching above the water to the region of cold beyond. 

**Also** that he says the surrounding cold vapor in that place penetrates its depth on all sides, because it gives entry to the cold in all parts. 

**Also** that the multiplied cold, with an equal amount of heat, the cold begins commonly to congeal, and there is a blowing into the mixture beyond the vapors. Because the cold is strong, it begins to congeal the flow toward that part; and because the cold is very dominant, it constricts the accompanying water and generates from it a great and full **hailstone** <note>original: "grandinem"</note>, according to whether the cold is greater or lesser. 

It can be said that the vapor itself is called "the highest water." This is according to two points: first, that **hail** does not fall except in warmer times <note>a classic Aristotelian observation: hail is a summer phenomenon because ground heat pushes cold higher and intensifies it</note> and in the part... <unclear>m</unclear>... because then the arriving heat and the heat of summer much attenuates the vapor and, on the other hand, raises it to the middle cold. From this, the vapor, surrounded on all sides by the cold, gives entry to the cold, and they are very strongly constricted. 

Just as water... <unclear>m</unclear>... in winter, it is frozen beyond [usual] when cold water is in the same place, because **warm water is much more rare** <term>rara; meaning less dense or having larger pores</term> and thus it gave entry to the cold.

**Another** argues that it is not directly round, and that the surrounding of the hailstone in a <unclear>smooth</unclear> form is the freezing. 

**Also** that when vapor is expanded in width, the containing cold is much more dominant from the sides than from the upper and lower parts; and when the freezing of the parts of the water is great, it flows toward it, and for another reason the accompanying flow adhered to it.

<summary>This page explains the medieval physics of hail formation, arguing that summer heat paradoxically causes hail by pushing vapors into the upper cold and making water "thinner" to allow cold to penetrate. It explores the concept of antiperistasis—the intensification of a quality when surrounded by its opposite.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the medieval scientific investigation into atmospheric phenomena. Having previously discussed how vapors form dew and "honey-dew," the text now moves into a technical explanation of how smoke, vapor, and heat interact to generate wind and vertical movement in the atmosphere.</meta>

Smoke is twofold: one is fire, and the other is a very hot <term>exhalation</term> <note>original: "exallatiō"; in medieval physics, this refers to dry, hot gases rising from the earth, as opposed to moist vapors</note> or vapor. Where, however, there is a rising in the air, [it moves] by falling, and then by weight it moves toward the side and not directly upward. **F**or vapor is equally gentle and moves without noise into a sudden motion and a confused agitation, because it is pushed to one side by impacts, and from the other side it loses the heat it once had. **S**till, in the theory of impulse from a [divine?] source, and because it inclines itself toward a certain region of the world, it is said to move as wind, which stands under the <unclear>alder</unclear> toward that direction. **A**nother [type of vapor] is very hot due to a double expulsion or impulse, because there is cold in those places and on the moist side; for example, when fire is poured into water, a sudden and impetuous expulsion occurs, moving everything from one thing to another, and the vapor within expels the moisture. From this, it follows that when there is stormy rain, it is like the wind mentioned before, driven by grinding and impulsive force.

**G**enerally, concerning wind on high: it arises from heat that is self-inflamed, and from moist vapor that is not inflamed. Finally, there is the matter of the wind and the [nature] of winds in the book, regarding their effects and the place of their generation. **W**ind which is made from thick vapor [has] two [causes]: **E**ffectively, it is produced by the heat of the sun raising this vapor without consuming it; and this action is such that it drives the vapor <unclear>outward</unclear>. This happens during the winter solstice, because then the sun, either by burning or drying, parches the elements, and thus the vapor raised at that time is rarer and more ruling; it appears cold and dry. 

**W**ind is generated in the <term>middle region</term> <note>original: "mediam rēom"; medieval thinkers believed the atmosphere had three layers, with the middle layer being the coldest, where clouds and winds were formed</note> because the cold there is greatest. **F**or a thin vapor falls there, and then as it runs through its <unclear>inclination</unclear>, the cold from those cold places meets it and pushes it, causing it to descend into a somewhat warmer area [closer to] the sun. In that place, it causes an expulsion from itself, for it is more strengthened in a dry [mass] than in moist vapor; for it still blows against it, and in this way the impulse of the wind is created, which is effectively the "breath of the air." **C**oncerning the movement and exit of the wind, there is a doubt, just as there is with rain coming from [various] parts...

<summary>This page explains the physics of wind generation, attributing it to the interaction between rising hot vapors and the extreme cold of the "middle region" of the atmosphere. It describes wind as an "impulse" or "expulsion" caused by temperature imbalances, comparing the process to the reaction of fire hitting water.</summary>
