<meta>This page continues the medieval scientific investigation into atmospheric phenomena. Having previously discussed how vapors form dew and "honey-dew," the text now moves into a technical explanation of how smoke, vapor, and heat interact to generate wind and vertical movement in the atmosphere.</meta>

The proposition is: what is it that generates wind more broadly? For a gust is found more in the outermost part of the earth and not above it. **The cause** of this is that the reflection of the sun’s rays is weak at the edges and strong in the middle; this is due to the angle of the rays, since at the edge there is a narrow junction. **In the middle**, however, the angle is small, which is "friendly" <note>This likely refers to a "congenial" or favorable physical condition for heat concentration</note> and obedient to the process. **Wind**, therefore, is the effect of the following: a weak heat and evaporation which, when strengthened within the earth at its extremities, then [bursts out] and does not disperse above the earth itself. **But** another kind, like a gust distant from the earth, declares its state and then ceases, because a gust is weak since cold lies hidden in that vapor. When that higher vapor is subjected to a very vehement depression, and when the heat is very widespread and infused with cold, it greatly agitates the air. This produces the gust that is said to descend toward the higher regions of the winds, and then over the lower ones.

**But** again, physical heat arising from the rays and their reverberation—at the point where it is said to dwell—raises a force and a mass from the <term>exhalation</term> <note>original: "alona"; likely a corruption of "exallatio" or a specific technical term for a dry vaporous mass</note>. This is not a rainy mixture of vapor, but once strengthened, it reveals a strong gust. This other wind is nothing but the earth's vapor which depends on a falling [force]. In this state, all vapor is very light and generative. Where the air moves equally over its own vapor, it is then expelled from the lower to the upper regions.

**In another way**, a strong gust is of a different kind and origin. **Wind** is that whose density lies hidden in narrow cold; it distends and, bearing upward, repels the air so that it carries a sound. Such a gust is called an "open movement." This is called distension, and then the vapor—according to what the <term>philosophers</term> <note>original: "phi"; a standard abbreviation for the "philosophi," referring here to Aristotle and his medieval commentators</note> say—follows a clear path of vapor. It is called a direct path of vapor, being more of a smoky nature through all its unevenness. Because it is of a smoky nature, it differs from watery vapor. **Wind**, however, is said by its nature to be [fluid], for it is obedient to every impulse and easily passes to every part. The sign of this is just as when a body is pushed into water; by a very strong and violent impulse, it pushes the water and it waves through the part being pushed until the violent force ceases. **Wind**, therefore, pushing first, gives such a strong motion through the world, and from the broadest smoke, it produces a gust.

<summary>This page explains the medieval theory of wind generation, contrasting "gusts" near the earth's surface with "winds" driven by solar heat, trapped cold, and the movement of smoky exhalations.</summary>
<keywords>wind generation, solar reflection, atmospheric pressure, smoky vapor, Aristotle's meteorology, fluid dynamics</keywords>

### Of the twelve winds appearing in this described figure, and first of the Subsolanus:

**<insert>Subsolanus</insert>** is so called because it is at the rising of the sun; it is more gusty and brings few rains and cold air. **<insert>Vulturnus</insert>** is a gentle blower and brings more rain and tepid air. **<insert>Eurus</insert>** is not said to be of great blowing, but is dry and not strong; **<insert>Eurus</insert>** sometimes takes away the rains, namely the eastern ones. **<insert>Subsolanus</insert>** on the other hand brings rains to that same [region]. **<insert>Aquilo</insert>** is called [so] because the North wind is a very strong blower; it washes away rains and purifies the air, and all things are said to shrink from the cold because it thickens the vapors and the wind.

<meta>This page continues the technical discussion of winds and their physical properties, moving from a list of names to a deeper explanation of the mechanics of heat, cold, and the influence of the north and south winds on nature and health.</meta>

...from the place of cold, as <term>Aristotle</term> <note>the Greek philosopher whose work formed the basis of medieval science</note> says <unclear>it</unclear> is stated in [Book] 10 of the <term>Physics</term> <note>original: "physice"; though the *Physics* has only 8 books, the author likely refers to a specific passage on motion or a commentary</note>. For them, it is because cold closes the pores of the earth and contains the essence of water so that it cannot evaporate, and thus it separates the air from the deep matter of the rain. By the same reasoning, it closes the pores of our bodies so that the interior vapor does not exit us because of the exterior cold; and from this, the hot vapor within is increased and strengthened, and thus it is made more powerful.

<insert>¶ (blue)</insert> Whence the waters that now break forth from the earth are warmer for everyone in winter than in summer; the cause is said to be the same <note>This refers to "antiperistasis," the medieval theory that a quality is intensified when surrounded by its opposite, such as heat being concentrated inside the earth by the cold on the surface</note>. 

<insert>¶ (red)</insert> Another sign is from the perception of <unclear>rare things</unclear> through which we can know when <unclear>rains</unclear> are about to arrive, and this is when the <term>Aquilo</term> <note>the North wind</note> blows, because it contains the vapors in the mountains and caverns so they do not evaporate, which is the cause of thunder. And it is said thus by Aristotle in the eighth book of <term>On the Soul</term> <note>original: "de aia" (De Anima); this specific lore about sheep is actually found in Aristotle’s *History of Animals*</note>: shepherds who want male offspring to be generated by their sheep make their sheep face toward the <term>Meridies</term> <note>the South</note> during the time of the North wind. 

Hence also the <term>Eurus</term> <note>the South-East wind</note> is from the northern part through the diameter; its blowing has an elevation and depression in its motion, and from this, it is that its blowing tends from the north into the south. And the more it comes into the south, the more it is directed toward the extreme heat, through which the vapor is rarefied and rises; it does not descend unless the vapors are rarefied. 

<insert>¶ (red)</insert> But the winds that draw themselves [from elsewhere], because they will be hot vapor and from a hot place, bring diseases to our bodies. They disturb and corrupt the hot and humid hot air because they condense the vapors, and it blows many rains, and [does so] tortuously <note>in a winding or indirect path</note> because its blowing tends toward a likeness from the middle; and thus the vapor inundates my <unclear>blowing</unclear> from the knees and having been blown into it. Consequently, at its end, it induces and draws a stream, and from that vivification of the blowing, they are <unclear>incontinent</unclear>; it corrupts the clouds and the heavy waters and makes them, etc. and this produces rains.

<insert>¶ (red)</insert> And it should be known that every wind blowing tortuously, and others <unclear>daily</unclear> regarding which there are impediments through their own causes, or <unclear>how</unclear> the North wind more commonly blows tortuously; and this happens because of the proximity to the worldly vapors toward which they move the highest winds there. But their winds [move] through hot places because then the southern wind more [directly] guides the blowing in the same straight line in the middle of the climates and around it; and from its own nature, it is said that it does not bring diseases.

Because another very narrow and thick cloud meets it, it is said that the flame in that recognized cloud [is seen] by appearing rather than by sounding.

<meta>This page continues the technical classification of meteorological phenomena, moving from the physical generation of "stones" (hail or thunderbolts) to a detailed categorization of six specific "cases" of vaporous eruptions, flames, and the influence of regional climates—both cold and hot—on thunder and wind.</meta>

Indeed, all flowing inflations are carried in the open, and certain external ones are released into the valleys. **Case 1** is the mode of white vapor, which appears as a protruding flame not fueled by any material <note>original: "non coalimentata"; meaning a flame that exists without burning a physical fuel like wood</note>, and this is a sign in the air that directs a great force through the base from which there is a fiery substance; it is extinguished more quickly because of this, so that to the observer they appear white and no sign of burning appears in them. In a short time, the whole inflation is driven forward, and through its dispersion it arrives in one single place and does not harm the surrounding paths, because its motion is stable and it passes through the things it creates.

**Case 2** is a bending away from a straight path by some vapor toward another; it strikes that fire and, being too weak to be consumed, it ceases and is extinguished. Its violence stops, and it does not harm men.

**Case 3** is the mode of the same vapor when it is weakened here; it harms nothing at all because it is immediately extinguished and blunted upon contact with the air, so that it ceases.

**It should be known** <note>original: "Sciendum"; a common markers in Scholastic texts to highlight a key principle</note> that there is also a violent impulse. **Case 4**: This must be known, that this time of wind is different in cold regions and in dark climates <note>original: "climatis scoto"; likely referring to northern or shadowed regions</note> etc., and it is different in hot regions. 

**Case 5**: In cold regions, winds rarely carry such things because in them there is a great summer vapor. **And also** in the hoary time <note>original: "tempore cano"; referring to winter or the season of frost</note> and the heat of the vapor, it is because it then creates an outward fire and raises the vapor, and then in those regions the thunder is greater. **In** other and hot regions, thunders occur at the **equinoxes** <term>equinoxes</term> <note>the two times of year—spring and autumn—when day and night are of equal length</note> and not so certainly in the heat of winter. **And also** in those times there are fires because of the element itself.

**Case 6**: **It should also be known** that they last for less time, namely because formerly the summer absorbs all the vapors above. This is not the case regarding distilled vapors, and in hot places it is due to the consumption of the thicker vapor. Otherwise, the great fire itself raises a large nearby flame and creates for you a great, spreading flame; it contributes to the color of the air and is seen according to an <unclear>observation</unclear> <note>original: "obsid[eratio]"</note> that is not to be considered purely by a few, even in the winter signs. **Or** in autumn, as we have heard thunder in the air, because of which there is fear in the eternal God and a strong rain throughout the whole year.

<meta>This page continues the discussion of meteorological phenomena, shifting focus from specific "cases" of atmospheric fire to the classical Aristotelian explanations for thunder and earthquakes. It references Aristotle's *Meteorologica* to explain the relationship between heat, trapped vapors, and geological instability, particularly noting observations from the region of Lombardy.</meta>

...will be observed as in another year. **It should be known** <note>original: "Sciendum est"; a common phrase used to introduce a fundamental principle in medieval university texts</note> that many great signs appear; much effort is expended so that they might be easily observed or hidden throughout the entire year. **Concerning thunder**, Aristotle says in his book *The Meteorology* <note>original: "libro metho," referring to Aristotle's *Meteorologica*, the standard textbook on natural science in the Middle Ages</note> that the Master <note>original: "Dominus," likely referring to an instructor or a specific commentator on Aristotle</note> says that thunder is a repetition of a natural power of sound. These sounds are not seen, because it is said that they are perceived by the ear rather than the eye. One sees something like a star or violent winds in the clouds, followed by a sudden release, an eruption, or an exhalation sent out with great splendor. As it descends from the coldness of the heights to the... <note>The text here is obscure, possibly referring to a specific atmospheric layer</note>

The Master says that in the first mode, even a small magnitude of force can cause a great explosion. **From every thunderclap** and flash of lightning, it is observed that although they are produced together, the sound reaches the ears of men more slowly <note>This is a remarkably accurate observation of the difference between the speed of light and the speed of sound</note>. **And concerning the opposite of thunder**, an **earthquake** is seen to be a dry, very thick, and earthy vapor. This vapor is produced by the heat of the sun penetrating deep within the earth at the time of winds. **Likewise**, when the earth is blocked <note>original: "opilatam," referring to the medical or physical concept of "oppilation" or a blockage of pores</note> in those places by such obstructions, it is said that the natural flow and ebb of the air is restricted. The place becomes blocked and the vapor is enclosed so that it cannot exhale. The vapor is trapped primarily by continuous splendor <note>likely meaning intense, prolonged heat</note> where the earth is solid and rocky, or made of continuous stone.

**Then**, in the attempted exit of this vapor, the heat multiplies because it cannot pass through the blocked pores during the time of the winds. **For** while it remains trapped, it is diminished or increased, being at times thick and subtle, or hot and tempered. **From these things**, it is concluded by the Master concerning the place and time of the outbreak. **In the time** of the outbreak, they rule that there is a mass of winds within the earth that extends and seeks an exit. **It was seen** with great and rapid damage in **Lombardy** <note>Lombardy, in Northern Italy, was a region known to medieval scholars for its seismic activity and weather patterns</note>, occurring during the winter time; it showed that in the end there was much wind within the earth at that time.

<summary>This page explains the medieval Aristotelian theory of thunder and earthquakes, attributing both to the movement of trapped "dry vapors" that cannot escape through the earth's pores.</summary>
